Yang–Mills–Utiyama Theory and Graviweak Correspondence
Yoshimasa Kurihara

Abstract
This report provides a geometrical Yang–Mills theory, including gravity. The theory treats the space-time symmetry of the local Lorentz group in the same manner as the internal gauge symmetry. We extend the theory into a more expansive space, which includes both Euclidean and Lorentzian metrics at its boundary. In the extended space, we can transport topological achievements from the Euclidean Yang–Mills theory into the Lorentzian Yang–Mills theory. This extension also provides a relation between space-time and internal symmetry. The perspective provided by the extended theory suggests the novel relation between gravity and the weak force, leading us to the graviweak correspondence.



\typearea

16

1
Introduction

The standard theory of particle physics was finally established in 2011 owing to the experimental discovery of the Higgs boson
1
1

1

For the standard theory of particle physics, see, e.g., ref.
[
1
]
, and for its experimental support, see ref.
[
2
]
.
Since then, the Standard theory has been supported by various precise experimental data.
The standard theory coherently treats three fundamental forces, electric, weak, and strong, and describes the behaviour of elementary particles with these interactions.
The standard theory consists of three layers. The first layer is the classical Yang–Mills theory
[
3
]
, which includes
U\!(1)
,
SU\!(2)
, and
SU\!(3)
gauge symmetries respectively corresponding to electromagnetic, weak and strong interactions.
The second layer is the quantum field theory with its perturbative expansion, which includes a renormalisation.
Though the original gauge symmetry is broken due to the gauge fixing during quantisation, a new kind of symmetry, the BRST symmetry
[
4
,
5
]
, appears and ensures the theory’s unitarity.
The perturbative quantisation of the Yang–Mills theory with the compact Lie group gauge symmetry has been established, and the renormalisability has been proven for all orders of perturbation.
The third layer is the spontaneous
U\!(1){\otimes}SU\!(2)
symmetry breaking, providing the mass to the gauge bosons and fermions, so-called the Higgs mechanism.
The spontaneous symmetry breaking has the broken symmetry of its vacuum, and the gauge boson obtains its effective mass while maintaining the gauge symmetry at the Lagrangian level.
On the other hand,
ad hoc
Yukawa coupling with the Higgs boson provides its mass for a fermion.

While the standard theory of particle physics considers three forces except the gravitational force among the four fundamental forces in nature, general relativity treats the gravitational force classically.
General relativity is supported by various experiments, including recent observations of the gravitational wave
[
6
]
.
General relativity is also the Yang–Mills type gauge theory with the local
SO(1,3)
gauge symmetry, as first Utiyama has clarified
[
7
]
.
A relation between the Yang–Mills theory and gravity is discussed by Cofano, Fu and Krasnov
[
8
]
and by Denfiz
[
9
]
.
Unfortunately, a perturbative renormalisation theory of gravity has not yet been established, in contrast to the Yang–Mills theory with the compact Lie group.
In other words, a theory of gravity is still in the first layer of the standard theory of particle physics.

After the original work of Yang and Mills in 1954, the Yang–Mills theory was refined geometrically and inspired mathematicians as a tool for investigating three- and four-dimensional Euclidean manifolds.
On the other hand, the Yang–Mills theory, including gravitation with the Lorentzian metric, which is indeed the target of physicists, is not at that level.
The primary objective of this study is to present the Yang–Mills theory with symmetries
SU\!(2)
and the local
SO(1,3)
using the language of geometry, then to accomplish the first layer of the standard theory, including gravity.
We refer to this theory as the
Yang–Mills–Utiyama
(YMU ) theory in this report.
This objective has two goals: One is to give new insight into constructing quantum gravity by describing general relativity using the same mathematical language as the Yang–Mills theory: it may bridge the first layer to the second layer of the standard theory, including gravity.
The other is to allow the importing of modern mathematical harvests into physics.
The secondary objective of this study is to provide novel tools to make it possible.
The current author has developed a mathematical tool
[
10
]
to connect the Lorentzian metric to the Euclidean one topologically
2
2

2

This report refers to the tool as the
amphometric
, named the
\theta
-metric in ref.
[
10
]
. The parametrisation of the metric in this report differs from that in the reference.
.
It allows topological results obtained in the Euclidean metric space to be imported into the Lorentzian metric space.
This study refines and adapts that method to the Yang–Mills theory.

In the third layer of the standard theory, the Yukawa coupling between the Higgs boson and the fermion gives the fermion a mass.
The theory insists that a fermion coupled to the
SU\!(2)
vacuum through the Higgs boson obtains the
pole mass
, which is a position of the divergence of the fermion propagator.
Consider the relationship between a gravitational mass and the quantum field theoretic mass in the nucleon-effective theory here.
Even though a proton is not an elementary particle with an internal structure consisting of quarks and gluons, the effective theory of nucleons treats it as a simple particle with a definite mass, charge, spin, and others.
In the effective theory, a proton
pole mass
is equivalent to the
gravitational mass
of 1mol of hydrogen gas divided by the Avogadro constant, which is
1.672621898(21)\times 10^{-27}
kg
[
2
]
.
The theory with this mass well describes a proton-electron scattering
[
11
]
.
We can recognise this as evidence of the equivalence theorem in the quantum field theoretic context: “
The pole in the fermion propagator is equivalent to the gravitational mass
.”
Suppose we recognise the equivalence principle as a true statement for the quantum field theory.
This fact insists that a source of fermionic matter’s gravitational mass is the vacuum’s weak
SU\!(2)
charge.
This is one of the variants of the
graviweak correspondence
.
Several authors
[
12
,
13
,
14
]
have tried unifying the weak and gravitational forces.

This study is also inspired by recent works by Woit
[
15
,
16
]
, who has proposed a novel approach to possible chirality asymmetric method to treat both Minkowski space-time and the Yang–Mills theory.
The Lie-algebra homomorphism
{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{o}(4)={\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}(2)\oplus{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}(2)
and
{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{o}(1,3)\otimes\mathbb{C}={\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C})\oplus\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C})}
gives us a hint about the graviweak correspondence.
This Lie-algebra homomorphism between rotational and unitary groups is accidental, appearing only in low-dimensional spaces such as
{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{o}(3)={\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}(2)
; it is not valid for higher dimensional rotation.
An angular momentum is an eigenvector of the
SO(3)
generator, and a spinor is that of
SU\!(2)
one.
Eigenvalues of
SU\!(2)
spinor can be treated as a half-integer valued angular momentum in physics owing to this accidental Lie-algebra homomorphism.
In the higher-dimensional spaces, a pair
SO(N)
and
SU\!(N\!-\!1)
does not share their Lie-algebra; thus, the
SU\!(N\!-\!1)
spinor is not interpreted as the
SO(N)
angular momentum when
N\geq 5
.
This observation tells us that accidental homomorphism in four-dimensional space-time plays an essential role in fixing the properties of fermions.

Only left-handed fermions have a weak charge.
On the other hand, we can describe a gravitational interaction using only half the space-time degree of freedom, say the right-handed part.
It suggests the origin of the
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2)
gauge symmetry is owing to another half of the space-time degree of freedom.
This is one aspect of the graviweak correspondence.
If it is true, we can understand the universe being weakly charged.
This observation tells us that accidental homomorphism in four-dimensional space-time plays an essential role in fixing the properties of fermions.
This study’s tertiary and last objective is to pursue the graviweak correspondence using the tool to connect Euclidean and Lorentzian metrics developed for achieving the secondary objective.

The structure of this report is as follows:
After this introductory section,
section 2
provides mathematical preliminaries utilised in this study.
In this section, we also introduce mathematical notations used throughout this report.

Section 3
is devoted to accomplishing the primary objective, establishing the general relativistic Yang–Mills theory using geometrical language.
After clearly defining principal bundles where the theory is defined, we write down the Lagrangian and the equation of motion of the Yang–Mills theory with symmetries
SU\!(2)
and the local
SO(1,3)
, namely the YMU theory, using the geometrical language.

Section 4
introduces a novel mathematical tool to connect Euclidean and Minkowskian spaces topologically and analytically.
Mathematical tools introduced in
section 1
and the YMU Lagrangian are redefined in the broader space, including Euclidean and Minkowskian spaces.
This section achieves the secondary objective of this study.
The perspective provided in
section 4
suggests the graviweak correspondence discussed in
section 5
.
We present the possible resolution of why the Higgs-fermion Yukawa coupling provides a fermion’s gravitational mass.
Two supplementary sections present a list of induced representations of
SL(2)
group (
Appendix A
) and a detailed discussion of the instanton in Euclidean and Minkowskian spaces as an example of the validity of the method introduced in
section 3
(
Appendix B
).

This report uses the following physical units:
a speed of light is set to unity
c=1
, and a gravitational constant
\kappa^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}=8\pi G_{\hskip-0.81949pt\text{N}}/c^{2}
(
G_{\hskip-0.81949pt\text{N}}
is the Newtonian constant of gravitation) and reduced Plank-constant
\hbar=h/2\pi
are written explicitly.
In these units, physical dimensions of fundamental constants are
[\hbar\hskip 1.00006pt\kappa^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}]=L^{2}=T^{2}
and
[\hbar/\kappa^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}]=E^{2}=M^{2}
, where
L
,
T
,
E
and
M
are, respectively, length, time, energy and mass dimensions.

2
Mathematical preliminaries

The standard theory of particle physics is based on the quantum gauge theory with
U\!(1){\otimes}SU\!(2)
and
SU\!(3)
groups, which are called the Yang-Mills theory
[
3
]
.
The former describes electroweak interaction, and the latter represents the strong interaction in nature.
In addition to the gauge symmetry, the Yang–Mills theory has the Poincaŕe symmetry owing to the
special principle of relativity
.
General relativity is also the gauge theory
[
7
]
with the
GL(4)
group owing to the
general principle of relativity
and describing a gravitational interaction.
In this report, we call the general relativistic Yang–Mills theory, including the gravitational force as the gauge interaction, as Yang–Mills–Utiyama (YMU ) theory.

At the classical level, the YMU theory utilises mathematics of differential geometry, algebraic topology, and group representation theory.
This section prepares mathematical preliminaries used in the YMU theory.

2.1
General relativity

General relativity
[
17
]
, developed by Einstein, is a geometrical theory that describes the gravitational force as an intrinsic property of space-time.
Einstein constructed his gravitational theory under two fundamental principles: the
general relativistic principle
and the
equivalent principle
.
The former requires that space-time is the smooth manifold constructing the principal bundle with the
GL(4)
structure group, and physical fields are given as sections in the bundle.
The latter insists that at any point in the manifold, we can find a local smooth manifold with the vanishing Levi-Civita connection, which means the locally vanishing gravity.
Physical matter fields and the forces among them correspond to sections and curvatures of the principal bundle, respectively.
This section introduces mathematical set-ups to treat general relativity.

2.1.1
Section, connection, and curvature

The fundamental force field corresponds to a curvature in a four-dimensional smooth manifold, with the compact Lie group as the structure group, and the physical matter field corresponds to the section of the bundle.
This section introduces a principal bundle and its
section
,
connection
, and
curvature
.
We denote the principal bundle by the tuple
(E,\pi,M,G)
, where the smooth manifolds
E
and
M
are total and base spaces, respectively, a diffeomorphism
\pi
is a bundle (projection) map and
G
is a principal group.

We introduce the vector space
V
defined in total space
E
as the section whose space is denoted as
\Gamma(M,V(E))
.
When we emphasise the vector
{\bm{v}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}V
has symmetry owing to the structure group
G
, we explicitly write as
\Gamma(M,V(E),G)
.
The group operator
g^{~}_{\textsc{g}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}G
acts on section
s\in\Gamma(M,V(E),G)
from the left.
A connection is a Lie algebra valued one-form
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\textsc{g}}\in{\Omega}^{1}(T^{*}\hskip-1.99997ptM)\otimes Ad(\mathfrak{g}^{~}_{G})
, where
{\Omega}^{p}_{~}(T^{*}\hskip-1.99997ptM)
is a space of
p
-forms in the cotangent bundle
T^{*}\hskip-1.00006ptM
and
\mathfrak{g}^{~}_{G}
is a Lie algebra of the structure group
G
.
The differential operator, covariant under the structural group, namely the covariant differential, is provided using the connection as

\displaystyle d_{\textsc{g}}:\Omega^{p}\rightarrow\Omega^{p+1}:{\mathfrak{a}}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mapsto}\hskip 1.99997ptd^{~}_{\textsc{g}}{\mathfrak{a}}:=d{\mathfrak{a}}-ic^{~}_{\textsc{g}}\hskip 1.00006pt[{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\textsc{g}},{\mathfrak{a}}]_{\wedge},~~\text{where}~~[{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\textsc{g}},{\mathfrak{a}}]_{\wedge}:={\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\textsc{g}}\wedge{\mathfrak{a}}-(-1)^{p}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{a}}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\textsc{g}}.

(2.1.1)

Corresponding curvature two-form
\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{g}}\in{\Omega}^{2}(T^{*}\hskip-1.99997ptM)\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{~}_{G}
is defined owing to the structure equation as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\textsc{g}}

\displaystyle:=d{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\textsc{g}}-ic^{~}_{\textsc{g}}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\textsc{g}}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\textsc{g}}=\sum_{I}\left(d{\mathfrak{A}}^{\hskip 1.63885ptI}_{\textsc{g}}+\frac{c^{~}_{\textsc{g}}}{2}\sum_{J,K}f^{I}_{\hskip 0.81949ptJ\hskip-0.81949ptK}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{A}}^{\hskip 1.63885ptJ}_{\textsc{g}}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}^{\hskip 1.63885ptK}_{\textsc{g}}\right)\tau^{~}_{I},

(2.1.2)

with

\displaystyle\left[\tau^{~}_{J},\tau^{~}_{K}\right]

\displaystyle=\tau^{~}_{J}\tau^{~}_{K}-\tau^{~}_{K}\tau^{~}_{J}=:i\sum_{I}f^{I}_{\hskip 0.81949ptJ\hskip-0.81949ptK}\tau^{~}_{I},

(2.1.3)

where
\tau^{~}_{I}
and
f^{I}_{\hskip 0.81949ptJK}
are the generator and the structure constant of the structure group.
The structure constant
f^{I}_{\hskip 0.81949ptJK}
is defined by (
2.1.3
).
Here, we introduce a coupling constant
c^{~}_{\textsc{g}}\in\mathbb{C}
in the covariant differential following the physics convention, though it does not appear in the standard mathematics textbook.
When a single bundle is considered, the coupling constant can be absorbed in defining a connection and a curvature scaled as
-ic^{~}_{\textsc{g}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{A}}_{\textsc{g}}\mapsto{\mathfrak{A}}_{\textsc{g}}
and
-ic^{~}_{\textsc{g}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{g}}\mapsto\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{g}}
.
Nevertheless, we explicitly write a coupling constant in this study because when multiple bundles are twisted over the same base manifold, each bundle’s coupling constant cannot be absorbed by rescaling and provides relative strength of interactions in physics.

The covariant differential of section
\bm{\phi}=\sum_{I}\phi^{I}\tau_{I}\in\Gamma(M,V(E),G)
is given using (
2.1.1
) for the zero-form object
\bm{\phi}
as

\displaystyle d_{\textsc{g}}\bm{\phi}

\displaystyle=d\bm{\phi}-ic^{~}_{\textsc{g}}\left[{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\textsc{g}},\bm{\phi}\right]_{\wedge}=\sum_{I}\left(d\phi^{I}+{c^{~}_{\textsc{g}}}\sum_{J,K}f^{I}_{\hskip 0.81949ptJK}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{A}}^{\hskip 1.63885ptJ}_{\textsc{g}}\hskip 1.00006pt\phi^{K}\right)\tau_{I}\in\Gamma(M,V(E),G).

(2.1.4)

2.1.2
Inertial bundle

We introduce the inertial bundle in general relativity.
A base manifold of the inertial bundle is the pseud-Riemannian manifold
({\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}},\bm{g})
, namely the global space-time manifold, as a model of the universe, where
{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}
is a smooth and oriented four-dimensional manifold, and
\bm{g}
is a metric tensor in
{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}
with a negative sign such that det
[\bm{g}]<0
.
In an open neighbourhood
U_{p}\subset{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}
, orthonormal bases in
T_{\hskip-0.81949ptp}{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}
and
T^{*}_{\hskip-0.81949ptp}{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}
are respectively introduced as
\partial/\partial x^{\mu}
and
dx^{\mu}
.
We use the abbreviation
\partial_{\mu}:=\partial/\partial x^{\mu}
throughout this report.
Two trivial vector-bundles
{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}}:=\bigcup_{p}T_{\hskip-0.81949ptp}{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}
and
{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}}:=\bigcup_{p}T^{*}_{\hskip-0.81949ptp}{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}
are referred to as a tangent and cotangent bundles in
{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}
, respectively.
General relativity requires the universe to be a four-dimensional smooth manifold equipping the
GL(4)
symmetry as the structure group.

In the global space-time manifold, the Levi-Civita (affine) connection is defined owing to the metric tensor as

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\lambda}_{~\mu\nu}(x\!\in\!{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}):=\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt{\sum_{\sigma=0}^{3}}\hskip 1.00006ptg^{\lambda\sigma}\left(\partial_{\mu}g_{\nu\sigma}(x)+\partial_{\nu}g_{\mu\sigma}(x)-\partial_{\sigma}g_{\mu\nu}(x)\right),

where
g_{\mu\nu}:=[\bm{g}]_{\mu\nu}
.
An inertial system, in which the Levi-Civita connection vanishes
\Gamma^{\lambda}_{~\mu\nu}(p)=0
, exists at any point
p{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}
.
A local inertial manifold at
p
denoted as
{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}(p)
, where suffix “
l
” stands for the principal
Lorentzian group
(
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO\hskip-1.00006pt}(1,3)
group).

An inertial bundle is the principal bundle such that:

\displaystyle\left({\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}\otimes{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}},\pi^{~}_{\textsc{i}},{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}},SO(1,3)\right),

(2.1.5)

where
{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}:=\bigcup_{p}{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}(p)
.
A projection map acts on the total space as

\displaystyle\pi^{~}_{\textsc{i}}:{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}\otimes{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}\rightarrow{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}:{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}(p)\mapsto{p}.

The existence of the local inertial manifold at any point in
{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}
is nothing other than “Einstein’s equivalence principle” in physics.
We denote an orthonormal basis on
{{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}
as
\partial_{a}
.
As for suffixes of vectors in
{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}
, Roman letters are used for components of the standard basis throughout this study; while Greek letters are used for them in
{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}
.
The metric tensor in
{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}
is denoted as
{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}}}:=\mathrm{diag}[1,-1,-1,-1]
using the standard basis.
The metric tensor and the Levi-Civita tensor (complete anti-symmetric tensor)
\bm{\epsilon}
, whose component is
[\bm{\epsilon}]^{~}_{0123}=\epsilon^{~}_{0123}=+1
, are constant tensors in
{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}
.

A pull-back
3
3

3

A pull-back (push-forward) of a map
\bullet
is denoted as
\bullet^{\sharp}
(
\bullet_{\sharp}
) instead of
\bullet^{*}
(
\bullet_{*}
) in this study, avoiding confusion with the complex conjugate.
of bundle map
\pi^{~}_{\textsc{i}}
induces one-form object
{\mathfrak{e}}\in\Gamma\left({{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}},{{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}},SO(1,3)\right)
represented using the standard basis as

\displaystyle\pi_{\textsc{i}}^{\sharp}:\Omega^{1}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}})\rightarrow\Gamma\left({{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}},{{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}},SO(1,3)\right):dx^{\mu}\mapsto{\mathfrak{e}}^{a}:={\mathcal{E}}^{a}_{\mu}(p)dx^{\mu},

where
{\mathcal{E}}^{a}_{\mu}\in{C^{\infty}({\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}})}
is a smooth function defined in the open neighbour
U\subset{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}
, namely a vierbein, which is determined using the bi-local function
\bm{\xi}(y,x)
for
x,y{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}U{\subset}{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}
as

\displaystyle\xi^{a}(x,x)=0{\quad\text{and}\quad}{\mathcal{E}}^{a}_{\mu}(x):=\left.\frac{\partial\xi^{a}(y,x)}{\partial y^{\mu}}\right|^{~}_{y=x}.

The Einstein convention for repeated indices (one in up and one in down) is exploited throughout this study.
The vierbein maps a vector in
{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}
to that in
{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}
and
vice versa
.
We use a Fraktur letter to represent differential forms
4
4

4

A Fraktur letter is also used showing Lie algebra.
defined in the cotangent bundle in this report.
Vierbain inverse
[{\mathcal{E}}^{-1}]_{a}^{\mu}={\mathcal{E}}_{a}^{\mu}
is also called the vierbein.
One-form object
{\mathfrak{e}}
is referred to as the vierbein form.
The vierbein form provides an orthonormal basis in
{{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}
; it is a dual basis of
\partial_{a}
such as
{\mathfrak{e}}^{a}\partial_{b}={\mathcal{E}}^{a}_{\mu}{\mathcal{E}}^{\nu}_{b}dx^{\mu}\partial_{\nu}=\delta^{a}_{b}
and is a rank-one tensor (vector) in
{{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}
.
The four-dimensional volume form is represented using vierbein forms as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{4!}\epsilon_{{\circ\circ}{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}=\mathrm{det}[\bm{{\mathcal{E}}}]\hskip 1.99997ptdx^{0}\wedge dx^{1}\wedge dx^{2}\wedge dx^{3},~~\mathrm{det}[\bm{{\mathcal{E}}}]>0.

(2.1.6)

Dummy
Roman
indices are often abbreviated to a small circle
{\circ}
(or
\star
) when the dummy-index pair of the Einstein convention is obvious as above.
When multiple circles appear in an expression, the pairing must be on a left-to-right order at upper and lower indices.
Metric tensors
\bm{g}
and
{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}}}
are related each other like

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\left[\bm{{\mathcal{E}}}^{t}{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\bm{{\mathcal{E}}}\right]_{\mu\nu}={{\eta^{~}_{\textsc{l}}}}_{\hskip 0.40974pt{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathcal{E}}^{\circ}_{\mu}{\mathcal{E}}^{\circ}_{\nu},~~\text{yielding}~~\textrm{det}[\bm{g}]=\textrm{det}[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}}}]\textrm{det}[\bm{{\mathcal{E}}}]^{2}=-\textrm{det}[\bm{{\mathcal{E}}}]^{2}<0.

Thus, we obtain from (
2.1.6
) that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{-\mathrm{det}[\bm{g}]}\hskip 1.99997ptdx^{0}{\wedge}dx^{1}{\wedge}dx^{2}{\wedge}dx^{3}\!.

(2.1.7)

Similarly, the two-dimensional surface and three-dimensional volume forms are defined as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}_{ab}:=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{ab{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}~~\text{and}~~\mathfrak{V}_{a}:=\frac{1}{3!}\epsilon_{a{\circ\circ}{\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}\!,

(2.1.8)

respectively.

We introduce a connection one-form concerning the local
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO\hskip-1.00006pt}(1,3)
group,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{w}}^{ab}=\omega^{~ab}_{\mu}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}{\hskip 1.99997pt\in\hskip 1.99997pt}V^{2}({{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})\otimes\Omega^{1}({{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}){\otimes}Ad(\mathfrak{g}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO\hskip-0.81949pt}(1,3)}),

namely the
spin connection form
.
Owing to (
2.1.1
), the
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO\hskip-1.00006pt}(1,3)
-covariant differential operator
d_{\mathfrak{w}}
is defined owing to the spin-connection form as

\displaystyle d_{\mathfrak{w}}{\mathfrak{a}}^{a}:=d{\mathfrak{a}}^{a}+{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\hskip 1.00006pt\eta^{~}_{\textsc{l}\hskip 0.81949pt{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{w}}^{a{\circ}}\wedge{\mathfrak{a}}^{\circ}~~\text{for}~~{\mathfrak{a}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}\Omega^{1}({{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}).

(2.1.9)

Two-form object

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{T}}^{a}:=d_{\mathfrak{w}}{\mathfrak{e}}^{a}\in V^{1}({{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})\otimes\Omega^{2}({{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})

(2.1.10)

is referred to as a
torsion form
.

The (
p
-tensor)-(
q
-form) object
{\mathfrak{a}}{\in}V^{p}({{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})\otimes\Omega^{q}({{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})
has a component representation owing to the standard basis such that

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{a}}=a^{i_{1}\cdots i_{p}}_{\hskip 16.38895ptj_{1}\cdots j_{q}}(\partial_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\partial_{i_{p}})({\mathfrak{e}}^{j_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{j_{q}}),

(2.1.11)

Raising and lowering Roman indices for both tensor- and form-indices are done using the metric tensor
{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}}}
as in (
2.1.9
).
Since we represent the tensor basis in
{{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}
with lower indices and the form bases in
{{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}
with upper indices, the tensor coefficient has upper indices and the form coefficient has lower indices, which is called the
intrinsic position
of indices in this report.

From the gauge theoretical point of view, choosing the inertial frame among general coordinate frames breaks the local
G\!L(4)
gauge symmetry as the gauge fixing does.
After the gauge fixing by choosing one of the inertial frames, the inertial manifold still keeps a local
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO\hskip-1.00006pt}(1,3)
symmetry, and its spin connection behaves as a massless gauge boson, a graviton.

In this study, a vector
{\bm{u}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}V({{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})
is defined as a column vector, and its component is represented with a superscript as the intrinsic position.
Components of its transpose
{\bm{u}}^{t}
are represented as a row vector with a subscript as the intrinsic position.
A local
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO\hskip-1.00006pt}(1,3)
invariant bilinear form of two vectors
{\bm{u}},{\bm{v}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}V({{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})
are defined utilizing the metric tensor as

\displaystyle\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{v}}\rangle^{~}_{\textsc{l}}={\bm{u}}^{t}{\!\cdot}{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}}}{\!\cdot}{\bm{v}}={{\eta^{~}_{\textsc{l}}}}_{\circ\circ}\hskip 1.00006ptu^{\circ}v^{\circ}=\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{u}}\rangle^{~}_{\textsc{l}}\in\mathbb{R},

(2.1.12)

yielding the pseudo-norm as
\|{\bm{u}}\|^{~}_{\textsc{l}}:={\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{u}}\rangle^{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}_{\textsc{l}}}^{~}
.
In this report, operator “
\cdot
” shows the standard multiplication between two matrices or a matrix and a vector.
This bilinear form degenerates in the inertial manifold with the Lorentzian metric and can have a negative value.
Local
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO\hskip-1.00006pt}(1,3)
group action
{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}{\in}End({{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})
is known as the Lorentz transformation.
We denote the (
{4\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt4}
)-matrix representation of the
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO\hskip-1.00006pt}(1,3)
group as
\bm{g}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}
, which acts on a vector from the left as

{\bm{u}}^{\prime}=\bm{g}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}\cdot{\bm{u}}
.
The Lorentz transformation of the vierbein form and the spin-connection form are provided as

\displaystyle{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}:\hskip 2.5pt{\mathfrak{e}}\hskip 1.49994pt\mapsto{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}(\hskip 1.49994pt{\mathfrak{e}}\hskip 1.49994pt)

\displaystyle=\hskip 1.49994pt{\mathfrak{e}}^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt=\bm{g}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}{\!\cdot}{\mathfrak{e}},

\displaystyle{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}:{\mathfrak{w}}\mapsto{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}({\mathfrak{w}})

\displaystyle={\mathfrak{w}}^{\prime}=\bm{g}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}{\!\cdot}{\mathfrak{w}}\cdot\bm{g}_{\textsc{l}}^{-1}+{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}}^{\hskip-4.09723pt-1}\bm{g}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}{\!\cdot}d\hskip-1.00006pt\bm{g}_{\textsc{l}}^{\hskip-0.81949pt-1},

yielding the Lorentz transformation of the covariant differential (
2.1.9
) as

\displaystyle{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}(d_{\mathfrak{w}}{\mathfrak{e}})

\displaystyle=d_{{\mathfrak{w}}^{\prime}}{\mathfrak{e}}^{\prime}.

The
O(1,3)
group is not simply connected but separated into two subgroups according to the sign of
[\bm{g}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}]^{0}_{\hskip 2.45836pt0}
.
We define the
orthochronous
and
anti-orthochronous
transformations of
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO\hskip-1.00006pt}(1,3)
as

\displaystyle SO^{\uparrow}(1,3)

\displaystyle:=\{\bm{g}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}SO(1,3)\big|[\bm{g}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}]^{0}_{\hskip 2.45836pt0}>0\},~~\text{and}~~SO^{\downarrow}_{~}(1,3):=\{\bm{g}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}SO(1,3)\big|[\bm{g}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}]^{0}_{\hskip 2.45836pt0}<0\},

respectively.
Elements of
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO\hskip-1.00006pt}^{\uparrow}(1,3)
are called the proper Lorentz transformation in physics.
There are two essential elements of subgroup
O(1,3){\setminus}SO(1,3)
such that:

\displaystyle\bm{O}^{\textsc{t}}:=\text{diag}[-1,1,1,1]~~\text{and}~~\bm{O}^{\textsc{p}}:=\text{diag}[1,-1,-1,-1],

where
\bm{O}^{\textsc{t}}
and
\bm{O}^{\textsc{p}}
are called time-reversal and parity-reversal operators in physics, respectively.

A
space-time curvature
is defined owing to the structure equation as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}^{ab}

\displaystyle:=d{\mathfrak{w}}^{ab}+{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}{\mathfrak{w}}^{a}_{~{\circ}}\wedge{\mathfrak{w}}^{{\circ}b}\in V^{2}({{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})\otimes\Omega^{2}({{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}){\otimes}Ad(\mathfrak{g}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO\hskip-0.81949pt}(1,3)}),

(2.1.13)

which is a two-form valued rank-
2
tensor represented using the standard basis as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}^{ab}

\displaystyle=\sum_{c<d}R^{ab}_{\hskip 5.7361ptcd}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}^{c}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{d}=\frac{1}{2}R^{ab}_{\hskip 5.7361pt{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}.

Tensor coefficient
R^{ab}_{\hskip 5.7361ptcd}
is referred to as the
Riemann curvature
tensor.

Ricci curvature
tensor and
scalar curvature
are defined, respectively, owing to the Riemann curvature tensor as

\displaystyle R_{ab}:=\eta_{\textsc{l}\hskip 0.81949pta\star}\hskip 1.00006ptR^{{\circ}\star}_{\hskip 5.7361pt{\circ}b}~~\textrm{and }~~R:=R^{{\circ}\star}_{\hskip 5.7361pt{\circ}\star}.

(2.1.14)

The first and second Bianchi identities are

\displaystyle d_{\mathfrak{w}}(d_{\mathfrak{w}}{\mathfrak{e}}^{a})={c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\hskip 1.99997pt\eta^{~}_{\textsc{l}\hskip 0.81949pt{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{R}^{a{\circ}}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{{\circ}}~~\mathrm{and}~~d_{\mathfrak{w}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{R}^{ab}=0.

(2.1.15)

We also introduce a Riemannian manifold with Euclidean metric
{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{e}}}}:=\text{diag[}1,1,1,1]
, denoted as
{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}
.
For objects belonging to the Euclidean space, we put the subscript
\bullet^{~}_{\textsc{e}}
instead of
\bullet^{~}_{\textsc{l}}
. E.g., the bilinear form in the Euclidean manifold is

\displaystyle{\bm{v}},{\bm{u}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}V({T\hskip-1.99997pt{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}}),~~\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{v}}\rangle^{~}_{\textsc{e}}={\bm{u}}^{t}{\!\cdot}{{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{e}}}}}{\!\cdot}{\bm{v}}={{\eta^{~}_{\textsc{e}}}}_{\circ\circ}\hskip 1.00006ptu^{\circ}v^{\circ}=\langle{\bm{v}},{\bm{u}}\rangle^{~}_{\textsc{e}}\in\mathbb{R}^{+},

which is positive definite for non-zero vectors and non-degenerate, such as
\|{\bm{u}}\|^{2}_{\textsc{e}}:=\langle{\bm{u}},{\bm{u}}\rangle^{~}_{\textsc{e}}=0\iff{\bm{u}}=0
.
The volume form of the Euclidean space is provided as

\displaystyle(\ref{VolForm})\big|_{\textsc{l}\rightarrow\textsc{e}}\implies\mathfrak{v}_{\textsc{e}}

\displaystyle{:=}\mathrm{det}[\bm{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\textsc{e}}]\hskip 1.99997ptdx^{0}\wedge dx^{1}\wedge dx^{2}\wedge dx^{3}~~\text{with}~~\textrm{det}[\bm{g}^{~}_{\textsc{e}}]=\textrm{det}[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{e}}}}]\textrm{det}[\bm{{\mathcal{E}}}]^{2}=\textrm{det}[\bm{{\mathcal{E}}}]^{2}>0;

thus,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}_{\textsc{e}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mathrm{det}[\bm{g}^{~}_{\textsc{e}}]}\hskip 1.99997ptdx^{0}{\wedge}dx^{1}{\wedge}dx^{2}{\wedge}dx^{3}.

2.1.3
co-Poincaré bundle

The Yang–Mills theory in the Lorentzian metric space has the Poincaré symmetry in addition to the gauge symmetry.
On the other hand, general relativity is not invariant under the four-dimensional translation.
The current author has introduced a modified Poincaré symmetry, keeping general relativity invariant, namely the co-Poincaré symmetry
[
18
,
19
]
.
This section introduces a principal bundle with the co-Poincaré group as the structure group.

The Poincaré group is the semidirect product of Lorentz group and the four-dimensional translation group as

\displaystyle I\hskip-1.49994ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO(1,3)=SO(1,3)\ltimes T^{4},

whose Lie algebra is given as

\displaystyle\left[P_{a},P_{b}\right]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\left[J_{ab},P_{c}\right]

\displaystyle=-\eta_{ac}\hskip 1.00006ptP_{b}+\eta_{bc}\hskip 1.00006ptP_{a},

(2.1.16)

\displaystyle\left[J_{ab},J_{cd}\right]

\displaystyle=-\eta_{ac}\hskip 1.00006ptJ_{bd}+\eta_{bc}\hskip 1.00006ptJ_{ad}-\eta_{bd}\hskip 1.00006ptJ_{ac}+\eta_{ad}\hskip 1.00006ptJ_{bc},

where
P_{a}
and
J_{ab}
are generators of the
T^{4}
group and the
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO\hskip-1.00006pt}(1,3)
group, respectively.
Although the Yang–Mills Lagrangian is invariant under the Poincaré group, the Einstein–Hilbert gravitational Lagrangian does not respect the symmetry
[
20
]
, obstructing quantisation of general relativity.
The current author has discovered a novel symmetry named the co-Poincaré symmetry
[
18
]
which allows the construction of general relativity in four-dimensional space-time and defined the invariant quadratic in four-dimensional space-time owing to the co-Poincaré group
[
21
]
.

The co-Poincaré group, denoted as
{G^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{c}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{p}}}}
, is the Poincaré group that replaced the translation operator with the co-translation operator.
A principal co-Poincaré bundle is defined as a tuple

\displaystyle\left({\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}\otimes{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}},\pi^{~}_{\textsc{i}},{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}},{G^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{c}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{p}}}}\right),

(2.1.17)

which is the same as the inertial bundle except the structure group
{G^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{c}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{p}}}}
.
A generator of the co-translation is defined as
P_{ab}:=P_{a}\iota_{b}
, where
\iota_{b}
is a contraction concerning trivial frame field
\partial_{b}
.
The generator of the co-Poincaré group can be represented by two (
{4\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt4}
)-matrices as

\displaystyle\left[\Theta_{I}\right]_{ab}:=\begin{cases}P_{ab},&I=1\\
J_{ab},&I=2\end{cases}\!,

(2.1.18a)

whose the Lie algebra are provided as

\displaystyle\left[P_{ab},P_{cd}\right]

\displaystyle=0,~~~\left[J_{ab},P_{cd}\right]=-\eta_{ac}\hskip 1.00006ptP_{bd}+\eta_{bc}\hskip 1.00006ptP_{ad}~~\text{and}~~\left[J_{ab},J_{cd}\right]=\text{(\ref{JJ})}.

(2.1.18b)

We denote the structure constants of the co-Poincaré group obtained from the above Lie algebra as

\displaystyle[\Theta_{I},\Theta_{J}]:={\mathcal{F}}_{~IJ}^{K}\hskip 1.00006pt\Theta_{K},

(2.1.19a)

yielding

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathcal{F}}_{~11}^{1}={\mathcal{F}}_{~11}^{2}={\mathcal{F}}_{~12}^{2}={\mathcal{F}}_{~21}^{2}={\mathcal{F}}_{~22}^{1}=0,\\
\left[{\mathcal{F}}_{~12}^{1}\right]_{ab;cd}^{ef}=-\left[{\mathcal{F}}_{~21}^{1}\right]_{cd;ab}^{ef}=\eta_{ac}\hskip 1.00006pt\delta^{e}_{b}\delta^{f}_{d}-\eta_{bc}\hskip 1.00006pt\delta^{e}_{a}\delta^{f}_{d},\\
\left[{\mathcal{F}}_{~22}^{2}\right]_{ab;cd}^{ef}=-\eta_{ac}\hskip 1.00006pt\delta_{b}^{e}\delta_{d}^{f}+\eta_{bc}\hskip 1.00006pt\delta_{a}^{e}\delta_{d}^{f}-\eta_{bd}\hskip 1.00006pt\delta_{a}^{e}\delta_{c}^{f}+\eta_{ad}\hskip 1.00006pt\delta_{b}^{e}\delta_{c}^{f}.\end{array}\right.

Co-Poincaré connection form
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{c}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{p}}
and curvature form
\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{c}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{p}}
are, respectively, introduced as Lie-algebra valued one- and two-form objects concerning the co-Poincaré group, and they are expressed using the trivial basis as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{A}}^{I}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{c}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{p}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}J_{\circ\circ}\otimes{\mathfrak{w}}^{\circ\circ},&\hskip 11.99998ptI=1,\\
P_{\circ\circ}\otimes{\mathfrak{S}}^{\circ\circ},&\hskip 11.99998ptI=2,\end{cases}\hskip 20.00003pt\in{\Omega}^{1}({{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})\otimes Ad(\mathfrak{g}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{c}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{p}}),

(2.1.20a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{I}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{c}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{p}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}J_{\circ\circ}\otimes\mathfrak{R}^{\circ\circ},&I=1,\\
P_{\circ\circ}\otimes d_{\mathfrak{w}}{\mathfrak{S}}^{\circ\circ},&I=2,\end{cases}\hskip 20.00003pt\in{\Omega}^{2}({{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})\otimes Ad(\mathfrak{g}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{c}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{p}}),

(2.1.20b)

where
\mathfrak{g}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{c}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{p}}
is a Lie algebra of the co-Poincaré symmetry.

We note that
P^{\circ\circ}\otimes d_{\mathfrak{w}}{\mathfrak{S}}_{\circ\circ}\in\Omega^{2}({{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})
due to
\iota_{\bullet}:\Omega^{p}\rightarrow\Omega^{p-1}
, e.g.,
\iota_{a}{\mathfrak{e}}^{b}=\delta^{b}_{a}
.

2.2
Hodge-dual operator

A Hodge-dual operator is an indispensable theoretical tool for describing the Yang–Mills theory.
One natural gauge-invariant equation of motion for the gauge field is, e.g.,
d_{G}\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{G}={\mathfrak{J}}
, where
{\mathfrak{J}}
is a three-form object owing to the source field.
However, the left-hand side is identically zero owing to the Bianchi identity.
We cannot exploit it as an equation of motion.
In reality, the gauge-invariant equation of motion is
{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}}(d_{G}{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}}(\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{G}))={\mathfrak{J}}
, where
{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}}
is the Hodge-dual operator.
Together with the structure equation, the equation of motion governs the system’s dynamics.
The Hodge-dual operator induces dynamics into the Yang–Mills theory while keeping the gauge invariance of the equation of motion.

Another role of the Hodge-dual operator is introducing a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading structure for the gauge fields.
Since the Hodge-dual operator maps a
p
-form object to an
(n\hskip-1.49994pt-\hskip-1.49994ptp)
-form object in an
n
-dimensional smooth Riemannian manifold, the Hodge-dual operator is an endomorphism for
n
-form objects in
2n
-dimensional manifolds.
It induces the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading vector space.
This section discusses the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading vector space owing to the Hodge-dual operator in the inertial manifold with the Euclidean and the Lorentzian metric tensors.

2.2.1
Definition and properties

Suppose
p
-form objects
{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{b}}
have coefficient representations such that:

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{a}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{p!}{a}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{p}}{\mathfrak{e}}^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{i_{p}}\hskip 1.99997pt\in{\Omega}^{p}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}_{\bullet}}),~~{\mathfrak{b}}:=\frac{1}{p!}{b}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{p}}{\mathfrak{e}}^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{i_{p}}\hskip 1.99997pt\in{\Omega}^{p}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}_{\bullet}}),\quad(0{\leq}i_{j}{\leq}n-1).

The
SO(4)
or
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO\hskip-1.00006pt}(1,3)
invariant bilinear form is defined using the metric tensor
{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\bullet}}}

(\bullet\in\{\textsc{e},\textsc{l}\})
as

\displaystyle\langle{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{b}}\rangle^{~}_{\bullet}:=\frac{1}{p!}\hskip 1.00006pt\eta_{\bullet}^{\hskip 1.63885pti_{1}j_{1}}\cdots\eta_{\bullet}^{\hskip 1.63885pti_{p}j_{p}}\hskip 1.00006pta^{~}_{i_{1}{\cdots}i_{p}}\hskip 1.00006ptb^{~}_{j_{1}{\cdots}j_{p}}=\langle{\mathfrak{b}},{\mathfrak{a}}\rangle^{~}_{\bullet}\in\mathbb{R},

which is consistent with the inner product in
{{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}
given by (
2.1.12
).
A (pseudo-)norm squared of
{\mathfrak{a}}
is given as

\displaystyle\|{\mathfrak{a}}\|_{\bullet}^{2}

\displaystyle:=\langle{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{a}}\rangle^{~}_{\bullet},

(2.2.1)

which is non-degenerate and positive-definite for
{\mathfrak{a}}\neq 0
in the Euclidean space.
We introduce the Hodge-dual operator in this space:

Definition 2.1

(Hodge-dual operator)
.

Suppose
{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\bullet}}}
is an
n
-dimensional oriented and smooth manifold and
{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{b}}\in{\Omega}^{p}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}_{\bullet}})
are
p
-form objects.
The Hodge-dual operator, denoted as
{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}}
, is defined to give

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{a}}\wedge{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}({\mathfrak{b}}):=\mathrm{det}[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\bullet}}}]^{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}\hskip 1.00006pt\langle{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{b}}\rangle^{~}_{\bullet}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\bullet},~~\bullet\in\{\textsc{e},\textsc{l}\}.

(2.2.2)

Our definition has the extra factor
\mathrm{det}[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\bullet}}}]^{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}
over the standard definition for later convenience.
This factor works to unify Euclidean and Lorentzian Hodge-dual operators in
Section
4
.
Since
{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\bullet}}}
is the constant tensor, the Hodge-dual operator is invariant under the
GL(4)
group.

The Hodge-dual operator has a component representation as

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}:\Omega^{p}\rightarrow\Omega^{n-p}:{\mathfrak{b}}\mapsto\hat{{\mathfrak{b}}}:={\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}({\mathfrak{b}})=\frac{\text{det}[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\bullet}}}]^{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}{p!(n-p)!}{b}_{i_{1}{\cdots}i_{p}}\left[\bm{\epsilon}\right]^{i_{1}{\cdots}i_{p}}_{\hskip 19.66667pti_{p+1}{\cdots}i_{n}}{\mathfrak{e}}^{i_{p+1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{i_{n}},

where

\displaystyle\left[\bm{\epsilon}\right]^{i_{1}{\cdots}i_{p}}_{\hskip 19.66667pti_{p+1}{\cdots}i_{n}}:=\frac{1}{p!}\eta_{\bullet}^{\hskip 1.63885pti_{1}j_{1}}{\cdots}\eta_{\bullet}^{\hskip 1.63885pti_{p}j_{p}}\hskip 1.00006pt\epsilon_{j_{1}\cdots j_{p}i_{p+1}{\cdots}i_{n}},

and
\bm{\epsilon}
is a
n
-dimensional completely anti-symmetric tensor with
[\bm{\epsilon}]_{01\cdots n-1}=+1
.
The operator acts on tensor coefficients of
{\mathfrak{b}}
as

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}:{b}_{i_{1}{\cdots}i_{p}}\mapsto

\displaystyle\hat{b}_{i_{p+1}{\cdots}i_{n}}:=\frac{\mathrm{det}[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\bullet}}}]^{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}{p!}{b}_{i_{1}{\cdots}i_{p}}\left[\bm{\epsilon}\right]^{i_{1}{\cdots}i_{p}}_{\hskip 19.66667pti_{p+1}{\cdots}i_{n}},

with

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathfrak{b}}}=\frac{1}{(n-p)!}\hat{b}_{i_{1}{\cdots}i_{n-p}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{i_{n-p}}.

We obtain the Hodge-dual inverse as

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}\left({\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}({\mathfrak{a}})\right)

\displaystyle=\text{det}[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\bullet}}}](-1)^{p(n-p)}{\mathfrak{a}}\implies{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{-1}}({\mathfrak{a}})=\text{det}[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\bullet}}}]^{-1}(-1)^{p(n-p)}{{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}}({\mathfrak{a}}).

(2.2.3)

The Hodge-dual operator relates with the volume form as

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}(1)

\displaystyle=\text{det}[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\bullet}}}]^{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}^{0}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{n-1}=\text{det}[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\bullet}}}]^{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{v}=\text{det}[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\bullet}}}]\mathrm{det}[\bm{{\mathcal{E}}}]\hskip 1.99997ptdx^{0}\wedge dx^{1}\wedge dx^{2}\wedge dx^{3}.

We define a co-differential operator
{{\hat{d}}}
for
p
-form object
{\mathfrak{a}}
in an
n
-dimensional oriented manifold as

\displaystyle{{\hat{d}}}:\Omega^{p}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}_{\bullet}})\rightarrow\Omega^{p-1}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}_{\bullet}}):{\mathfrak{a}}\mapsto{{\hat{d}}}{\mathfrak{a}}:=

\displaystyle(-1)^{p}\hskip 1.99997pt{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{-1}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(d{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}({\mathfrak{a}})\right)=\text{det}[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\bullet}}}]^{-1}(-1)^{p(n-p+1)}\hskip 1.99997pt{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}\left(d{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}({\mathfrak{a}})\right),

(2.2.4)

where we use (
2.2.3
).
The co-differential is nilpotent like
{{\hat{d}}}\hskip 1.00006pt{{\hat{d}}}=0
as well as the external differential.
We note that the Hodge-dual operator depends on the metric tensor; thus, the co-differential also does.
We omit subscript
d_{\bullet}
(
\hat{d}_{\bullet}
) for simplicity.
The co-differential operator is a weak-adjoint of external differential in the compact manifold
{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\bullet}}}
with
{\mathfrak{a}}\in\Omega^{p-1}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}_{\bullet}})
and
{\mathfrak{b}}\in\Omega^{p}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}_{\bullet}})
such as

\displaystyle\int\langle{d{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{b}}}\rangle^{~}_{\bullet}\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\bullet}

\displaystyle=\text{det}[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\bullet}}}]^{-{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\int d{\mathfrak{a}}\wedge{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}({\mathfrak{b}})=\text{det}[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\bullet}}}]^{-{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\hskip 1.00006pt(-1)^{p}\int{\mathfrak{a}}{\wedge}d{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}({\mathfrak{b}}),

\displaystyle=\text{det}[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\bullet}}}]^{-{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\hskip 1.00006pt\int{\mathfrak{a}}{\wedge}{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}\left(\left(-1\right)^{p}{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{-1}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(d{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}\left({\mathfrak{b}}\right)\right)\right)=\int\langle{{\mathfrak{a}},{{\hat{d}}}{\mathfrak{b}}}\rangle^{~}_{\bullet}\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\bullet}.

The Storks theorem and an assumption that
{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}}
is compact are used here.

The Laplace–Beltrami operator is defined owing to the co-differential as

\displaystyle\Delta^{~}_{\bullet}

\displaystyle:=d\hskip 1.00006pt{{\hat{d}}}+{{\hat{d}}}\hskip 1.00006ptd,

(2.2.5)

which is a linear endomorphism on
\Omega^{p}({{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}})
and is commutable with the Hodge-dual operator.
We note that it is self weak-adjoint like

\displaystyle\langle\Delta^{~}_{\bullet}{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{b}}\rangle^{~}_{\bullet}=\langle d{\hat{d}}{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{b}}\rangle^{~}_{\bullet}+\langle{{\hat{d}}}d{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{b}}\rangle^{~}_{\bullet}

\displaystyle=\langle{\mathfrak{a}},d{{\hat{d}}}{\mathfrak{b}}\rangle^{~}_{\bullet}+\langle{\mathfrak{a}},{{\hat{d}}}d{\mathfrak{b}}\rangle^{~}_{\bullet}=\langle{\mathfrak{a}},\Delta^{~}_{\bullet}{\mathfrak{b}}\rangle^{~}_{\bullet}.

Suppose
n=2m
(
m\in\mathbb{Z}
) is an even integer.
The Hodge-dual operator is endomorphism on the
(m)
-form object since
{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}({\mathfrak{a}}\in\Omega^{m})\in\Omega^{m}
.
For
{\mathfrak{a}}{\in}\Omega^{m}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}})
, the Hodge-dual operator fulfils

\displaystyle\text{(\ref{DHi})}\implies{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}\left({\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}({\mathfrak{a}})\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m^{2}}\text{det}[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\bullet}}}]\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{a}}\in\Omega^{p}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}});

thus,
{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}
has two-eigenvalues of
\pm 1
or
\pm i
depend on a value of
n
and a metric.
An orthonormal basis of
m
-form objects is constructed using the standard basis as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{I}:={\mathfrak{e}}^{i_{p(1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{i_{m)}},~~\textrm{with}~~I=1,\cdots,l:=\frac{n!}{(m!)^{2}},

where
p(1\cdots m)
runs over all possible permutations.
We note that
l
is even integer for any even
n
.
Owing to the definition of the Hodge-dual operator, we can always take
(l/2)
-pairs of bases
(\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{I},\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{J})
fulfiling

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{I})=(-1)^{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{J}~~\textrm{and}~~{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{J})=\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{I}.

Thus, we can construct new bases using pairs
(\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{I},\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{J})
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm}:=\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{I}\pm(-1)^{m}\hskip 1.00006pt\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{J},

(2.2.6)

yielding

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}\left(\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}\left(\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{J}+\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{I}\right)=+(-1)^{m}\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}\in{{\mathbb{V}}^{+}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}(\Omega^{m}),

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}\left(\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{-}\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}\left(\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{J}-\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{I}\right)=-(-1)^{m}\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{-}\in{{\mathbb{V}}^{-}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}(\Omega^{m}).

The
m
-form objects
\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm}
are linearly independent from one another, and any
m
-form objects can be represented using a linear combination of these forms.
Therefore, a space of
m
-form objects splits into two subspaces concerning eigenvalues of the Hodge-dual operator like

\displaystyle{\mathbb{V}}(\Omega^{m})={\mathbb{V}}_{~}^{+}(\Omega^{m})\oplus{\mathbb{V}}_{~}^{-}(\Omega^{m}).

(2.2.7)

Consequently, we confirm that the Hodge-dual operator induces the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading vector space in
\Omega^{m}
.

So far, in this section, we have discussed the Hodge-dual operator in the general
n
-dimensional manifold.
Hereafter, we restrict a space-time manifold to four-dimensional space equipping the Euclidean or the Lorentzian metric.
Projection operators owing to the Hodge-dual operator are defined as

\displaystyle{P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}:=\frac{1}{2}\left(1\pm{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}\right),

(2.2.8)

which fulfils the definition of the projection operator
{P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}\hskip 1.00006pt{P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}={P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}
and
{P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}\hskip 1.00006pt{P^{\mp}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}=0
.
This operator projects a two-form object onto one of two eigenspaces such that

\displaystyle{P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}{\mathfrak{a}}

\displaystyle={\mathfrak{a}}^{\pm}\implies{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}({\mathfrak{a}}^{\pm})=\pm{\mathfrak{a}}^{\pm}\in{{\mathbb{V}}^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}\left(\Omega^{2}_{~}\left({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}_{\bullet}}\right)\right).

(2.2.9)

The two-form object belonging to
{{\mathbb{V}}^{+}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}
(
{{\mathbb{V}}^{-}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}
) is referred to as the self-dual (SD) (the anti-self-dual (ASD)) forms, respectively.
We also call the sign of the eigenvalue as the
chirality
owing to the analogy with the spinor space.
We assign chiral-right to SD-forms and chiral-left to ASD-forms.

2.2.2
two-form bases of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading vector space

Euclidean metric:

For the standard one-form bases
{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{a}{\in}\Omega^{1}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}})

(a=0,1,2,3)
, the Hodge-dual operator on two-form objects provides that

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\textsc{e}}^{~}}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{1})={\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{2}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{3},~~~{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\textsc{e}}^{~}}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{2})=-{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{1}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{3},~~~{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\textsc{e}}^{~}}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{3})={\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{1}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{2},

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\textsc{e}}^{~}}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{1}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{2})={\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{3},~~~{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\textsc{e}}^{~}}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{1}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{3})=-{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{2},~~~{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\textsc{e}}^{~}}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{2}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{3})={\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{1}.

Thus, we obtain the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading two-form bases as, e.g.,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}^{+\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\textsc{e}}:=\left\{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{1}+{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{2}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{3},\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{2}-{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{1}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{3},\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{3}+{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{1}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{2}\right\},

(2.2.10a)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}^{-\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\textsc{e}}:=\left\{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{1}-{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{2}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{3},\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{2}+{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{1}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{3},\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{3}-{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{1}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{e}}^{2}\right\},

(2.2.10b)

yielding

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\textsc{e}}^{~}}\left({\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm}_{\textsc{e}}\right)=\pm{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm}_{\textsc{e}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}{{\mathbb{V}}^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}},~~{P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}\left({\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm}_{\textsc{e}}\right)={\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm}_{\textsc{e}},~~\text{and}~~{P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}\left({\mathfrak{S}}^{\mp}_{\textsc{e}}\right)=0.

(2.2.11)

Lorentzian metric:

We can introduce the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading two-form bases in the Lorentzian metric space as the same as it in the Euclidean space.
Using the orthonormal bases
{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{a}{\in}\Omega^{1}({{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})

(a=0,1,2,3)
in the Lorentzian metric space, we provide the Hodge-dual operator on two-form objects as

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\textsc{l}}^{~}}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{1})=\hskip 8.99994pti\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{2}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{3},~~~{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\textsc{l}}^{~}}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{2})=-i\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{1}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{3},~~~{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\textsc{l}}^{~}}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{3})=\hskip 8.99994pti\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{1}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{2},

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\textsc{l}}^{~}}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{1}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{2})=-i\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{3},~~~{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\textsc{l}}^{~}}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{1}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{3})=\hskip 8.99994pti\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{2},~~~{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\textsc{l}}^{~}}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{2}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{3})=-i\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{1},

where imaginary unit “
i
” appears owing to factor det
[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}}}]
in the definition.
The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading two-form bases are provided as, e.g.,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}^{+\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\textsc{l}}:=\left\{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{1}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{2}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{3},\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{2}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{1}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{3},\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{3}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{1}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{2}\right\},

(2.2.12a)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}^{-\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\textsc{l}}:=\left\{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{1}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{2}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{3},\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{2}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{1}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{3},\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{0}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{3}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{1}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\textsc{l}}^{2}\right\},

(2.2.12b)

yielding
(\ref{DHES})
with “
e
”
\rightarrow
“
l
”.
The Hodge-dual operator induces the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading spaces for curvature two-form
\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{l}}
in the Lorentzian metric space, namely SD and ASD curvatures, as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{l}}^{\pm}:={P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{l}}\implies{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\textsc{l}}^{~}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{l}}^{\pm})=\pm\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{l}}^{\pm}\in{{\mathbb{V}}^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}\left(\Omega_{\textsc{l}}^{2}\right),

where
\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{l}}^{\pm}
is, respectively, spanned by
\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm}_{\textsc{l}}
.

2.3
Group representations

The special principle of relativity requires that representations of the space-time symmetry group present the classical field in the local inertial space.
The Yang-Mills theory involves the space-time symmetry group and the
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(\!N\!)
gauge group.
These groups have homomorphism among various Lie groups.
E.g., four-dimensional vector spaces with Euclidean and Lorentzian metrics, respectively, have accidental Lie-algebra homomorphism as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{o}(4)

\displaystyle={\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}(2)\oplus{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}(2),

(2.3.1a)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{o}(1,3)\otimes\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle={\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C})\oplus\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C})}.

(2.3.1b)

The subspaces of them have

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{o}(3)

\displaystyle={\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}(2),

(2.3.1c)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{o}(3)\otimes\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle={\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}(2)\otimes\mathbb{C}={\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C}).

(2.3.1d)

According to these homomorphisms, four-dimensional vectors in the Euclidean and Lorentzian metric spaces have complex valued (
{2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2}
)-matrix representations.

Spinor spaces have following group homomorphisms:

\displaystyle Spin(4)

\displaystyle=SU(2)\times SU(2)~~\text{and}~~Spin(1,3)={S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})},

(2.3.2a)

inducing corresponding Lie algebra homomorphisms.
The spin group doubly covers the rotation group as

\displaystyle Spin(4)=SO(4)\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}U(1)~~\text{and}~~Spin(1,3)=SO(1,3)\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}U(1).

(2.3.2b)

This section introduces irreducible representations of
{SO(4)}
and
{SO(1,3)}
, according to Refs.
[
22
,
23
]
.
Owing to abovementioned homomorphisms, irreducible representations of
{SO(4)}
and
{SO(1,3)}
groups can be constructed using those of
SU(2)
and
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
, respectively.
We introduce
SU(2)
and
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
irreducible representations below.

2.3.1
Irreducible representations

a) Euclidean metric space:

We introduce a space of two complex numbers on the unit three-sphere,

\displaystyle S^{3}

\displaystyle:=\left\{z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{C}\hskip 1.00006pt\big|\hskip 1.00006pt|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2}=1\right\}.

(2.3.3)

Each point on
S^{3}
induces an
SU(2)
matrix

\displaystyle\bm{M}

\displaystyle:=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}z_{1}&-z_{2}\\
z_{2}^{*}&z_{1}^{*}\end{array}\right)~~\text{yielding}~~\text{det}[\bm{M}]=1,~~\bm{M}^{\dagger}{\!\cdot}\bm{M}=\bm{1}_{2},

where
\bm{1}_{2}
is a unit (
{2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2}
)-matrix.
We define a
SU(2)
-spinor as a polynomial of the form

\displaystyle{{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{e}}_{\hskip-1.63885pts}}\ni\lambda_{s}^{~}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq k\leq 2s}c_{k}z_{1}^{k}z_{2}^{2s-k},~~\text{where}~~c_{k}\in\mathbb{R},~~\{z_{1},z_{2}\}\in S^{3},

(2.3.4)

where
{{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{e}}_{\hskip-1.63885pts}}
is a space of
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2)
spinors.
Owing to homomorphism (
2.3.1a
), we call the
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2)
spinor the
Euclidean
spinor and put the suffix “
e
” on one with the
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2)
symmetry.
A parameter
s
takes a positive half-integer, namely a
spin
of spinor
\lambda_{s}^{~}
.

For group action
\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}SU(2)
, its representation
\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pts}\left(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}\right)
acts on the spinor
\lambda_{s}^{~}(z_{1},z_{2})
as

\displaystyle\left(\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pts}(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}){\circ}\lambda_{s}^{~}\right)(z_{1},z_{2})=\lambda_{s}^{~}(z^{\prime}_{1},z^{\prime}_{2}),~~~\text{with}~~~\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}z^{\prime}_{1}\\
z^{\prime}_{2}\end{array}\!\right)=\bm{g}^{-1}_{\textsc{e}}\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}z^{~}_{1}\\
z^{~}_{2}\end{array}\!\right).

The complex column vector in (
2.3.1
) represents the Euclidean spinor.
The Lie-algebra representation is provided by the derivative of the group representation such as

\displaystyle\dot{\pi}_{\hskip-0.81949pts}\left(\mathfrak{g}^{~}_{\textsc{e}}\right){\circ}\lambda_{s}^{~}

\displaystyle:=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\lambda_{s}^{~}(e^{t\mathfrak{g}}\hskip 1.00006ptz_{1},e^{t\mathfrak{g}}\hskip 1.00006ptz_{2})\right|_{t=0},

where
\mathfrak{g}^{~}_{\textsc{e}}\in{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}(2)
is any (
{2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2}
) unitary-matrix.

We define an
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2)
-invariant inner product for spinors as the Gaussian integral such that

\displaystyle\langle\lambda^{\prime}_{s},\lambda_{s}^{~}\rangle

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}{\lambda^{\prime}_{s}}^{*}\lambda_{s}^{~}e^{-(|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2})}dx^{~}_{1}\hskip 1.00006ptdy^{~}_{1}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{~}_{2}\hskip 1.00006ptdy^{~}_{2},~~z^{~}_{a}:=x^{~}_{a}+iy^{~}_{a}<\infty,

(2.3.9)

yielding the norm

\displaystyle\|\lambda_{s}^{~}\|

\displaystyle:=\langle\lambda_{s}^{~},\lambda_{s}^{~}\rangle^{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}.

(2.3.10)

Representation
\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pts}
is the irreducible, labelled by the half-integer
s
.
Each spinor
\lambda_{s}^{~}
consists of
2s+1
monomials corresponding to the spin projection on the specific axis in physics.
When we set monomial’s coefficient
c^{~}_{k}
for
\lambda_{s}^{~}
in (
2.3.4
) as

\displaystyle c^{~}_{k}=\left(k!(2s-k)!\right)^{-{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}},

monomials
c^{~}_{k}z^{k}_{1}z^{2s-k}_{2}
compose the orthonormal basis
\{\lambda_{s}^{~}\}
by means of (
2.3.9
).

When
s={1\hskip-1.00006pt/\hskip-0.50003pt2}
, we have a matrix representation as follows:
The
SU(2)
-spinor at
\bm{z}:=(z_{1},z_{2})^{t}
is represented using the coefficient vector
\tilde{\bm{c}}:=(\tilde{c}_{1},\tilde{c}_{2})^{t}
as

\displaystyle\lambda_{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}^{~}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\bm{c}}^{t}{\!\cdot}\bm{z}=(\tilde{c}_{1},\tilde{c}_{2})\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}z_{1}\\
z_{2}\end{array}\!\right)=\tilde{c}_{1}z_{1}+\tilde{c}_{2}z_{2},

where
\tilde{c}_{i}
is relabeling of
c_{i}
as
(c_{0},c_{1})\rightarrow(\tilde{c}_{2},\tilde{c}_{1})
.
Accordingly, the group action is written as

\displaystyle\left(\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}){\circ}\lambda_{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}^{~}\right)(z_{1},z_{2})=\lambda_{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}^{~}(z^{\prime}_{1},z^{\prime}_{2})=\tilde{\bm{c}}^{t}{\!\cdot}\left(\bm{g}^{\dagger}_{\textsc{e}}{\!\cdot}\bm{z}\right)=\left(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}{\!\cdot}\tilde{\bm{c}}\right)^{\dagger}{\!\cdot}\bm{z}.

Therefore, we obtain
\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}})=\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}
; thus,
\dot{\pi}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}(\mathfrak{g}^{~}_{E})=\mathfrak{g}^{~}_{E}
.

The irreducible unitary representation of
Spin(4)\hskip-1.00006pt=\hskip-1.00006ptSU(2){\times}SU(2)
consists of a tensor product

\displaystyle\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pts^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt1}\hskip-1.63885pt{\otimes}s^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt2}}^{\textsc{e}}

\displaystyle:=\left(\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pts^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt1}}\!(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}})\hskip 1.00006pt{\otimes}\hskip 1.00006pt\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pts^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt2}}\!(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}),{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{e}}_{\hskip-1.63885pts^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt1}}{\otimes}{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{e}}_{\hskip-1.63885pts^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt2}}\right)~~\text{with}~~s^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt1},s^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt2}\hskip-1.00006pt=\hskip-1.00006pt0,\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{3}{2},2,\cdots,

(2.3.11)

yielding the Lie-algebra representation of
{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{o}(4)
as
\dot{\pi}_{\hskip-0.81949pts^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt1}\hskip-1.63885pt{\otimes}s^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt2}}^{\textsc{e}}:=\dot{\pi}_{s^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt1}}{\otimes}\hskip 1.99997pt\dot{\pi}_{s^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt2}}
, owing to homomorphism (
2.3.1a
).

b) Lorentzian metric space:

Similarly, we can label
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
spinors as the complex
SU(2)
representation owing to the Lie-algebra homomorphism of (
2.3.1d
).

{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
has two fundamental (non-unitary) complex-representations for
\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
as

\displaystyle\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}})=\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}=\left(\bm{g}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}^{t}\right)^{-1}~~~\text{and}~~~{\pi}_{\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}^{~}(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}})=\bm{g}_{\textsc{l}}^{*}=\left(\bm{g}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}^{\dagger}\right)^{-1}.

(2.3.12)

Owing to the homomorphism (
2.3.1b
), we call the
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
representations the
Lorentzian
and use the suffix “
l
” on one having the
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
symmetry.

In this case,
\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}
acts on
\{z^{~}_{1},z^{~}_{2}\}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}\mathbb{C}^{2}
as (
2.3.1
). Corresponding Lie algebra
\dot{\pi}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}(\mathfrak{g}_{\textsc{l}})=\mathfrak{g}_{\textsc{l}}
is non-unitary, thus,
\mathfrak{g}_{\textsc{l}}^{\dagger}\neq\mathfrak{g}_{\textsc{l}}^{-1}\implies\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}})\neq{\pi}_{\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}^{~}(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}})
.
Since
SO(1,3)
is not simply connected,
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
, which is simply connected, cannot cover whole
SO(1,3)
but covers
SO^{\uparrow}\hskip-1.00006pt(1,3)
entirely.

Owing to the Lie algebra homomorphism (
2.3.1b
), we obtain the representation of
{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{o}(1,3)
as a tensor product of these two
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
representations as

\displaystyle\dot{\pi}_{\hskip-0.81949pts^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt1}\hskip-1.63885pt{\otimes}\bar{s}^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt2}}^{\textsc{l}}

\displaystyle:=\dot{\pi}_{\hskip-0.81949pts^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt1}}\!(\mathfrak{g}^{~}_{\textsc{l}})\hskip 1.00006pt{\otimes}\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{\pi}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\hskip-0.81949pt\overline{s}^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt2}}\!(\mathfrak{g}^{~}_{\textsc{l}})~~\implies~~\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pts^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt1}\hskip-1.63885pt{\otimes}\bar{s}^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt2}}^{\textsc{l}}:=\left(\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pts^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt1}}\!(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}})\hskip 1.00006pt{\otimes}\hskip 1.00006pt\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pt\hskip-0.81949pt\overline{s}^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt2}}\!(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}),{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-1.63885pts^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt1}}{\otimes}{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-1.63885pt\overline{s}^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt2}}\right),

(2.3.13)

where
s^{~}_{1}
and
s^{~}_{2}
take a value on half-integers as in (
2.3.11
).
The vector spaces equipping Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C})
(
\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C})}
) is denoted as
{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-1.63885pts}
(
{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-1.63885pt\overline{s}}
), respectively.
We also introduce Lorentzian bi-spinor spaces as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-1.63885pt\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}},{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}:={\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-1.63885pt\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}{\otimes}{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-1.63885pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}.

(2.3.14)

2.4
Clifford algebra

We introduce the Clifford algebra to define the spin group.
The Clifford algebra induces the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading vector space corresponding to left and right chiralities.
The chiral structure of the Yang-Mills theory is an essential ingredient in the standard theory of particle physics.
While the Hodge-dual operator induces the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading vector space for two-form objects in the four-dimensional manifold, the Clifford algebra does it for spinors.
In physics, a spinor field corresponds to a fermion, and a curvature two-form does to a bosonic force field.
Thus, both fermions and bosons in particle physics are categorised owing to chirality, thanks to the Clifford algebra and the Hodge-dual operator.

Suppose
V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}
is an
n
-dimensional vector space defined in a manifold equipping metric tensor
\bm{\eta}
.
The Clifford algebra in
V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}
, denoted as
{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}(V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn})
, is an algebra fulfiling

\displaystyle\zeta,\chi\hskip 1.00006pt{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}(V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}),~~~\zeta\chi+\chi\zeta=2\langle\zeta,\chi\rangle_{{\mathcal{C}\hskip-0.81949ptl}}^{~},

(2.4.1a)

where
\langle\bullet,\bullet\rangle_{{\mathcal{C}\hskip-0.81949ptl}}^{~}
is the bilinearr form defined owing to the metric tensor as

\displaystyle\langle\zeta,\chi\rangle_{{\mathcal{C}\hskip-0.81949ptl}}^{~}

\displaystyle:={\bm{\zeta}}^{t}\cdot\bm{\eta}\cdot{\bm{\chi}}=\eta^{~}_{\circ\circ}\hskip 1.00006pt\zeta^{\circ}\chi^{\circ}\!.

(2.4.1b)

We represent the Clifford algebra primarily as a column vector and denote its component with a superscript; its transpose is a row vector denoted with a subscript.
Corresponding to each orthonormal base vector
e^{a}\in V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}
, the Clifford algebra
\gamma^{a}
exists as

\displaystyle\gamma:V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}(V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}):e^{a}\mapsto\gamma^{a},~~\text{with}~~a=0,1,\cdots,n-1

A space of
{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}(V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn})
is spanned by the power set
\{(\gamma^{0})^{i_{0}}(\gamma^{1})^{i_{1}}\cdots(\gamma^{n-1})^{i_{n-1}}\}
with
i_{a}=0
or
1
; thus,
{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}(V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn})
is a (
2^{n}
)-dimensional space.

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}_{0}(V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn})
is a subgroup containing an even number of Clifford algebras.
The spinor group in
V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}
, denoted as
Spin(V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn})
, is defined as

\displaystyle Spin(V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}):=\left\{\gamma{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}{\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.00006ptl}_{0}(V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn})\big|\overset{*}{\gamma}\gamma=\pm\bm{1}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt},\hskip 1.99997pt\overset{*}{\gamma}{\bm{v}}\gamma{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}~\text{for}~\forall{\bm{v}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}\right\}\!,

where
\gamma=\gamma^{a^{~}_{1}}_{~}\cdots\gamma^{a^{~}_{m}}_{~}
and
\overset{*}{\gamma}:=\gamma^{a^{~}_{m}}_{~}\cdots\gamma^{a^{~}_{1}}_{~}
.
It forms a group concerning the Clifford product.
We define operator
\tau(\gamma){\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}End(V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn})
acting on
{\bm{v}}{\in}V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}
as

\displaystyle\tau(\gamma)({\bm{v}})

\displaystyle:=\gamma{{\bm{v}}}\overset{\hskip 1.63885pt*}{\gamma},

(2.4.2)

which preserves the bilinear form of two vectors
{\bm{v}},{\bm{u}}\in V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}
as

\displaystyle\langle\tau(\gamma)({\bm{v}}),\tau(\gamma)({\bm{u}})\rangle=\langle\gamma{{\bm{v}}}\overset{\hskip 1.63885pt*}{\gamma},\gamma{{\bm{u}}}\overset{\hskip 1.63885pt*}{\gamma}\rangle=\left(\text{sign}[\gamma\overset{*}{\gamma}]\right)^{2}\langle{{\bm{v}}},{{\bm{u}}}\rangle=\langle{{\bm{v}}},{{\bm{u}}}\rangle.

When
n\geq 3
, operator
\tau(\bullet)
induces double covering of
O(n)
, such that
Spin(V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn})/\pm 1{\rightarrow}O(n)
.

Hereafter, we discuss the Clifford algebra in four-dimensional spaces.
When
V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}
has the
SO(4)
or
SO(1,3)
symmetry, we denote
Spin(V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn})
as
Spin(4)
or
Spin(1,3)
, respectively.
We define the chiral operator as

\displaystyle Spin(V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}){\ni}\Gamma^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pts}:=\sqrt{\frac{1}{\text{det}[\bm{\eta}]}}\gamma^{0}\gamma^{1}\gamma^{2}\gamma^{3},~~

\displaystyle\text{where}~~\text{det}[\bm{\eta}]=\begin{cases}+1&\bm{\eta}={{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{e}}}}\\
-1&\bm{\eta}={{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}}}\end{cases},

(2.4.3)

yielding
\Gamma^{2}_{\hskip-0.81949pts}=\bm{1}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
owing to the algebra (
2.4.1a
).
The chiral operator induces the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading structure in
Spin(V_{\hskip-0.81949ptn})
as follows:
Since
\Gamma^{2}_{\hskip-0.81949pts}=\bm{1}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
, the chiral operator has two eigenvectors with eigenvalues
\pm 1
.
We denote a space of the eigenvectors of the chiral operator as
{{\mathbb{V}}^{~}_{\hskip-1.63885pts}}
, namely the spinor space.
Owing to the definition, we obtain that

\displaystyle(\ref{Gamma})

\displaystyle\implies\Gamma^{~}_{\!s}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma^{a}=-\gamma^{a}\hskip 1.00006pt\Gamma^{~}_{\!s},~~\text{for}~~^{\forall}\!a\in\{0,1,2,3\}.

(2.4.4)

We define two projection operators using the chiral operator as

\displaystyle P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pts}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\bm{1}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\pm\Gamma^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pts}\right)\hskip 3.00003pt{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip 3.00003ptEnd({{\mathbb{V}}^{~}_{\hskip-1.63885pts}})\implies P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pts}P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pts}=P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pts}~~\text{and}~~P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pts}P^{\mp}_{\hskip-0.81949pts}=0.

(2.4.5)

The spinor space splits into a disjoint pair,

\displaystyle{{\mathbb{V}}^{~}_{\hskip-1.63885pts}}={{\mathbb{V}}^{+}_{\hskip-1.63885pts}}{\oplus}{{\mathbb{V}}^{-}_{\hskip-1.63885pts}},

(2.4.6a)

such as

\displaystyle\xi^{\pm}

\displaystyle:=P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pts}\xi\hskip 3.00003pt{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip 3.00003pt{{\mathbb{V}}^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.63885pts}},~~\text{where}~~{{\mathbb{V}}^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.63885pts}}:=\text{Im}\left(P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pts}\right)=\text{Ker}\left(P^{\mp}_{\hskip-0.81949pts}\right).

(2.4.6b)

An eigenvector of the chiral operator,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts}^{~}:End({{\mathbb{V}}^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.63885pts}}):\xi^{\pm}\mapsto\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts}^{~}\hskip 1.00006pt\xi^{\pm}=\pm\xi^{\pm},

(2.4.7)
