is called the
chiral spinor
, and its eigenvalue is called the
chirality
in this study.
We assign the spinor with a positive eigenvalue to the
chiral-right
and a negative eigenvalue to the
chiral-left
.
The following sections introduce concrete examples of the Clifford algebra with Euclidean and Lorentzian metrics.

2.4.1
Clifford algebra in Euclidean metric

a) representation of dimension-2 :

The Clifford algebra has the
({2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2})
-matrix representation, e.g.,

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}^{~}_{{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\gamma^{0}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}},\gamma^{1}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}},\gamma^{2}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}},\gamma^{3}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\right\}^{t}=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&i\\
-i&0\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&j\\
-j&0\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&k\\
-k&0\end{array}\right)\right\}^{t}\!,

where
\{i,j,k\}
are imaginary quaternions fulfiling

\displaystyle i^{2}=j^{2}=k^{2}=-1,~~~ij+ji=jk+kj=ki+ik=0,~~\text{and}~~ij=k.

(2.4.8i)

Each
({2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2})
-matrix in column vector (
2.4.8
) fulfils relation (
2.4.1a
) with the Euclidean metric
\bm{\eta}={{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{e}}}}
.
We note that each Euclidean Clifford algebra is Hermitian as
(\gamma^{a}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}})^{\dagger}=\gamma^{a}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}
for
a\in\{0,1,2,3\}
, where complex conjugate is taken by means of quaternions.
We refer to this representation as the Euclidean
representation of dimension-2
and put the suffix “
se
’.

The chiral operator is given as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{1}{\text{det}[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{e}}}}]}}\gamma^{0}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\gamma^{1}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\gamma^{2}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\gamma^{3}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}=\gamma^{0}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\gamma^{1}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\gamma^{2}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\gamma^{3}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{rc}-1&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right),

yielding the projection operator as

\displaystyle P^{+}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}:=\frac{1+\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}}{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)~~\text{and}~~P^{-}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}:=\frac{1-\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}}{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right).

b) representation of dimension-4:

Linear combinations of unit quaternions forms the symplectic group denoted as
Sp(1)
.
The symplectic group has group homomorphism
Sp(1){=}SU(2)
; thus, quaternions have the
({2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2})
-matrix representation using the Pauli matrices.
When we replace quaternions with Pauli matrices as

\displaystyle\Sigma^{~}_{\textsc{e}}:Sp(1){\rightarrow}SU(2):\{1,i,j,k\}\mapsto{{\bm{\sigma}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}

\displaystyle:=\{\bm{1}^{~}_{2},-i\hskip 1.00006pt\bm{\sigma}\},

(2.4.15)

they follow the quaternion algebra (
2.4.8i
), where
\bm{\sigma}=(\bm{\sigma}^{1},\bm{\sigma}^{3},\bm{\sigma}^{3})
are the Pauli matrices.
Thus, this replacement on the spinor representation (
2.4.8
) (in addition, zero and unity are replaced by corresponding
({2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2})
-matrices) yields a four-component vector consisting of the
({4\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt4})
-matrix like

\displaystyle\Sigma^{~}_{\textsc{e}}:\bm{\gamma}^{~}_{{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}\mapsto\bm{\gamma}^{~}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}^{0},\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}^{1},\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}^{2},\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}^{3}\right)^{t},

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}\bm{0}_{2}&\bm{1}_{2}\\
\bm{1}_{2}&\bm{0}_{2}\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}\bm{0}_{2}&-i\bm{\sigma}^{1}\\
i\bm{\sigma}^{1}&\bm{0}_{2}\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}\bm{0}_{2}&-i\bm{\sigma}^{2}\\
i\bm{\sigma}^{2}&\bm{0}_{2}\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}\bm{0}_{2}&-i\bm{\sigma}^{3}\\
i\bm{\sigma}^{3}&\bm{0}_{2}\end{array}\right)\right)^{t}\!,

where transpose is not concerned with matrices but with the four vectors to set them as column ones.
Direct calculations show
\bm{\gamma}^{~}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}
fulfils relation (
2.4.1a
) with the Euclidean metric
{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{e}}}}
and

\displaystyle\gamma^{a}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}\gamma^{a\dagger}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}=\bm{1}_{2},~~\text{det}[\gamma^{a}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}]=1\implies\gamma^{a}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}SU(2)~~\text{for}~~a\in\{0,1,2,3\}.

The projection operator is also obtained by
\Sigma^{~}_{\textsc{e}}
like

\displaystyle\Sigma^{~}_{\textsc{e}}:P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}\mapsto P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}=P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}\big|^{~}_{0\rightarrow\bm{0}_{2},1\rightarrow\bm{1}_{2}}.

(2.4.24)

These operators’ operands are four-component
SU(2)
spinors.
In this report, we called this representation the
representation of dimension-4
and put the suffix “
ve
”.

2.4.2
Clifford algebra in Lorentzian metric

For the Lorentzian Clifford algebra, we start from the (
{4\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt4}
)-representation.

a) representation of dimension-4:

Instead of map (
2.4.15
), the following replacement of (
2.4.8
) provides the Clifford algebra with the Lorentzian metric:

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\pm}_{\textsc{l}}:Sp(1){\rightarrow}{\cal{H}}(2)\cap{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}:\{1,i,j,k\}\mapsto\bm{\sigma}^{\pm}_{\textsc{l}}

\displaystyle:=\{\bm{1}^{~}_{2},\pm\bm{\sigma}\},

(2.4.25)

where
{\cal{H}}(2)
shows a space of
({2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2})
-Hermitian matrices.
When we set
\bm{\eta}={{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}}}
in (
2.4.1b
), (
2.4.25
) yields the Clifford algebra (
2.4.1a
).
The Clifford algebra with the Lorentzian metric in the vector representation is provided as

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}^{~}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}=\left(\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{0},\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{1},\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{2},\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{3}\right)^{t}=\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}\bm{0}_{2}&\bm{1}_{2}\\
\bm{1}_{2}&\bm{0}_{2}\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}\bm{0}_{2}&\bm{\sigma}^{1}\\
-\bm{\sigma}^{1}&\bm{0}_{2}\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}\bm{0}_{2}&\bm{\sigma}^{2}\\
-\bm{\sigma}^{2}&\bm{0}_{2}\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}\bm{0}_{2}&\bm{\sigma}^{3}\\
-\bm{\sigma}^{3}&\bm{0}_{2}\end{array}\right)\right)^{t},

yielding the chiral representation of the gamma matrices in particle physics.
Direct calculations show
\bm{\gamma}^{~}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}
fulfils relation (
2.4.1a
) with the Lorentzian metric
{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}}}
and

\displaystyle\gamma^{a}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}=\gamma^{a\dagger}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}},~~\text{det}[\gamma^{a}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}]=1\implies\gamma^{a}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}{\cal{H}}(2)\cap{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}~~\text{for}~~a\in\{0,1,2,3\}.

We refer to this representation as the Lorentzian
representation of dimension-4
and put the suffix “
vl
’.
For this representation, the chiral operator is given as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{1}{\text{det}[{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}}}]}}\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{0}\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{1}\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{2}\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{3}=i\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{0}\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{1}\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{2}\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{rc}-\bm{1}_{2}&\bm{0}_{2}\\
\bm{0}_{2}&\bm{1}_{2}\end{array}\right).

The projection operator is also obtained by
\Sigma^{\pm}_{\textsc{l}}
like

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\pm}_{\textsc{l}}:P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}\mapsto P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}=P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}\big|^{~}_{0\rightarrow\bm{0}_{2},1\rightarrow\bm{1}_{2}}.

(2.4.36)

Chiral operator (
2.4.2
) is the same as the vector representation of the Euclidean chiral operator (
2.4.1
) thanks to the factor
\sqrt{1/\text{det}[\bm{\eta}]}
in (
2.4.3
).
Thus, the vector representation of the Lorentzian projection operator is also the same as that in the Euclidean as

\displaystyle P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}=P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}=\text{(\ref{2x2PE})}.

(2.4.37)

b) representation of dimension-2 :

Next, we discuss the (
{2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2}
)-representation.
Corresponding to the two irreducible representations,
{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C})
and
\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C})}
,
\bm{\gamma}^{~}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}
is split into two representations, such that

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}^{~}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\begin{cases}\bm{\gamma}^{~}_{{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}:=\left\{\gamma^{0}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}},\hskip 8.00003pt\sqrt{-1}\gamma^{1}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}},\hskip 8.00003pt\sqrt{-1}\gamma^{2}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}},\hskip 8.00003pt\sqrt{-1}\gamma^{3}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\right\}^{t}&\text{for}~~{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C}),\\
\overline{\bm{\gamma}}^{~}_{{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}:=\left\{\gamma^{0}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}},-\sqrt{-1}\gamma^{1}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}},-\sqrt{-1}\gamma^{2}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}},-\sqrt{-1}\gamma^{3}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\right\}^{t}=\bm{O}^{\textsc{p}}{\!\cdot}\bm{\gamma}^{~}_{{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}&\text{for}~~\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C})}.\end{cases}

(2.4.38)

We refer to this representation as the Lorentzian
representation of dimension-2
and put the suffix “
sl
”.
These representations have two different imaginary units as
\sqrt{-1}\in\mathbb{C}
and
i\in\mathbb{H}
.
We define the Clifford module
\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathbb{H}(2)
as

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{C},\beta_{i}\in\mathbb{H}\hskip 6.00006pt\longrightarrow(\alpha_{1}\otimes\beta_{1})(\alpha_{2}\otimes\beta_{2})=\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\otimes\beta_{1}\beta_{2},

(2.4.39a)

\displaystyle\alpha\in\mathbb{C},\beta\in\mathbb{H}(2)\longrightarrow(\alpha\otimes\beta)^{\dagger}=\alpha^{*}\otimes\beta^{\dagger},

(2.4.39b)

where
\mathbb{H}(2)
is a set of (
{2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2}
) quaternion matrices.
We note that

\displaystyle(\sqrt{-1}\otimes 1)\times(1{\otimes}i)=\sqrt{-1}{\otimes}i\neq-1\xrightarrow{\text{s.h.n}.}\sqrt{-1}\hskip 1.99997pti\neq-1

where the last expression is the shorthand notation (s.h.n.).
When we distinguish imaginary unit
\sqrt{-1}\in\mathbb{C}
from quaternion
i\in\mathbb{H}
, we can omit “
\otimes
” without any confusion.
Hereafter, we denote the imaginary unit in
\mathbb{C}
as
\sqrt{-1}\in\mathbb{C}
to distinguish it from
i\in\mathbb{H}
, when necessary.

These two Clifford algebras induce chiral operators as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{-1}\gamma_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{0}\gamma_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{1}\gamma_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{2}\gamma_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{3}=\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}=-\overline{\Gamma}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}},

(2.4.40)

which yielding projection operators as

\displaystyle P^{+}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}=\bar{P}^{-}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}=P^{+}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}~~\text{and}~~P^{-}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}=\bar{P}^{+}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}=P^{-}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}.

(2.4.41)

The Lorentzian Clifford algebra maintains

\displaystyle(\gamma^{\hskip 3.27773pta}_{\bullet\textsc{l}})^{\dagger}=\gamma^{\hskip 3.27773pt0}_{\bullet\textsc{l}}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma^{\hskip 3.27773pta}_{\bullet\textsc{l}}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma^{\hskip 3.27773pt0}_{\bullet\textsc{l}},~~~a\in\{0,1,2,3\},

for both
\bullet=\textsc{v}
and
\bullet=\textsc{s}
, as well as the Euclidean Clifford algebra
\bm{\gamma}_{\bullet\textsc{e}}
.

2.5
Chiral spinors

We introduced the chiral spinors
\xi^{\pm}
as the eigenvector of the chiral operator (
2.4.7
).
This section discusses them in detail in Euclidean and Lorentzian metric spaces.

2.5.1
Higgs spinor

The
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2)
-spinor with spin
s={1\hskip-1.00006pt/\hskip-0.50003pt2}
has a two-dimensional column-vector representation as introduce in (
2.3.1
) such that

\displaystyle\lambda^{~}_{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}({\phi_{\textsc{h}}^{\textsc{u}}},{\phi_{\textsc{h}}^{\textsc{d}}})

\displaystyle:={\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}=\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}{\phi_{\textsc{h}}^{\textsc{u}}}\\
{\phi_{\textsc{h}}^{\textsc{d}}}\end{array}\!\right){\in\hskip 1.00006pt}{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{e}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}},~~{\phi_{\textsc{h}}^{\textsc{u}}},{\phi_{\textsc{h}}^{\textsc{d}}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}\mathbb{C}.

We put suffix “
h
” on the Higgs spinor.
Two-component spinor has two eigenvectors concerning to the Euclidean (
SU(2)
) chiral operator as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}\hskip 1.99997pt{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{~}}^{\textsc{u}}}

\displaystyle=-{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{~}}^{\textsc{u}}}\in{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{eu}}_{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}\implies P^{-}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}={\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{~}}^{\textsc{u}}}=\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}{\phi_{\textsc{h}}^{\textsc{u}}}\\
0\end{array}\!\right),

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}\hskip 1.99997pt{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{~}}^{\textsc{d}}}

\displaystyle=+{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{~}}^{\textsc{d}}}\in{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{ed}}_{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}\implies P^{+}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}={\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{~}}^{\textsc{d}}}=\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
{\phi_{\textsc{h}}^{\textsc{d}}}\end{array}\!\right),

then we have

\displaystyle\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}

\displaystyle\in\left\{{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{~}}^{\textsc{u}}},{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{~}}^{\textsc{d}}}\right\}{\subset}{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{e}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}={\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{eu}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\oplus{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{ed}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}},

(2.5.3e)

which is named a
Higgs-spinor
in this report.

We introduce unit Higgs-spinors as

\displaystyle{\hat{\bm{\phi}}_{\textsc{h}}^{\textsc{u}}}

\displaystyle:={{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{\textsc{u}}}}/{{\phi_{\textsc{h}}^{\textsc{u}}}}=\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
0\end{array}\!\right)~~\text{and}~~{\hat{\bm{\phi}}_{\textsc{h}}^{\textsc{d}}}:={{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{\textsc{d}}}}/{{\phi_{\textsc{h}}^{\textsc{d}}}}=\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
1\end{array}\!\right),

as a basis of the Higgs spinor.

We denote a component of the Higgs-spinor
{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}
as
[{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}]^{~}_{A}=\phi^{~}_{A}
with
A\in\{\textsc{u},\textsc{d}\}
.
In contrast to the vector in
{T\hskip-1.99997pt{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}}
, the column vector representation of the spinor is given with a subscript.
We can denote the
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2)
transformation of the Higgs-spinor using the component representation as

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}_{A}

\displaystyle=[\bm{g}_{\textsc{e}}^{{\dagger}}]_{A}^{\hskip 3.27773ptB}\phi^{~}_{B}.

The Einstein convention is utilized for repeated indices running “
u
” and “
d
”.
The
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2)
-invariant bilinear form is introduced for
{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}},\bm{\varphi}^{~}_{\textsc{w}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{e}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}
as

\displaystyle\langle{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}},\bm{\varphi}^{~}_{\textsc{w}}\rangle

\displaystyle:=\phi^{*}_{A}\hskip 1.49994pt{\delta}^{AB}\varphi^{~}_{B}=\left(\phi_{~}^{A}\right)^{*}\varphi^{~}_{A}={\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}^{\!\dagger}\!\cdot\bm{\varphi}^{~}_{\textsc{w}},\quad\text{where}\quad{\delta}^{AB}\>=[\bm{1}_{2}]^{A}_{\hskip 2.45836ptB}.

(2.5.8)

In this study, we mainly consider a following subgroup
\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{r}})\subset\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}(\bm{g}_{\textsc{e}}^{~})
:

\displaystyle\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{r}})=\bm{g}^{a}_{{r}}(\varphi):=e^{-i({\varphi}/{2})\sigma^{a}}=\begin{cases}\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}\cos{\varphi/2}&-i\sin{\varphi/2}\\
-i\sin{\varphi/2}&\cos{\varphi/2}\end{array}\right)&a=1\\
\hskip 10.00002pt\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}\cos{\varphi/2}&-\sin{\varphi/2}\\
\sin{\varphi/2}&\cos{\varphi/2}\end{array}\right)&a=2\hskip 20.00003pt\in SU(2)\\
\hskip 20.00003pt\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{-i\varphi/2}&0\\
0&e^{i\varphi/2}\end{array}\right)&a=3\end{cases},

(2.5.9)

where actions with
a=1,2
are induced by subgroups (
A.1a
), and that with
a=3
is induced by (
A.1j
) given in
Appendix
A
.

Spin(4)
doubly covers
SO(4)
as mentioned in the first homomorphism of (
2.3.2b
).
We denote the group homomorphism as

\displaystyle Spin(4)

\displaystyle=SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.63885ptR}(2){\times}SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.63885ptL}(2)=SO(4){\otimes}\{R,L\}.

(2.5.10)

Subscripts “
R
” and “
L
” stands for the
right
and the
left
, respectively.
An
SU(2)
has a unique irreducible representation; thus, this categorisation is just a convention in physics.
The Higgs spinors conventionally belong to
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.63885ptR}(2)
or
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.63885ptL}(2)
and are transformed to each other under the
SU(2)
group.

2.5.2
Weyl spinor

There are two irreducible representations for an
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
group such that
\pi_{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}^{\textsc{l}}(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}})
and
\pi^{\textsc{l}}_{\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}})
, whose Lie algebras are denoted, respectively, as
{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(1,\mathbb{C})
and
\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(1,\mathbb{C})}
.
The
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
-spinor with spin
s={1\hskip-1.00006pt/\hskip-0.50003pt2}
has a two-dimensional column-vector representation respectively for
{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(1,\mathbb{C})
and
\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(1,\mathbb{C})}
as

\displaystyle\lambda^{~}_{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}(\xi^{~}_{\textsc{u}},\xi^{~}_{\textsc{d}})

\displaystyle:=\bm{\xi}=\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}\xi^{~}_{\textsc{u}}\\
\xi^{~}_{\textsc{d}}\end{array}\!\right){\in\hskip 1.00006pt}{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}},~~\xi^{~}_{\textsc{u}},\xi^{~}_{\textsc{d}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}\mathbb{C},

and

\displaystyle\lambda^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}(\dot{\xi}^{~}_{\dot{\textsc{u}}},\dot{\xi}^{~}_{\dot{\textsc{d}}})

\displaystyle:=\dot{\bm{\xi}}=\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}\dot{\xi}^{~}_{\dot{\textsc{u}}}\\
\dot{\xi}^{~}_{\dot{\textsc{d}}}\end{array}\!\right){\in\hskip 1.00006pt}{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}},~~\dot{\xi}^{~}_{\dot{\textsc{u}}},\dot{\xi}^{~}_{\dot{\textsc{d}}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}\mathbb{C}.

We call
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
-spinors as the
Weyl-spinor
.
As shown in (
2.5.11
), a spinor belonging to
{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}
is denoted as
\dot{\bm{\xi}}
and called a
dotted spinor
, whose component is also represented by the dotted index as
[\dot{\bm{\xi}}]^{~}_{\dot{A}}
, using the
van der Waerden notation
.
In contrast to the dotted one, spinor
\bm{\xi}\in{\mathbb{V}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}^{\textsc{l}}
is called the
undotted spinor
.
In physics, an undotted (dotted) Weyl spinor is also called a right-handed (left-handed) spinor, respectively.

The undotted and dotted Weyl spinors are, respectively, transformed as the fundamental representation under the
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
as

\displaystyle\bm{\xi}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}^{\textsc{l}}(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}})\bm{\xi}=\bm{g}_{\textsc{l}}^{{\dagger}}(\varphi)\bm{\xi},\hskip 21.00009pt[\bm{\xi}^{\prime}]_{\!A}=[\bm{g}_{\textsc{l}}^{~}(\varphi)]_{\!A}^{\hskip 3.27773ptB}[\bm{\xi}]_{\!B},

(2.5.12a)

and

\displaystyle\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pt\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}^{\textsc{l}}(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}})\dot{\bm{\xi}}=\bm{g}_{\textsc{l}}^{*}(\varphi)\dot{\bm{\xi}},~~~~~\left[\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{\prime}\right]_{\!\dot{A}}=\left[\bm{g}_{\textsc{l}}^{*}(\varphi)\right]_{\!\dot{A}}^{\hskip 3.27773pt\dot{B}}\left[\dot{\bm{\xi}}\hskip 1.00006pt\right]_{\!\dot{B}}\!,

(2.5.12b)

where dotted and undotted indices run over
\{\textsc{u},\textsc{d}\}
and
\{\dot{\textsc{u}},\dot{\textsc{d}}\}
, respectively.
We note that
(\bm{g}_{\textsc{l}}^{\dagger})^{*}=\bm{g}_{\textsc{l}}^{t}=\bm{g}_{\textsc{l}}^{*}
since
\bm{g}_{\textsc{l}}^{~}
is the Hermitian matrix.
We raise and lower Weyl-spinor indices using the (
{2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2}
)-tensor
\bm{\epsilon}^{~}_{2}
as

\displaystyle\xi_{~}^{A}=\epsilon^{\hskip 2.45836ptAB}_{2}\xi^{~}_{B},~~\dot{\xi}_{~}^{\dot{A}}=\epsilon^{\hskip 2.45836pt\dot{A}\dot{B}}_{2}\dot{\xi}^{~}_{\dot{B}},

where

\displaystyle\left[\bm{\epsilon}^{~}_{2}\right]^{AB}

\displaystyle=\left[\bm{\epsilon}^{~}_{2}\right]^{\dot{A}\dot{B}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right)=-\left[\bm{\epsilon}^{~}_{2}\right]_{AB}=-\left[\bm{\epsilon}^{~}_{2}\right]_{\dot{A}\dot{B}}.

The
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
-invariant bilinear forms of undotted spinors (
\bm{\xi},\bm{\zeta}{\hskip 1.99997pt\in\hskip 1.99997pt}{\mathbb{V}}_{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}^{\textsc{l}}
) and dotted spinors (
\dot{\bm{\xi}},\dot{\bm{\zeta}}{\hskip 1.99997pt\in\hskip 1.99997pt}{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}})
are, respectively, given as

\displaystyle\langle\bm{\xi},\bm{\zeta}\rangle

\displaystyle:=\xi^{~}_{A}\hskip 1.99997pt\epsilon^{\hskip 2.45836ptAB}_{2}\hskip 1.99997pt\zeta_{B}=\xi^{~}_{\textsc{u}}\zeta_{\textsc{d}}-\xi^{~}_{\textsc{d}}\zeta_{\textsc{u}},~~\text{and}~~\langle\dot{\bm{\xi}},\dot{\bm{\zeta}}\rangle:=\dot{\xi}^{~}_{\dot{A}}\hskip 1.99997pt\epsilon^{\hskip 2.45836pt\dot{A}\dot{B}}_{2}\hskip 1.99997pt\dot{\zeta}_{\dot{B}}=\dot{\xi}^{~}_{\dot{\textsc{u}}}\dot{\zeta}_{\dot{\textsc{d}}}-\dot{\xi}^{~}_{\dot{\textsc{d}}}\dot{\zeta}_{\dot{\textsc{u}}}.

(2.5.13)

In contrast with the Higgs-spinor case, we do not take complex conjugate for a bra-part of the bilinear form since
\bm{g}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}
and
\bm{g}^{*}_{\textsc{l}}
belong to the different representations.
This bilinear form is
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
-invariant such as

\displaystyle\langle\bm{\xi}^{\prime},\bm{\zeta}^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}\bm{\xi},\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}\bm{\zeta}\rangle=

\displaystyle[\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}\bm{\xi}]_{A}\hskip 1.99997pt\epsilon^{\hskip 2.45836ptAB}_{2}\hskip 1.99997pt[\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}\bm{\zeta}]_{B}=\xi^{~}_{A}[\bm{g}_{\textsc{l}}^{t}]^{A}_{\hskip 3.27773ptC}\hskip 1.99997pt\epsilon^{\hskip 2.45836ptCD}_{2}\hskip 1.99997pt[\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}]_{D}^{\hskip 3.27773ptB}\hskip 1.00006pt\zeta_{B}=\xi^{A}\hskip 1.99997pt\zeta_{A}=\langle\bm{\xi},\bm{\zeta}\rangle,

where we use

\displaystyle[\bm{g}_{\textsc{l}}^{t}]^{A}_{\hskip 3.27773ptC}\hskip 1.99997pt\epsilon^{\hskip 2.45836ptCD}_{2}\hskip 1.99997pt[\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}]_{D}^{\hskip 3.27773ptB}=\epsilon^{\hskip 2.45836ptAB}_{2}.

The same is true for the dotted spinors.
We denote a skew-transpose for Weyl spinors by
\bullet^{T}
using
\bm{\epsilon}^{~}_{2}
as

\displaystyle\left[\bm{\xi}^{T}\right]^{B}:=\xi_{A}^{~}\hskip 1.00006pt\epsilon^{\hskip 2.45836ptAB}_{2}=\xi^{B}_{~},~~~\left[\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{T}\right]^{\dot{B}}:=\dot{\xi}^{~}_{\dot{A}}\hskip 1.00006pt\epsilon^{\hskip 2.45836pt\dot{A}\dot{B}}_{2}=\dot{\xi}^{\dot{B}}_{~}\implies\langle\bm{\xi},\bm{\zeta}\rangle=\bm{\xi}^{T}{\!\cdot}\bm{\zeta},~~~\langle\dot{\bm{\xi}},\dot{\bm{\zeta}}\rangle=\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{T}{\!\cdot}\dot{\bm{\zeta}}.

(2.5.14)

Three group actions (
2.5.9
) are also the isometric transformation for Lorentzian vectors since they belong to the subgroup
SO(3){\subset}SO(1,3)
.
In addition to that, we consider the group actions of
SO(1,3){\setminus}SO(3)
such that

\displaystyle\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptb})=\bm{g}^{a}_{b}(\chi):=e^{({\chi}/{2})\sigma^{a}}=\begin{cases}\hskip 10.00002pt\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}\cosh{\chi/2}&\sinh{\chi/2}\\
\sinh{\chi/2}&\cosh{\chi/2}\end{array}\right)&a=1\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}\cosh{\chi/2}&-i\sinh{\chi/2}\\
i\sinh{\chi/2}&\cosh{\chi/2}\end{array}\right)&a=2\hskip 20.00003pt\in{\cal{H}}(2)\\
\hskip 20.00003pt\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{\chi/2}&0\\
0&e^{-\chi/2}\end{array}\right)&a=3\end{cases}.

(2.5.15)

Actions with
a=1,2
belong to subgroups (
A.1a
), and those with
a=3
belong to (
A.1
) given in
Appendix
A
.

A space of Weyl spinors consists of two subspaces owing to the chirality such as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}={\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{ul}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\oplus{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{ld}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}.

(2.5.16)

For the undotted spinors, we have eigenvectors of the chiral operator as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}\hskip 1.99997pt\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}

\displaystyle=-\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}\in{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{lr}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\implies P^{-}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\bm{\xi}=\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}=\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}\xi^{~}_{\textsc{u}}\\
0\end{array}\!\right)\!,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}\hskip 1.99997pt\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}

\displaystyle=+\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}\in{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{ld}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\implies P^{+}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\bm{\xi}=\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}=\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
\xi^{~}_{\textsc{d}}\end{array}\!\right)\!,

then we have

\displaystyle\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle\in\left\{\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{u}},\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}\right\}{\subset}{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}={\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{lu}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\oplus{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{ld}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}.

(2.5.17e)

We call the Weyl spinor
\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}
(
\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}
) spin-up (spin-down) state of fermion in physics.
We also obtain the dotted chiral Weyl-spinors; although the result is trivial, we show them for completeness.

\displaystyle\overline{\Gamma}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}\hskip 1.99997pt\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}

\displaystyle=+\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}\in{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{lu}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}\implies\bar{P}^{+}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{\bm{\xi}}=\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}=\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}\dot{\xi}^{~}_{\dot{\textsc{u}}}\\
0\end{array}\!\right),

\displaystyle\overline{\Gamma}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}\hskip 1.99997pt\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}

\displaystyle=-\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}\in{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{ld}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}\implies\bar{P}^{-}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\dot{\bm{\xi}}=\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}=\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
\dot{\xi}^{~}_{\dot{\textsc{d}}}\end{array}\!\right),

then we have

\displaystyle\dot{\bm{\xi}}

\displaystyle\in\left\{\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{~}_{\textsc{u}},\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}\right\}{\subset}{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}={\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{lu}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}\oplus{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{ld}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}.

(2.5.18e)

We note that the chirality of dotted and undotted spinors with the same spin state has an opposite to each other;
\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}
and
\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}
have a positive chirality and
\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}
and
\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}
have a negative chirality.

The Lie algebra homomorphism (
2.3.1d
) allows us to equate each representation of (
2.3.13
) to the angular momentum of a physical field, which justifies terms “
chiral-right
” and “
chiral-right
” as a helicity state of a particle.
Group
Spin(1,3)
doubly covers
SO(1,3)
as shown in (
2.3.2b
).
We denote the Lie-algebra homomorphism as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{~}_{Spin(1,3)}

\displaystyle={\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C})\oplus\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C})}={\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{o}(1,3){\otimes}\{R,L\},

(2.5.19)

similarly to the Higgs spinor.
However, chiral-right and chiral-left Weyl spinors are not transformed to each other under the
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
group in contrast with the Higgs spinor.

2.5.3
Dirac spinor

We introduce the four-component spinor, namely the Dirac spinor, owing to the Clifford algebra representation of dimension-4 introduced in
section
2.4
, as

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle:=\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}\dot{\bm{\xi}}\\
\bm{\xi}\end{array}\!\right)\in{\mathbb{V}}^{{\textsc{l}}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}\otimes\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}:={\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\otimes{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}},~~\text{with}~~~\bm{\xi}\in{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}},~~\dot{\bm{\xi}}\in{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}.

In this representation, chiral and projection operators are provided as the same as the Euclidean case as

\displaystyle\psi_{~}^{L}

\displaystyle:=P^{-}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}\hskip 3.00003pt\psi=\left(\!\begin{array}[]{l}\dot{\bm{\xi}}\\
\bm{0}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}\end{array}\!\right),~~\Gamma^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}\hskip 1.99997pt\psi_{~}^{L}=-\psi_{~}^{L},

\displaystyle\psi_{~}^{R}

\displaystyle:=P^{+}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\psi=\hskip 1.00006pt\left(\!\begin{array}[]{l}\dot{\bm{0}}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}\\
\bm{\xi}\end{array}\!\right),~~\Gamma^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}\hskip 1.99997pt\psi_{~}^{R}=+\psi_{~}^{R}.

Superscripts “
R
” and “
L
” on
\psi
stand for the “
right-handed
” and “
left-handed
” according to those of the Weyl spinors.

2.6
Vectors

An ordered list of four real numbers provides the standard representation of a four-vector.
In four-dimensional Euclidean (Lorentzian) metric space, we can represent it by
({2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2})
complex matrices using the irreducible representation of
SU(2)
(
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
).
This section introduces the representation of dimension-2 for Euclidean and Lorentzian vectors.

2.6.1
Euclidean vectors

Suppose
\hat{{\bm{v}}}_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}=(\hat{v}^{0},\hat{v}^{1},\hat{v}^{2},\hat{v}^{3})^{t}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}V({T\hskip-1.99997pt{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}})
is a real unit-vector
\|\hat{{\bm{v}}}_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\|^{~}_{\textsc{e}}=1
in the four-dimensional Euclid manifold
({\cal M},{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{e}}}})
.
A space of unit vectors is equivalent to
S^{3}
as shown in (
2.3.3
).
The transformation,

\displaystyle\Sigma^{~}_{\textsc{e}}:S^{3}{\rightarrow}{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2):\hat{{\bm{v}}}_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\mapsto\hat{{\bm{v}}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}:=\bm{\sigma}_{\textsc{e}}^{t}\cdot\hat{{\bm{v}}}_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}

\displaystyle=\left(\!\begin{array}[]{rr}\hat{v}^{0}-i\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{v}^{3}&-i\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{v}^{1}-\hskip 4.49997pt\hat{v}^{2}\\
-i\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{v}^{1}+\hskip 4.49997pt\hat{v}^{2}&\hat{v}^{0}+i\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{v}^{3}\\
\end{array}\!\right),

maps a unit vector in
V({T\hskip-1.99997pt{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}})
to an
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2)
matrix.
There is a one-to-one correspondence between Euclid vectors and
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2)
-spinors as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{e}}_{\hskip-1.63885pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2},{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\ni\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}{\phi_{\textsc{u}}^{~}}\\
{\phi_{\textsc{d}}^{~}}\end{array}\!\right)\otimes\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}{\phi_{\textsc{u}}^{~}}\\
{\phi_{\textsc{d}}^{~}}\end{array}\!\right)^{\!*}\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}{\phi_{\textsc{u}}^{~}}&-{\phi_{\textsc{d}}^{~}}^{*}\\
{\phi_{\textsc{d}}^{~}}&{\phi_{\textsc{u}}^{~}}^{*}\end{array}\right)\!.

Thus, Euclidean vectors belong to the spinor space (
2.3.4
).
The
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2)
invariant bilinear form and the norm are given as

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\bm{u}}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}|\hat{\bm{v}}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\rangle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left({\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}[\hat{\bm{u}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}]\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}[\hat{\bm{v}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}]-{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}[\hat{\bm{u}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\hskip 1.99997pt\hat{\bm{v}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}]\right)=\hat{u}^{0}_{~}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{v}^{0}_{~}+\hat{u}^{1}_{~}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{v}^{1}_{~}+\hat{u}^{2}_{~}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{v}^{2}_{~}+\hat{u}^{3}_{~}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{v}^{3}_{~},

(2.6.3a)

\displaystyle\|\hat{{\bm{u}}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\|^{2}:=

\displaystyle\langle\hat{{\bm{u}}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}|\hat{{\bm{u}}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\rangle=\text{det}[{\hat{{\bm{u}}}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}]=1,

(2.6.3b)

in the representation of dimension-2.
Any non-zero vector
{\bm{u}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}
is represented as

\displaystyle{\bm{u}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}

\displaystyle=\|{\bm{u}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\|\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{\bm{v}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}},~~\text{with}~~\|{\bm{u}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\|^{2}>0.

Thus, the transformation (
2.6.1
) maps a pint in
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO\hskip-1.00006pt}(4)
to the
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2)
matrix as

\displaystyle\Sigma^{~}_{\textsc{e}}:V({\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO\hskip-1.00006pt}(4)){\rightarrow}SU(2)\otimes\mathbb{R}:\bm{{\bm{v}}}^{~}_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\mapsto\bm{{\bm{v}}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}.

Group action (
2.5.9
) acts on a vector as the adjoint representation such that:

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{v}}^{\prime}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}

\displaystyle=\tau\left(\bm{g}^{a}_{{r}}(\varphi)\right)\left(\hat{\bm{v}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\right)=\bm{\sigma}_{\textsc{e}}^{t}\cdot\begin{cases}\hskip 3.99994pt\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}\hat{v}^{0}\\
\hat{v}^{1}\\
\hat{v}^{2}\cos{\varphi}-\hat{v}^{3}\sin{\varphi}\\
\hat{v}^{2}\sin{\varphi}+\hat{v}^{3}\cos{\varphi}\\
\end{array}\!\right)&a=1\\
\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}\hat{v}^{0}\\
\hskip 6.99997pt\hat{v}^{1}\cos{\varphi}+\hat{v}^{3}\sin{\varphi}\\
\hat{v}^{2}\\
-\hat{v}^{1}\sin{\varphi}+\hat{v}^{3}\cos{\varphi}\\
\end{array}\!\right)&a=2\\
\hskip 3.99994pt\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}\hat{v}^{0}\\
\hat{v}^{1}\cos{\varphi}-\hat{v}^{2}\sin{\varphi}\\
\hat{v}^{1}\sin{\varphi}+\hat{v}^{2}\cos{\varphi}\\
\hat{v}^{3}\end{array}\!\right)&a=3\end{cases}\!,

(2.6.4)

where

\displaystyle\tau(\bm{g}^{~}_{r})({\bm{v}}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}})

\displaystyle:=\bm{g}^{~}_{r}\hskip 1.00006pt{\bm{v}}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\hskip 1.00006pt\bm{g}_{r}^{\dagger}.

(2.6.5)

Group actions (
2.5.9
) are isometric transformations keeping the bilinear form (
2.6.3a
) invariant under the
SU(2)
transformation.
In reality, (
2.6.4
) induces an anti-clockwise
\varphi
-rotation of
\hat{{\bm{v}}}
around the
\hat{v}^{a}
-axis.
Actions
\bm{g}^{a}_{{r}}(\varphi)
and
-\bm{g}^{a}_{{r}}(\varphi)
map vector
\hat{{\bm{v}}}
to the same vector
\hat{\bm{v}}^{\prime}
; thus, this is a
two-to-one
transformation.

The subgroups
G
, which have continuous homomorphism fulfiling

\displaystyle g:\mathbb{R}{\rightarrow}G:\varphi{\mapsto}g(\varphi),~~g(\vartheta+\varphi)=g(\vartheta)\hskip 1.00006ptg(\varphi)~~\text{and}~~g(0)=\bm{1}_{\text{id}},

is called a
one-parameter subgroup
.
Group elements in (
2.5.9
) are the one-parameter subgroup of
SU(2)=S^{3}=Spin(3)=SO(3)\otimes\{\pm 1\}
.
The general element of the
SU(2)
group is given as (
A.4
) in
Appendix
A
.
This one-parameter subgroup belongs to the subgroup
SO_{~}^{\perp}\hskip-1.00006pt(3){\subset}SO(4)
, where
SO_{~}^{\perp}\hskip-1.00006pt(3)
is the rotation group of the three-dimensional Euclidean space perpendicular to the
\hat{v}^{0}
-axis.

2.6.2
Lorentzian vectors

For Lorentzian vector
\hat{{\bm{u}}}_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}=(\hat{u}^{0},\hat{u}^{1},\hat{u}^{2},\hat{u}^{3})^{t}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}V({{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})
with
\|\hat{{\bm{u}}}_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}\|^{~}_{\textsc{l}}=\pm 1
, we have the representation of dimension-2 for
\hat{{\bm{u}}}_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}
as

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\pm}_{\textsc{l}}:\text{Hyp}^{3}{\rightarrow}{\cal{H}}(2)\cap({S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}\otimes\{-1,1\}):\hat{{\bm{u}}}_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}\mapsto\hat{{\bm{u}}}^{\pm}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}:=\left(\bm{\sigma}^{\pm}_{\textsc{l}}\right)^{t}\cdot\hat{{\bm{u}}}_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}

\displaystyle=\left(\!\begin{array}[]{rr}\hat{u}^{0}\pm\hskip 4.49997pt\hat{u}^{3}&\pm\hat{u}^{1}\mp i\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{u}^{2}\\
\pm\hat{u}^{1}\pm i\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{u}^{2}&\hat{u}^{0}\mp\hskip 4.49997pt\hat{u}^{3}\\
\end{array}\!\right),

where
\text{Hyp}^{3}
is a three-dimensional hyperbolic plane given by
\|\hat{{\bm{u}}}_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}\|^{~}_{\textsc{l}}=\pm 1
.

{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}\otimes\{-1,1\}
shows a space of complex
({2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2})
-matrices with their determinant being
\pm 1
.
Transformation (
2.6.2
) maps a Lorentzian vector to an
{\cal{H}}(2)\cap({S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}\otimes\{-1,1\})
matrix.
The Lorentzian vectors belong to the bi-spinor space (
2.3.14
) as
V({{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})={\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-1.63885pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2},\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}
.
We can construct any vector in the Lorentzian metric space using a bi-spinor as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-1.63885pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2},\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}\ni\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}\xi_{\textsc{u}}\\
\xi_{\textsc{d}}\end{array}\!\right)\otimes\left(\!\begin{array}[]{c}\xi_{\textsc{u}}\\
\xi_{\textsc{d}}\end{array}\!\right)^{\!*}\mapsto\left(\!\begin{array}[]{ccr}(\xi_{\textsc{u}}+\xi^{*}_{\textsc{u}})/2&\xi^{*}_{\textsc{d}}\\
\xi_{\textsc{d}}&i(\xi^{*}_{\textsc{u}}-\xi_{\textsc{u}})/2\end{array}\!\right).

The
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
invariant bilinear form is provided for
\hat{{\bm{u}}}_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}},\hat{{\bm{v}}}_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}\in{\cal{H}}(2)\cap({S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}\otimes\{-1,1\})
as

\displaystyle\langle\hat{{\bm{u}}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}},\hat{{\bm{v}}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}\rangle

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\left({\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}[\hat{{\bm{u}}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}]\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}[\hat{{\bm{v}}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}]-{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}[\hat{{\bm{u}}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}\hskip 1.99997pt\hat{{\bm{v}}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}]\right)=\hat{v}^{0}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{v}^{0}-\hat{u}^{1}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{v}^{1}-\hat{u}^{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{v}^{2}-\hat{u}^{3}\hskip 1.00006pt\hat{v}^{3},

(2.6.8a)

\displaystyle\|\hat{{\bm{u}}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle:=\langle\hat{{\bm{u}}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}|\hat{{\bm{u}}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}\rangle=(\hat{u}^{0})^{2}-(\hat{u}^{1})^{2}-(\hat{u}^{2})^{2}-(\hat{u}^{3})^{2}=\text{det}[\hat{{\bm{u}}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}];

(2.6.8b)

thus, the Lorentzian metric is correctly embedded in the map
\Sigma^{\pm}_{\textsc{l}}
.

Transformation (
2.6.2
) maps a vector in
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO\hskip-1.00006pt}(1,3)
to
({2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2})
-Hermitian matrices:

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\pm}_{\textsc{l}}:V({\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO\hskip-1.00006pt}(1,3)){\rightarrow}{\cal{H}}(2)\otimes\mathbb{R}:\bm{{\bm{u}}}^{~}_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}\mapsto\bm{{\bm{u}}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}.

Action (
2.5.15
) acts on the vector
{\bm{u}}^{+}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}
, and
{\pi}_{\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}^{~}(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptb})=\bm{g}_{\hskip-0.81949ptb}^{*}=(\bm{g}_{\hskip-0.81949ptb}^{\dagger})^{-1}
acts on
{\bm{u}}^{-}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}
as

\displaystyle{\bm{u}}^{\pm^{\prime}}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\hskip-3.99994pt\begin{array}[]{l}\tau(\hskip 1.00006pt\bm{g}^{a}_{b}(\chi))\left({\bm{u}}^{+}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}\right)\\
\tau({\bm{g}^{a}_{b}}^{*}(\chi))\left({\bm{u}}^{-}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}\right)\end{array}\right.={\bm{\sigma}^{\pm}_{\textsc{l}}}^{t}\cdot\begin{cases}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{u}^{0}\cosh{\chi}+{u}^{1}\sinh{\chi}\\
{u}^{0}\sinh{\chi}+{u}^{1}\cosh{\chi}\\
{u}^{2}\\
{u}^{3}\end{array}\right)&a=1,\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{u}^{0}\cosh{\chi}+{u}^{2}\sinh{\chi}\\
{u}^{1}\\
{u}^{0}\sinh{\chi}+{u}^{2}\cosh{\chi}\\
{u}^{3}\end{array}\right)&a=2,\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{u}^{0}\cosh{\chi}+{u}^{3}\sinh{\chi}\\
{u}^{1}\\
{u}^{2}\\
{u}^{0}\sinh{\chi}+{u}^{3}\cosh{\chi}\end{array}\right)&a=3,\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle\tau(\bm{g}_{b}^{~})({\bm{v}}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}})

\displaystyle:=\bm{g}_{b}^{~}\hskip 1.00006pt{\bm{v}}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}\hskip 1.00006pt\bm{g}_{b}^{\dagger},

(2.6.11)

which induce a
\chi
-boost along the
{u}^{a}
-axis.
This group action is also a
two-to-one
transformation as the Euclidean case.
These representation belongs to the subgroup of
SO(1,3)
concerning the boost operation.
We denote the group action of
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
as

\displaystyle\bm{g}_{\hskip-0.81949ptL}^{+}(\varphi,\chi):=\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{r}}(\varphi)\hskip-1.00006pt\otimes\hskip 1.00006pt\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptb}(\chi)~~

\displaystyle\text{and}~~\bm{g}_{\hskip-0.81949ptL}^{-}(\varphi,\chi):=\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{r}}(\varphi)\hskip-1.00006pt\otimes\hskip 1.00006pt\bm{g}_{\hskip-0.81949ptb}^{*}(\chi).

(2.6.12)

We nothe that they are commutable as

\displaystyle\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{r}}(\varphi){\circ}\hskip 1.00006pt\bm{g}_{\hskip-0.81949ptb}^{(*)}(\chi)=\bm{g}_{\hskip-0.81949ptb}^{(*)}(\chi){\circ}\hskip 1.00006pt\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{r}}(\varphi).

Both
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
and
SO^{\uparrow}\hskip-1.00006pt(1,3)
are simply connected and
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
entirely covers
SO^{\uparrow}\hskip-1.00006pt(1,3)
.
The representation of dimension-2 for
Spin(1,3)\hskip-1.99997pt=\hskip-1.99997pt{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
has two fundamental representations as (
2.3.12
) and is labeled by two half-integers
\{s^{~}_{1},s^{~}_{2}\}
as (
2.3.13
).

2.7
Gravitational fields

This section discusses irreducible representations of the gravitational fields, i.e., space-time curvature, spin connection, and vierbein fields, in the Lorentzian inertial manifold.
This section omits the suffix “
l
” of tensors for simplicity.

a) Riemann curvature:

The Riemann curvature tensor,
R^{{\bullet\bullet}}_{\hskip 4.09723pt{\bullet\bullet}}
, is a rank-two in the tangent space and a rank-two in the cotangent space of the inertial manifold as

\displaystyle\Omega^{2}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}){\otimes}V^{2}({T\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}})\ni\mathfrak{R}=\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{R}^{ab}(\partial_{a}\times\partial_{b})=\frac{1}{4}R^{ab}_{\hskip 7.37497ptcd}\hskip 1.99997pt({\mathfrak{e}}^{c}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{d})(\partial_{a}\times\partial_{b}).

(2.7.1)

The Riemann curvature is a reducible representation: an irreducible decomposition of the Riemann curvature has the following visualization
[

]
using Young tableaux as

(

.

b) vierbein and perturbed metric filed:

The vierbein form has a coefficient representation as

\displaystyle\Omega^{1}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}}}){\otimes}V^{1}({{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})\ni{\mathfrak{e}}={\mathfrak{e}}^{a}\partial_{a}={\mathcal{E}}^{\hskip 3.27773pta}_{\mu}(x\in{\mathscr{M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{g}}})\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{\mu}\hskip 1.99997pt\partial_{a},

(2.7.2)

where
{\mathfrak{e}}
is the cotangent vector (differential form) in the global manifold and, at the same time, the tangent vector in the local inertial bundle.
The coefficient function
{\mathcal{E}}^{a}_{\mu}
is defined in the global manifold; thus, we treat it as the global vector.
Irreducible representations of the corresponding metric tensor are given as

Amongthreeirreduciblerepresentations

,

c) spin connection:

The spin connection has a coefficient representation as

\displaystyle\Omega^{1}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}){\otimes}V^{2}({T\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}})\ni{\mathfrak{w}}=\frac{1}{2}{\mathfrak{w}}^{ab}(\partial_{a}\times\partial_{b})=\frac{1}{2}\omega^{\hskip 3.27773ptab}_{c}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}^{c}(\partial_{a}\times\partial_{b}),

(2.7.13)

which has two antisymmetric upper-indices in
{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}
and one vector index in
{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}
.
The irreducible decomposition of the spin connection representation is given as

Basis

2.8
Dirac operator

The standard theory of particle physics utilises the differential operator contracting with the Clifford algebra, namely the Dirac operator, to describe the electron’s equation of motion.
Dirac invented it as the “
square-root of the Laplacian
”.
The Dirac operator flips the chirality of the spinor; it induces the Atiyah–Singer index theorem
[
25
]
.
The Dirac operator is generally introduced in the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading vector space owing to the Clifford algebra.
This study defines the Dirac operator following Ref.
[
26
]
and provides representations of dimension-2 and dimension-4.
We provide an application of the index theorem in
Appendix
B
.

Definition 2.2

(Dirac operator)
.

Suppose
{\mathbb{V}}^{\pm}
is the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading vector space and
\xi^{\pm}{\in}{\mathbb{V}}^{\pm}
.
The Dirac operator
\not\!\partial
is a first-order differential operator flipping a chirality such that:

\displaystyle\not\!\partial:{{\mathbb{V}}^{\pm}}\rightarrow{{\mathbb{V}}^{\mp}}:\xi^{\pm}\mapsto\not\!\partial\xi^{\pm}\in{{\mathbb{V}}^{\mp}}.

A Dirac operator squared,
\not\!\partial^{2}
, is called a generalized Laplacian.

We construct the Dirac operator using the contraction concerning the Clifford algebra as

\displaystyle{\not\!d}

\displaystyle:=\iota^{~}_{\gamma}d:\Gamma\left({\cal M},{\Omega}^{0}\right)\xrightarrow{d}\Gamma\left({\cal M},{\Omega}^{1}\right)\xrightarrow{\iota^{~}_{\gamma}}\Gamma\left({\cal M},{\Omega}^{0}\right),

(2.8.1)

where
\iota^{~}_{\gamma}
is the contraction with the corresponding Clifford algebra.
Owing to the Clifford algebra, the Dirac operator flips an undotted spinor to the dotted one in the Lorentzian metric space, and vice versa, like

\displaystyle\bm{\xi}\in{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\rightarrow{\not\!d}\bm{\xi}\in{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}~~\text{and}~~\dot{\bm{\xi}}\in{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}\rightarrow{\not\!d}\dot{\bm{\xi}}\in{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}},

(2.8.2)

which follows from (
2.4.4
) and (
2.4.41
).
Thus, it flips the chirality and maintains the definition.

2.8.1
representation of dimension-2

a) Euclidean metric:

The representation of dimension-2 of the Dirac operator in the Euclidean metric space is provided as

\displaystyle\iota^{~}_{\gamma}{\not\!d}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}

\displaystyle=\not\!\partial_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}:=\gamma_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}^{\circ}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{\circ}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&h^{\circ}_{\textsc{e}}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{\circ}\\
\bar{h}^{\circ}_{\textsc{e}}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{\circ}&0\end{array}\right),

where

\displaystyle h^{a}_{\textsc{e}}

\displaystyle:=\left(1,i,j,k\right)~~\text{and}~~\bar{h}^{a}_{\textsc{e}}:=\left(1,-i,-j,-k\right),

(2.8.3c)

owing to the Clifford algebra (
2.4.8
).
Direct calculations yield that

\displaystyle P^{\pm}_{s}{\not\!\partial}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}P^{\pm}_{s}=0~~\text{and}~~P^{\mp}_{s}{\not\!\partial}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}P^{\pm}_{s}={\not\!\partial}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}P^{\pm}_{s}.

Thus, the differential operator (
2.8.3
) is the Dirac operator.
We can also confirm
\left({\not\!\partial}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\right)^{2}=\Delta_{\textsc{e}}
by direct calculations.

b) Lorentzian metric:

Depending on the operand is undotted or dotted spinors, there are two types of the (
{2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2}
)-Dirac operator in the Lorentzian metric space such as:

\displaystyle{\not\!d}_{\textsc{sl}}\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=\iota^{~}_{\gamma}d_{\textsc{sl}}\bm{\xi}:=\gamma_{\textsc{sl}}^{\circ}(\partial_{\circ}\bm{\xi})=:{\not\!\partial}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}},~~~~\overline{{\not\!d}}_{\textsc{sl}}\dot{\bm{\xi}}=\iota^{~}_{\overline{\gamma}}\overline{d}_{\textsc{sl}}\dot{\bm{\xi}}:=\overline{\gamma}_{\textsc{sl}}^{\circ}(\partial_{\circ}\dot{\bm{\xi}})=:\overline{\not\!\partial}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}\bm{\xi},

(2.8.4)

where
\bm{\gamma}^{~}_{\textsc{sl}}
and
\overline{\bm{\gamma}}^{~}_{\textsc{sl}}
are given in (
2.4.38
).
The Dirac operator (
2.8.4
) yields
\left({\not\!\partial}_{\textsc{sl}}\right)^{2}=\left(\overline{\not\!\partial}_{\textsc{sl}}\right)^{2}=\Delta_{\textsc{l}}\bm{1}_{2}
.
We can confirm the chirality flip owing to the Dirac operator in this representation by direct calculations from (
2.8.4
).

c) (
{2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2}
) differential operator:

We introduce the (
{2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2}
)-differential operators as

\displaystyle{\bm{\partial}}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}

\displaystyle:={{{\bm{\sigma}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}}^{t}\cdot\bm{\partial}\hskip 3.99994pt=\bm{1}^{~}_{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{0}-i\bm{\sigma}^{1}\partial_{1}-i\bm{\sigma}^{2}\partial_{2}-i\sigma^{3}\bm{\partial}_{3}=\left(\hskip-5.0pt\begin{array}[]{rr}\partial_{0}-i\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{3}&-i\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{1}-\hskip 4.49997pt\partial_{2}\\
-i\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{1}+\hskip 4.49997pt\partial_{2}&\partial_{0}+i\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{3}\\
\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle{\bm{\partial}}_{\textsc{sl}}

\displaystyle:={\bm{\sigma}^{+}_{\textsc{l}}}^{t}\cdot\bm{\partial}=\bm{1}^{~}_{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{0}+\hskip 3.00003pt\bm{\sigma}^{1}\partial_{1}+\hskip 3.00003pt\bm{\sigma}^{2}\partial_{2}+\hskip 3.00003pt\bm{\sigma}^{3}\partial_{3}=\hskip 1.99997pt\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}\partial_{0}+\hskip 4.49997pt\partial_{3}&\partial_{1}-i\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{2}\\
\partial_{1}+i\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{2}&\partial_{0}-\hskip 4.49997pt\partial_{3}\\
\end{array}\right),

those are
not
the Dirac operator.
Operators (
2.8.5
) and (
2.8.5
) act on the (
{2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2}
)-vector as

\displaystyle\langle\bm{\partial}_{\textsc{s}\bullet},{\bm{v}}_{\textsc{s}\bullet}\rangle

\displaystyle=\partial_{0}v^{0}\pm\partial_{1}v^{1}\pm\partial_{2}v^{2}\pm\partial_{3}v^{3},

where an upper (lower) sign for the Euclidean (Lorentzian) metric, respectively.
The inner product is defined by (
2.6.3a
) for the Euclidean metric and (
2.6.8a
) for the Lorentzian metric, respectively.

2.8.2
representation of dimension-4

a) Lorentzian metric:

The (
{4\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt4}
) Dirac operator acts on the Dirac spinor from the left as

\displaystyle{\not\!d}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}\psi

\displaystyle=\iota^{~}_{\gamma}d\psi=\iota^{~}_{\gamma}\left((\partial_{j}\psi){\mathfrak{e}}^{j}\right)=\gamma^{\circ}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{\circ}\psi=:\not\!\partial_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}\psi,

(2.8.6)

In the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading vector space introduced in (
2.4.6b
) and (
2.4.7
), we obtain

\displaystyle\psi^{\pm}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}{\mathbb{V}}^{{\textsc{l}}^{\pm}}_{\hskip-1.63885pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}\otimes\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}\hskip 1.99997pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 1.99997pt\not\!\partial_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}\psi^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\not\!\partial_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}\left(\frac{\bm{1}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\pm\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}}{2}\psi\right)=\frac{\bm{1}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\mp\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}}{2}\left(\not\!\partial_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}\psi\right)\hskip 3.00003pt{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip 3.00003pt{\mathbb{V}}^{{\textsc{l}}^{\mp}}_{\hskip-1.63885pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}\otimes\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}},

(2.8.7)

where
\{\psi^{+}_{~},\psi^{-}_{~}\}=\{\psi^{R},\psi^{L}\}
given by (
2.5.22
) and (
2.5.22
).
Thus, differential operator
\not\!\partial_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}
in (
2.8.6
) fulfil the definition of the Dirac operator.

A square of the Dirac operator provides

\displaystyle\not\!\partial^{2}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\partial_{0}\right)^{2}-\left(\partial_{1}\right)^{2}-\left(\partial_{2}\right)^{2}-\left(\partial_{3}\right)^{2}\right)\bm{1}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}=:\Delta^{~}_{{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}},

thus,
\Delta^{~}_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}
is the Laplacian in the four-dimensional Lorentzian metric space.

b) Euclidean metric:

The representation of dimension-4 of the Euclidean Dirac operator can be obtained by replacing the Lorentzian Clifford algebra and the metric tensor to the Euclidean ones denoted by
\bullet_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}
as

\displaystyle{\not\!d}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}

\displaystyle=\iota^{~}_{\gamma}d=\iota^{~}_{\gamma}\left((\partial_{j}){\mathfrak{e}}^{j}\right)=\gamma^{\circ}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{\circ}=:\not\!\partial_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}},

(2.8.8)

yielding

\displaystyle\not\!\partial^{2}_{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\partial_{0}\right)^{2}+\left(\partial_{1}\right)^{2}+\left(\partial_{2}\right)^{2}+\left(\partial_{3}\right)^{2}\right)\bm{1}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}=:\Delta^{~}_{{{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}}.

c) Lorentzian Dirac operator on Higgs spinor:

We prepare the Lorentzian Dirac operator for the Higgs spinor and its bilinear form here.
In the standard theory of particle physics, the Higgs field is the
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2)
spinor defined in the Lorentzian manifold.
Thus, we treat the Lorentzian Dirac operator applying on the
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2)
spinor in the Yang–Mills theory.
The Lorentzian Dirac operator (
2.8.4
) acts on the Higgs spinor as

\displaystyle{\not\!d}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}

\displaystyle=\not\!\partial^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}},~~\text{yielding}~~\not\!\partial^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}\left(P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}\right)=P^{\mp}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}\left(\not\!\partial^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}\right),

(2.8.9)

owing to the formal relation
P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}}=P^{\pm}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}
.
Therefore, the Lorentzian Dirac operator fulfils
Definition
2.2
for the Higgs spinor.

We introduce a co-Dirac operator as

\displaystyle i{\not\!\delta}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}

\displaystyle:=i{\not\!d}^{\dagger}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}},\quad\text{yielding a weak relation}\quad\langle i{\not\!\delta}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}},{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}\rangle=\langle{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}},i{\not\!d}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}\rangle

(2.8.10)

such that

\displaystyle\int_{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt\textsc{l}}}}\langle i{\not\!\delta}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}},{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}\rangle

\displaystyle=-i\int_{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt\textsc{l}}}}\left({\gamma^{\circ}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}^{\dagger}\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{\circ}{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}\right)^{\dagger}{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}=-i\int_{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt\textsc{l}}}}\left(\partial_{\circ}{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}^{\dagger}\right){\gamma^{\circ}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}{{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}},

\displaystyle=-i\int_{{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt\textsc{l}}}}}\partial_{\circ}\left({\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}^{\dagger}{\gamma^{\circ}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}{{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}}\right)+i\int_{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt\textsc{l}}}}{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}^{\dagger}{\gamma^{\circ}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}}(\partial_{\circ}{{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}}),

\displaystyle=\int_{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt\textsc{l}}}}\langle{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}},i{\not\!d}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}\rangle,

where we utilise the integration by parts, and assume the vanishing Higgs spinor at the boundary.
The Dirac operator acts on the Euclidean bilinear form as

\displaystyle{\not\!d}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}\langle{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}},{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}\rangle

\displaystyle:=\langle{\not\!\delta}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}},{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}\rangle+\langle{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}},{\not\!d}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}\rangle=2\langle{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}},{\not\!d}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}\rangle.

3
Yang–Mills–Utiyama (YMU ​) theory

We refer to the Yang–Mills theory treating the local space-time
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO\hskip-1.00006pt}(1,3)
on equal footing with the internal gauge symmetry as the
Yang–Mills-Utiyama
(YMU ​) theory in this report.
This section constructs the YMU theory in the inertial
Lorentzian
manifold based on mathematical tools prepared up to the previous section.
The subscript “
l
” is omitted for simplicity through this section.
The YMU theory treated in this study is the gauge theory with internal
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2)
symmetry and local Lorentz symmetry.
We formulate the theory in the inertial manifold; after fixing the frame and treating the theory to the local inertial frame, the global
GL(4)
symmetry is broken while keeping the local
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO\hskip-1.00006pt}(1,3)
symmetry.
The local information of the global manifold is encapsulated in the spin-connection form
{\mathfrak{w}}
in this formalism.

3.1
Principal bundles

We introduce principal bundles with the structure groups
Spin(1,3)
and
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2)
and twist them together as the Whitney sum.
The YMU theory is formulated in the twisted spinor-gauge bundle, where internal and space-time symmetries are treated equally.

3.1.1
Spinor bundle

Suppose Lorentzian inertial manifold
{\cal M}
is globally equipping a spin structure.
We define the principal spinor bundle as

\displaystyle\left({\cal M}{\otimes}\left({\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-1.63885pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\oplus{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\hskip-1.63885pt\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}\right),\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt},{\cal M},G^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{c}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{p}}}{\otimes}Spin(1,3)\right).

A total space is the trivially combined Weyl-spinor spaces (or the Dirac-spinor space) with Lorentzian manifold.
Projection map
\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
is provided owing to the covering map,

\displaystyle\tau_{\hskip-0.81949ptcov}:Spin(1,3)\rightarrow SO(1,3)\otimes\{R,L\},

yeilding

\displaystyle\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}:=\tau_{\hskip-0.81949ptcov}/\{R,L\}:{\cal M}{\otimes}\left({\mathbb{V}}^{~}_{\hskip-1.63885pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\oplus{\mathbb{V}}^{~}_{\hskip-1.63885pt\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}\right)\rightarrow{\cal M}/\{R,L\}:\left.{\bm{\xi}}\right|_{p}\hskip 1.00006pt\mapsto p\in{\cal M},

where
\bm{\xi}
is the Weyl spinor representing both dotted and undotted spinors defined in
section
2.5.2
(or the Dirac spinor defined in
section
2.5.3
), and it is the section
\bm{\xi}\in\Gamma_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\hskip-3.00003pt\left({\cal M},{\mathbb{V}}^{~}_{\hskip-1.63885pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\oplus{\mathbb{V}}^{~}_{\hskip-1.63885pt\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}},Spin(1,3)\right)
.
The structure group of the inertial bundle,
SO(1,3)
, is lifted to
Spin(1,3)
owing to projection map
\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
.

The connection of the spinor bundle for dotted and undotted Weyl spinors are provided, respectively, as

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathfrak{A}}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}:={\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}^{~}_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-0.81949ptl}[{\mathfrak{w}}\hskip 1.00006pt\overline{S}]

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}{\mathfrak{w}}_{~}^{{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\overline{S}}_{{\circ\circ}},~~~~{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}:={\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}^{~}_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-0.81949ptl}[{\mathfrak{w}}{S}]=\frac{i}{2}{\mathfrak{w}}^{{\circ\circ}}{S}_{{\circ\circ}},

(3.1.1)

where
\overline{S}
and
S
are generators of
Spin(1,3)
given as

\displaystyle\overline{S}^{ab}_{~}

\displaystyle:=i\hskip 1.00006pt\left[\frac{\overline{\gamma}^{a}_{~}}{2},\frac{\overline{\gamma}^{b}_{~}}{2}\right],~~~~S^{ab}_{~}:=i\hskip 1.00006pt\left[\frac{\gamma^{a}_{~}}{2},\frac{\gamma^{b}_{~}}{2}\right].

(3.1.2)

The connection
\overline{{\mathfrak{A}}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
and
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}
provide curvatures through the structure equation as

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{F}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}

\displaystyle:=d\overline{{\mathfrak{A}}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\overline{{\mathfrak{A}}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\wedge\overline{{\mathfrak{A}}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}=\frac{1}{2}\overline{S}^{~}_{{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{R}_{~}^{{\circ\circ}},~~~~\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}:=d{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}=\frac{1}{2}S^{~}_{{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{R}_{~}^{{\circ\circ}}.

(3.1.3)

Covariant Dirac operators concerning
Spin(1,3)
on the dotted and undotted Weyl spinor are provided, respectively, as

\displaystyle\overline{{\not\!d}}_{{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}^{~}\dot{\bm{\xi}}

\displaystyle:=\iota^{~}_{\gamma}\overline{d}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}^{~}\dot{\bm{\xi}}=\left(\overline{\not\!\partial}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\hskip 1.00006pt\overline{\not\!{\mathfrak{A}}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\right)\dot{\bm{\xi}},~~{\not\!d^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}}\bm{\xi}:=\iota^{~}_{\gamma}d_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\bm{\xi}=\left(\not\!\partial-i\hskip 1.00006pt{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\hskip-1.99997pt\not\!{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\right)\bm{\xi}.

(3.1.4)

Although the Lorentzian Clifford algebra consists of quaternions, we denote the imaginary unit as
i\in\mathbb{C}
instead of
\sqrt{-1}
here since Clifford-algebraic calculations without the concrete quaternion representation are enough to discuss details of the YMU theory.
When we perform direct calculations using the component representations, we use
\sqrt{-1}\in\mathbb{C}
treating
\bm{\gamma}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}
as the Clifford module
\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathbb{H}(2)
given in (
2.4.39a
) and (
2.4.39b
).

3.1.2
Gauge bundle

In the inertial manifold, we treat a principal bundle with a Lie group, namely the principal gauge bundle.
We exploit the
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
group as the structure group of the principal bundle.
We denote this gauge group as
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
to distinguish that owing to Euclidean space-time.
The Higgs spinor appears as a section of the gauge bundle.

A principal gauge bundle is defined as a tuple such that:

\displaystyle\left({\cal M}\otimes{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{e}}_{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}},\pi^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}},{\cal M},SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)\right).

We introduce the gauge group in the inertial bundle and treat two fundamental fields, the gauge connection
{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}
and the spinor section field
{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}
.
A space of the
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
-spinor is introduced as a section
{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}\Gamma\left({\cal M},{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{e}}_{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}},{SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)}\right)
belonging to the fundamental representation of the
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
symmetry.
The Higgs spinor is the
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
-spinor and is two independent scalar functions concerning the local Lorentz group.

A gauge connection
{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}:={\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{I}\hskip 1.99997pt\tau^{~}_{I}\in\Omega^{1}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}){\otimes}Ad({\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}(2))
is a Lie-algebra valued one-form object, where
\tau^{~}_{I}
is a generator of the
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
group and has the (
{2\hskip-1.99997pt\times\hskip-1.99997pt2}
) Hermitian matrix representation using the Pauli matrix, such as
\tau_{I}^{~}=\bm{\sigma}^{I}/2
.
Connection
{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}
belongs to an adjoint representation of the gauge group transformed like

\displaystyle{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left({\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\right)

\displaystyle=\bm{g}^{-1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.99997pt\bm{g}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}+i\hskip 1.00006pt{c^{-1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\bm{g}^{-1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.00006ptd\bm{g}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}=\bm{g}^{-1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.99997pt\bm{g}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~},

(3.1.5)

where
\bm{g}^{-1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.00006ptd\bm{g}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}=0
is used.
At the same time,
{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}
is a vector in
{{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}
and is Lorentz transformed as

\displaystyle{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}:End\left(\Omega^{1}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}})\right):{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\mapsto{{G}_{\hskip-1.63885ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO}}({\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt})

\displaystyle=\bm{\Lambda}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}.

(3.1.6)

Corresponding gauge curvature two-form
\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}
is defined through a structure equation such that:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}=\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{I}\hskip 1.99997pt\tau^{~}_{I}

\displaystyle:=d{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}=\left(d{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{I}+\frac{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}}{2}f^{I}_{~JK}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{J}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{K}\right)\tau^{~}_{I}{\hskip 1.99997pt\in\hskip 1.99997pt}\Omega^{2}({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}){\otimes}Ad({\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}(2)).

(3.1.7)

The covariant differential on
p
-form object
{\mathfrak{a}}\in\Omega^{p}({{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})
concerning the
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
symmetry is provided as

\displaystyle d^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{a}}\hskip 1.00006pt:=\bm{1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\mathfrak{a}}-\frac{i}{2}\hskip 1.00006pt{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}}\hskip 1.00006pt[{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt},{\mathfrak{a}}]_{\wedge}=\bm{1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\mathfrak{a}}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\wedge{\mathfrak{a}}.

(3.1.8)

Real constant
{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\in\mathbb{R}
is a coupling constant of the gauge interaction in physics.
The first and second Bianchi identities are

\displaystyle\left(d^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg}{\wedge}d^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg}\right){\mathfrak{a}}^{a}=-i{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{F}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\wedge{\mathfrak{a}}^{a}\quad\textrm{and}\quad d^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{F}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}=0.

(3.1.9)

The gauge connection and curvature are represented using the standard bases in
{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}
, respectively, as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}

\displaystyle={\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{I}\hskip 1.99997pt\tau^{~}_{I}=:{\mathscr{A}}^{I}_{a}{\mathfrak{e}}^{a}\hskip 1.99997pt\tau^{~}_{I},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}=\mathfrak{F}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}^{I}\hskip 1.99997pt\tau^{~}_{I}=:\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{ab}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathfrak{e}}^{a}\hskip-1.00006pt\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{b}\hskip 1.99997pt\tau^{~}_{I}.

(3.1.10)

In this component representation, tensor coefficients of the gauge curvature is provided using the gauge connection as

\displaystyle(\ref{SUCRV})\implies\mathscr{F}^{I}_{ab}

\displaystyle=\partial_{a}{\mathscr{A}}^{I}_{b}-\partial_{b}{\mathscr{A}}^{I}_{a}+{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{I}_{~JK}{\mathscr{A}}^{J}_{a}{\mathscr{A}}^{K}_{b},

(3.1.11)

The
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
covariant Dirac operator acts on the
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
-spinor as

\displaystyle{\not\!d}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg}\hskip 1.00006pt{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}}

\displaystyle:=\left(\bm{1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\!\not\!\partial-i\hskip 1.00006ptc^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\not\!{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\right){\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{~}},\quad\text{where}\quad\not\!{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}=\gamma_{~}^{{\circ}}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathscr{A}}^{I}_{\circ}\tau^{~}_{I}.

(3.1.12)

The
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
generator acts on the Higgs spinor as

\displaystyle[\tau^{~}_{I}\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}]^{~}_{A}:=[\tau_{I}]^{\hskip 3.27773ptB}_{A}\hskip 1.99997pt\phi^{~}_{B},\quad A,B\in\{\textsc{u},\textsc{d}\}.

(3.1.13)

3.1.3
Spinor-gauge bundle

A spinor-gauge bundle is a Whitney sum of spinor and gauge bundles given as

\displaystyle\left({\cal M}{\otimes}\left({\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\oplus{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}\right){\otimes}{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{e}}_{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}},\pi^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}\oplus\pi^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}},{\cal M},G^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{c}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{p}}{\otimes}Spin(1,3)\otimes{SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)}\right).

The total space of the gauge bundle is lifted to the spin manifold owing to the bundle map
\pi^{~}_{{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}
.
A connection and a curvature are provided, respectively, as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{A}}_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}
