\bm{\xi}(\theta)=&\bm{\xi}(-1)&{\longleftrightarrow}&\bm{\xi}(0)&{\longleftrightarrow}&\bm{\xi}(1)\\
&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{=}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{=}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{=}\\
&\dot{\bm{\xi}}&{\longleftrightarrow}&\bm{\phi}^{~}_{\textsc{h}}&{\longleftrightarrow}&\bm{\xi}\end{array}

The following diagram shows a schematic view of relations among Lie algebras
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}(2)
,
{{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C})}
and
\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C})}
in the amphometric space:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccccccccc}\theta=-1&{\longleftrightarrow}&-1<\theta<0&{\longleftrightarrow}&\theta=0&{\longleftrightarrow}&0<\theta<1&{\longleftrightarrow}&\theta=1\\
\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow\\
\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2.\mathbb{C})}&\longleftrightarrow&{{\mathfrak{C}}{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{u}}{\mathfrak{s}}}(2)&\longleftrightarrow&{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}(2)&\longleftrightarrow&{{\mathfrak{C}}{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{u}}{\mathfrak{s}}}(2)&\longleftrightarrow&{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2.\mathbb{C})\\
\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}\\
\bm{g}^{a}_{r}(\varphi;-1)&\longleftrightarrow&\bm{g}^{a}_{r}(\varphi;\theta)&\longleftrightarrow&\bm{g}^{a}_{r}(\varphi;0)&\longleftrightarrow&\bm{g}^{a}_{r}(\varphi;\theta)&\longleftrightarrow&\bm{g}^{a}_{r}(\varphi;1)\\
\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{=}&&&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{=}&&&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{=}\\
{\bm{g}^{a}_{r}(\varphi;1)}^{*}&&&&\bm{g}^{a}_{r}(\varphi)&&&&\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pt{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptr})\\
\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{=}\\
\pi_{\hskip-0.81949pt\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}(\bm{g}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptr})\end{array}\!.

4.3
Hodge-dual operator and chiral bases

We extend the Hodge-dual operator into the amphometric space modifying
Definition
2.1
for
{\mathfrak{a}}^{~}_{\theta},{\mathfrak{b}}^{~}_{\theta}\in\Omega({{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}})
as

\displaystyle\text{(\ref{HodgeD})}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{a}}^{~}_{\theta}\wedge{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\theta}^{~}}({\mathfrak{b}}^{~}_{\theta})

\displaystyle:=\text{det}[{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}}]^{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}\hskip 1.00006pt\langle{\mathfrak{a}}^{~}_{\theta}|{\mathfrak{b}}^{~}_{\theta}\rangle\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta},

(4.3.1a)

where we utilise a convention as

\displaystyle\text{det}[{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}}]^{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}

\displaystyle=e^{3i\pi\theta/2}\!.

(4.3.1b)

Thus, a real part of
\text{det}[{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}}]^{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}
can be negative.

The
{C\hskip-1.00006ptaus}(2)
-invariant bilinear form and pseudo-norm are also extended accordingly into the amphometric space as

\displaystyle\langle{\mathfrak{a}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\bullet}|{\mathfrak{b}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\bullet}\rangle\xrightarrow{{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\bullet}}}\rightarrow{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}}}\langle{\mathfrak{a}}^{~}_{\theta}|{\mathfrak{b}}^{~}_{\theta}\rangle~~\text{and}~~\|{\mathfrak{a}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\bullet}\|\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\bullet}\xrightarrow{{{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\bullet}}}\rightarrow{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}}}\|{\mathfrak{a}}^{~}_{\theta}\|\hskip 1.99997pt\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta},~~\bullet\in\{\textsc{e},\textsc{l}\},

with

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta}:=\frac{1}{4!}\epsilon^{~}_{{\circ\circ}{\circ\circ}}{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{\circ}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{\circ}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{\circ}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{\circ}~~\text{and}~~{\mathfrak{a}}^{~}_{\theta}=\frac{1}{p!}{a}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{p}}{\mathfrak{e}}^{i_{1}}_{\theta}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{i_{p}}_{\theta},

where
{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{a}{\in}\Omega^{1}({{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}})

(a\in\{0,1,2,3\}
) are the standard bases in
{{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}}
yielding

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{a}\big|^{~}_{\theta=0}={\mathfrak{e}}^{a}_{\textsc{e}}~~\text{and}~~{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{a}\big|^{~}_{\theta=\pm 1}={\mathfrak{e}}^{a}_{\textsc{l}}.

We introduce the ampho-base-vectors as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{a}

\displaystyle=\left({\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{0},{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{1},{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{2},{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{3}\right):=\left({\mathfrak{e}}^{0},i\kappa(\theta){\mathfrak{e}}^{1},i\kappa(\theta){\mathfrak{e}}^{2},i\kappa(\theta){\mathfrak{e}}^{3}\right)\!,

(4.3.2)

which can be understood analogically as analytic continuation owing to the replacement
t\rightarrow it
utilised in the quantum field theory.
The pseudo-norm (
2.2.1
) has complex values at other than
\theta=0,\pm 1
; thus, it is not the norm and is called the amphonorm.
Co-derivative and Laplace–Beltrami operators defined in (
2.2.4
) and (
2.2.5
) are extended into the amphometric space accordingly.
E.g., the Laplace–Beltrami operator in the amphometric space is

\displaystyle\Delta^{~}_{\theta}

\displaystyle=d^{~}_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006pt{{\hat{d}}}^{~}_{\theta}+{{\hat{d}}}^{~}_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006ptd^{~}_{\theta}=\partial^{~}_{0}+e^{i\pi\theta}(\partial^{~}_{1}+\partial^{~}_{2}+\partial^{~}_{3}).

The Hodge-dual operator acts on two-form objects in the amphometric space as

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\theta}^{~}}({{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{0}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{1}})={{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{2}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{3}},~~~{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\theta}^{~}}({{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{0}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{2}})={{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{3}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{1}},~~~{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\theta}^{~}}({{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{0}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{3}})={{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{1}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{2}},

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\theta}^{~}}({{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{1}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{2}})={{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{0}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{3}},~~~{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\theta}^{~}}({{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{1}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{3}})={{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{2}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{0}},~~~{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\theta}^{~}}({{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{2}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{3}})={{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{0}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{1}},

and the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading two-form bases are provided, e.g., as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}^{+\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\theta}:=\left\{{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{0}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{1}}+{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{2}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{3}},\hskip 1.99997pt{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{0}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{2}}-{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{1}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{3}},\hskip 1.99997pt{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{0}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{3}}+{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{1}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{2}}\right\},

(4.3.3a)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}^{-\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\theta}:=\left\{{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{0}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{1}}-{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{2}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{3}},\hskip 1.99997pt{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{0}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{2}}+{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{1}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{3}},\hskip 1.99997pt{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{0}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{3}}-{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{1}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{2}}\right\},

(4.3.3b)

yielding

\displaystyle{P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\theta}=\pm{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\theta}

(4.3.3c)

and

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\theta}\wedge{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm\hskip 0.81949ptb}_{\theta}=\begin{cases}~~~0&a\hskip 3.00003pt{\neq}\hskip 3.00003ptb\\
\pm 2\hskip 1.00006pte^{3i\pi\theta/2}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta}&a=b\end{cases},~~\text{and}~~{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\theta}\wedge{\mathfrak{S}}^{\mp\hskip 0.81949ptb}_{\theta}=0.

(4.3.3d)

They provide
(\ref{DHES})
by replacement “
e
”
\mapsto
“
\theta
”.
One can easily confirm that

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm\hskip 0.81949pta}_{1}:=\left.{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\theta}\right|^{~}_{\theta=1}={\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\textsc{l}},~~~{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm\hskip 0.81949pta}_{-1}:=\left.{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\theta}\right|^{~}_{\theta=-1}={\mathfrak{S}}^{\mp\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\textsc{l}}~~\text{and}~~{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm\hskip 0.81949pta}_{0}:=\left.{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\theta}\right|^{~}_{\theta=0}={\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\textsc{e}}.

(4.3.4)

Operators
{P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}
with
{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\bullet}^{~}}\rightarrow{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\theta}^{~}}
in (
2.2.8
) are still the projection operator in the amphometric space fulfiling
{P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}{\circ}{P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}={P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}
and
{P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}{\circ}{P^{\mp}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}=0
.
Here, we do not put the subscript “
\theta
” from the projection operators as
{P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}
for simplicity since the metric is clear owing to the operand.
The projection operator in the amphometric space yields

\displaystyle(\ref{PSpm})\implies P^{\pm}_{0}

\displaystyle:={P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}\big|^{~}_{\theta=0}=P^{\pm}_{\textsc{e}}~~\text{and}~~P^{\pm}_{1}:={P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}\big|^{~}_{\theta=1}=P^{\pm}_{\textsc{l}}=P^{\mp}_{-1};

thus, the middle formula of (
4.3.4
) means that

\displaystyle P^{\pm}_{-1}{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm}_{-1}=P^{\mp}_{\textsc{l}}{\mathfrak{S}}^{\mp}_{\textsc{l}}={\mathfrak{S}}^{\mp}_{\textsc{l}}.

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading space of curvature two-forms and their Hodge-dual in the amphometric space are provided as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}^{\pm}:={P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}~~\textrm{and}~~{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\theta}^{~}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}^{\pm})=\pm\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}^{\pm}\in{{\mathbb{V}}^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}\left(\Omega_{\theta}^{2}({{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}})\right){\otimes}Ad(\mathfrak{g}),

(4.3.5)

where
\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}^{\pm}
are, respectively, spanned by
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm}_{\theta}
.

The curvature is the two-form object from the structure equation concerning the one-form connection.
The Hodge-dual operator is not endomorphism for one-form objects; thus, their space does not have the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading structure.
Nevertheless, splitting the one-form object space into two subspaces concerning the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading structure of the curvature two-form is useful.
We discuss this subject in the amphometric space below.
We introduce a chiral basis for the one-form objects as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{e}}_{ch}^{a}

\displaystyle:=\left({\mathfrak{e}}^{0}-\kappa(\theta){\mathfrak{e}}^{3},{\mathfrak{e}}^{0}+\kappa(\theta){\mathfrak{e}}^{3},{\mathfrak{e}}^{1}+i{\mathfrak{e}}^{2},{\mathfrak{e}}^{1}-i{\mathfrak{e}}^{2}\right)\!.

(4.3.6)

In the Lorentzian metric space,
{\mathfrak{e}}_{ch}^{3}
(
{\mathfrak{e}}_{ch}^{4}
) corresponds to a chiral-right (-left) polarization vector for a massless vector-boson travelling along the third axis, respectively.
Though the one-form object cannot be an eigenvector concerning the Hodge-dual operator, we observe the chiral structure of this one-form basis, such as

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lc}{\mathfrak{e}}^{1}_{ch}{\wedge}{\mathfrak{e}}^{3}_{ch}=[{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}_{\theta}]^{1}{+}i\hskip 1.00006pt[{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}_{\theta}]^{2}\in{{\mathbb{V}}^{+}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}\left(\Omega_{\theta}^{2}\right),&{\mathfrak{e}}^{1}_{ch}{\wedge}{\mathfrak{e}}^{4}_{ch}=[{\mathfrak{S}}^{-}_{\theta}]^{1}{-}i\hskip 1.00006pt[{\mathfrak{S}}^{-}_{\theta}]^{2}\in{{\mathbb{V}}^{-}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}\left(\Omega_{\theta}^{2}\right)\\
{\mathfrak{e}}^{2}_{ch}{\wedge}{\mathfrak{e}}^{3}_{ch}=[{\mathfrak{S}}^{-}_{\theta}]^{1}{+}i\hskip 1.00006pt[{\mathfrak{S}}^{-}_{\theta}]^{2}\in{{\mathbb{V}}^{-}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}\left(\Omega_{\theta}^{2}\right),&{\mathfrak{e}}^{2}_{ch}{\wedge}{\mathfrak{e}}^{4}_{ch}=[{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}_{\theta}]^{1}{-}i\hskip 1.00006pt[{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}_{\theta}]^{2}\in{{\mathbb{V}}^{+}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}\left(\Omega_{\theta}^{2}\right)\end{array}\!.

Owing to this chiral structure, we assign base vectors
{\mathfrak{e}}^{1}_{ch}
as the chiral-right and
{\mathfrak{e}}^{2}_{ch}
as the chiral-left.
A two-form object in
{{\mathbb{V}}^{+}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}\left(\Omega_{\theta}^{2}\right)
(
{{\mathbb{V}}^{-}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}\left(\Omega_{\theta}^{2}\right)
) is, respectively, SD (ASD); thus, the chiral-right polarization corresponds to the SD two-form and the chiral-right to ASD.
We have exploited the chiral representation for the Clifford algebra, which corresponds to taking the third-axis
{\mathfrak{e}}^{3}
as the spin quantisation axis.
Thus, the chiral-right (-left) spinor moving along the third axis has a positive (negative) helicity, respectively.
We show a relation between the chirality of the Weyl spinor and the two-form object schematically as

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\text{Weyl spinor with chiral representation}\longrightarrow\text{spin quantisation-axis}={\mathfrak{e}}^{3}\!,

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{lllcr}{\mathfrak{e}}^{3}_{ch}\in\text{right polarization}&\xrightarrow{\text{(\ref{eeSS})}}&\{{\mathfrak{e}}^{1}_{ch},{\mathfrak{e}}^{3}_{ch}\}\in\text{chiral-right}&=&\text{SD}\\
{\mathfrak{e}}^{4}_{ch}\in\text{left polarization}&\xrightarrow{\text{(\ref{eeSS})}}&\{{\mathfrak{e}}^{2}_{ch},{\mathfrak{e}}^{4}_{ch}\}\in\text{chiral-left}&=&\text{ASD}\end{array}\right.\!.

4.4
Clifford algebra and Dirac operator

A representation of Clifford algebra in
Spin({\cal M}_{\theta})
with the fixed
\theta
-value, namely the
amphoClifford algebr
a is defined to fulfil
algebra

\displaystyle\{\gamma_{\theta}^{\hskip 2.45836pta},\gamma_{\theta}^{\hskip 2.45836ptb}\}=2\hskip 1.00006pt\eta_{\theta}^{\hskip 1.63885ptab}\hskip 1.00006pt\bm{1}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\implies\gamma^{\hskip 2.45836pt0}_{\theta}:=\gamma^{\hskip 2.45836pt0}_{\textsc{e}}~~\textrm{and}~~\gamma^{\hskip 2.45836pta}_{\theta}:=e^{i\pi\theta/2}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma^{\hskip 2.45836pta}_{\textsc{e}}~~\textrm{for}~~a\in\{1,2,3\}.

(4.4.1)

The definition (
4.4.1
) fulfils the amphoClifford algebra at any values of
\theta\in[-1,1]
.
The amphoClifford algebra provides Euclidean and Lorentzian ones as

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}^{~}_{\theta}=\begin{cases}\bm{\gamma}^{~}_{\textsc{l}},&\theta=+1\\
\bm{\gamma}^{~}_{\textsc{e}},&\theta=\hskip 8.00003pt0\\
\bar{\bm{\gamma}}^{~}_{\textsc{l}},&\theta=-1\\
\end{cases}.

(4.4.2)

According to introducing the amphoClifford algebra, we obtain a generator of
Ampho(2)
as

\displaystyle S^{ab}_{\theta}:=i\hskip 1.00006pt\left[\frac{\gamma^{\hskip 2.45836pta}_{\theta}}{2},\frac{\gamma^{\hskip 2.45836ptb}_{\theta}}{2}\right].

(4.4.3)

For the chiral operator in the amphometric space, namely the
amphochiral operator
, we exploit the definition (
2.4.3
) with
\bm{\eta}=\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}
and obtain it as

\displaystyle\Gamma^{~}_{\!\theta}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{1}{\text{det}[{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}}]}}\gamma_{\theta}^{\hskip 2.45836pt0}\gamma_{\theta}^{\hskip 2.45836pt1}\gamma_{\theta}^{\hskip 2.45836pt2}\gamma_{\theta}^{\hskip 2.45836pt3}=f_{\Gamma}(\theta)\gamma_{\textsc{e}}^{\hskip 1.63885pt0}\gamma_{\textsc{e}}^{\hskip 1.63885pt1}\gamma_{\textsc{e}}^{\hskip 1.63885pt2}\gamma_{\textsc{e}}^{\hskip 1.63885pt3}\quad\text{with}\quad f_{\Gamma}(\theta):=\frac{e^{3i\pi\theta/2}}{\sqrt{e^{3i\pi\theta}}}.

(4.4.4)

Function
f_{\Gamma}(\theta)
provides a factor at boundaries of the amphometric space as

\displaystyle f_{\Gamma}(\theta)=\begin{cases}+1,&\theta\rightarrow 1+0,\quad\theta\rightarrow-1-0\\
-1,&\theta\rightarrow 1-0,\quad\theta\rightarrow-1+0\end{cases},\quad\text{owing to the standard convention }\text{Re}[\sqrt{\bullet}]\geq 0.

(4.4.5)

Thus, in the amphometric space with
\theta\in[-1,1]
, we obtain
f_{\Gamma}(1)=-f_{\Gamma}(-1)=1
, and it has discontinuity at
\theta=\pm 1/3
.
To avoid this discontinuity, we exploit the convention

\displaystyle\sqrt{e^{3i\pi\theta}}=e^{3i\pi\theta/2}\implies f_{\Gamma}(\theta)=1

(4.4.6)

for the amphochiral operator (
4.4.4
).
As a result, the factor in the chiral operator compensates a
\theta
-dependence in
\gamma^{~}_{\theta}
, and we have a
\theta
-independent amphochiral operator as

(
4.4.4
)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{\textsc{e}}^{\hskip 1.63885pt0}\gamma_{\textsc{e}}^{\hskip 1.63885pt1}\gamma_{\textsc{e}}^{\hskip 1.63885pt2}\gamma_{\textsc{e}}^{\hskip 1.63885pt3}=\begin{cases}\left(\!\begin{array}[]{rc}-\bm{1}_{2}&\bm{0}_{2}\\
\bm{0}_{2}&\bm{1}_{2}\end{array}\!\right),&\text{dim-4}\\
\hskip 5.0pt\left(\!\begin{array}[]{rc}-1&0\\
0&1\end{array}\!\right),&\text{dim-2}\end{cases},

(4.4.7)

yielding the
\theta
-independent projection operator as

\displaystyle P^{\pm}_{\!\theta}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\bm{1}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\pm\Gamma_{\!\theta}\right)=\text{(\ref{Ppm})}\implies P^{\pm}_{\!\theta}{{\circ}}P^{\pm}_{\!\theta}=P^{\pm}_{\!\theta},~P^{\pm}_{\!\theta}P^{\mp}_{\!\theta}=0.

(4.4.8)

The chirality of the amphospinor is consistently defined owing to the projection operators (
4.4.8
) at any
\theta\in[-1,1]
; thus, the amphospinor space
{\mathbb{V}}^{\theta}_{\!{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}
splits into a disjoint pair as

\displaystyle\Gamma^{~}_{\!\theta}\hskip 1.99997pt\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=-\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}(\theta)\implies P^{-}_{\!\theta}\bm{\xi}(\theta)=\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}(\theta)\in{\mathbb{V}}^{\theta\textsc{u}}_{\!{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}},

(4.4.9a)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{~}_{\!\theta}\hskip 1.99997pt\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=+\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}(\theta)\implies P^{+}_{\!\theta}\bm{\xi}(\theta)=\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}(\theta)\in{\mathbb{V}}^{\theta\textsc{d}}_{\!{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}.

(4.4.9b)

We extend amphospinors, shown in the diagram (
4.2
), into the chiral amphospinors such that

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rccccc}&\theta=-1&{\longleftrightarrow}&\theta=0&{\longleftrightarrow}&\theta=1\\
&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow\\
\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}(\theta)=&\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}(-1)=\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}&{\longleftrightarrow}&\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}(0)=\bm{\phi}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}&{\longleftrightarrow}&\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}(1)=\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{u}}\\
\\
\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}(\theta)=&\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}(-1)=\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}&{\longleftrightarrow}&\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}(0)=\bm{\phi}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}&{\longleftrightarrow}&\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}(1)=\bm{\xi}^{~}_{\textsc{d}}\\
&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow\\
\text{(\ref{VpmthetaR})}=&\text{(\ref{phiLp2})}&{\longleftrightarrow}&\text{(\ref{phiEm})}&{\longleftrightarrow}&\text{(\ref{phiLm1})}\\
\\
\text{(\ref{VpmthetaL})}=&\text{(\ref{phiLm2})}&{\longleftrightarrow}&\text{(\ref{phiEp})}&{\longleftrightarrow}&\text{(\ref{phiLp1})}\end{array}.

The Dirac operator in the amphometric space is defined as

\displaystyle{\not\!d}_{\theta}

\displaystyle:=\iota_{\gamma}{d_{\theta}}=\not\!\partial_{\theta}=\gamma^{a}_{\theta}\hskip 1.99997pt\partial_{a}^{~}\implies\not\!\partial^{~}_{\theta}P^{\pm}_{\!\theta}=P^{\mp}_{\!\theta}\hskip-1.99997pt\not\!\partial^{~}_{\theta}.

(4.4.19)

The operator flips the chirality; thus, it is the Dirac operator.
The generalised Lapracian is provided as

\displaystyle\left(\not\!\partial_{\theta}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{~}_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006pt\bm{1}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}~~\text{where}~~\Delta^{~}_{\theta}:=(\partial^{~}_{0})^{2}+e^{i\pi\theta}\left((\partial^{~}_{1})^{2}+(\partial^{~}_{2})^{2}+(\partial^{~}_{3})^{2}\right)\!.

(4.4.20)

We can confirm that

\displaystyle\not\!\partial_{\textsc{v}\theta}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\theta=0}\not\!\partial_{\textsc{ve}}\Rightarrow\text{(\ref{diracdE})},\hskip 8.99994pt\not\!\partial_{\textsc{v}\theta}\xrightarrow{\theta=1}\not\!\partial_{\textsc{vl}}\Rightarrow\text{(\ref{diracd})},

\displaystyle\not\!\partial_{\textsc{s}\theta}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\theta=0}\not\!\partial_{\textsc{se}}\Rightarrow\text{(\ref{gammadelE})},~\not\!\partial_{\textsc{s}\theta}\xrightarrow{\theta=1}\not\!\partial_{\textsc{sl}}\Rightarrow\text{(\ref{gammadelL})},~~\not\!\partial_{\textsc{s}\theta}\xrightarrow{\theta=-1}\overline{\not\!\partial}_{\textsc{sl}}\Rightarrow\text{(\ref{gammadelL})}.

4.5
YMU theory with amphometric

Section
3
formulated the classical YMU theory in the local inertial manifold with internal
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
and local
SO(1,3)
groups.
This section extends the theory into the amphometric space.

SO(4)
and
SO(1,3)
groups compose the ampshometric space as the one-parameter subgroup of
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
.
As a result, we showed symmetries of the Euclidean space are homotopic to those of the Minkowski space through the amphometric, which allows us to import some kinds of mathematical achievements of the YMU theory in the Euclidean space into the Minkowski space
[
10
]
.

4.5.1
Gauge bosons

a) Yang–Mills gauge boson:

We extend the Yang–Mills gauge boson Lagrangian into the amphometric space.
In the spinor-gauge bundle, we introduced connection
{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}
and curvature
\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}
in
section
3.1.2
.
We replace the metric tensor in connections and curvatures as discussed in
section
4.4
and denote them as
{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\theta}
and
\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\rightarrow\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\theta}
, where we omit subscript “
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}
” for simplicity.
The gauge boson Lagrangian is defined in the amphometric space as

(
3.2.7
)

\displaystyle\implies{\mathfrak{L}}_{\textsc{y\hskip-0.70004ptm}}({\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\theta}):=\text{det}[{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}}]^{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}\left[\|\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\theta})\|^{2}\right]\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta}={\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}\left[\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\theta})\wedge\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\theta}(\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\theta})\right],

(4.5.1a)

where we treat square-root function det
[{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}}]^{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}
through (
4.3.1b
).
We utilise Carten’s
repére mobile
formalism and represent Lagrangians the coordinate-free way like (
4.5.1a
).
Although this representation can show the theory in a frame-independent way, the role of the metric tensor is not apparent.
Here, we show the Lagrangian form using a component representation as,

(
4.5.1a
)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}{\eta^{~}_{\theta}}^{{\circ}\star}{\eta^{~}_{\theta}}^{{\circ}\star}\mathscr{F}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta\hskip 0.81949pt{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.99997pt\mathscr{F}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta\hskip 0.81949pt{\star\star}}\hskip 1.99997pt\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta}~~\text{and}~~\mathscr{F}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta\hskip 0.81949ptab}:=\mathscr{F}^{I}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta\hskip 0.81949ptab}\hskip 1.99997pt\tau_{I},

(4.5.1b)

and clarify the role of metric tensors.
In the amphometric space, we obtain a component representation of the equation of motion for the
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
Yang–Mills gauge boson as

\displaystyle\hat{d}_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\theta}}\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}

\displaystyle=\hat{d}\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}+\frac{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}{2}\left[\hat{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\theta},\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}\right]^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\wedge}={{\eta^{~}_{\theta}}}^{\circ\circ}\left(\partial_{\circ}\mathscr{F}^{I}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta\hskip 0.81949pt{\circ}\star}+{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\hskip 1.00006ptf^{I}_{~JK}\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathscr{A}}^{J}_{\theta\hskip 0.81949pt{\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathscr{F}^{K}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta\hskip 0.81949pt{\circ}\star}\right){\mathfrak{V}_{\theta}}^{\star}=\dot{0},

(4.5.2)

corresponding to (
3.2.10
) in the Lorentzian metric space, where
{\mathscr{A}}^{I}_{\theta\hskip 0.81949pta}
is a coefficient function of
{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\theta}
.
The structure constant
f^{I}_{~JK}
is due to the internal
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
and has nothing to do with the metric tensor of the space-time manifold.

A space of the curvature two-form has the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading structure owing to the projection operator concerning the Hodge-dual as discussed in
section
2.2
.
We can decompose the curvature two-form and its Hodge-dual, respectively, as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\theta}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{\theta}+\mathfrak{F}^{-}_{\theta}~~\text{and}~~\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\theta}=\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{\theta}-\mathfrak{F}^{-}_{\theta},

(4.5.3)

where
\mathfrak{F}^{\pm}_{\theta}
is defined by (
4.3.5
).
Consequently, we obtain the Lagrangian form using
\mathfrak{F}^{\pm}_{\theta}
as

(
4.5.1a
)

\displaystyle=\left(\|\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{\theta}\|^{2}+\|\mathfrak{F}^{-}_{\theta}\|^{2}\right)\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta}.

(4.5.4)

The extremal of the bosonic action integral concerning a amphoconnection
{\mathfrak{A}}^{~}_{\theta}
is given by the SD curvature (
\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{\theta}
) or ASD curvature (
\mathfrak{F}^{-}_{\theta}
) in the
Euclidean space
as known well.
We prove this remark for investigating the difference between Euclidean and Minkowski spaces.

Remark 4.2
.

The Yang–Mills action in the closed and oriented Euclidean metric space has extremal at the SD- or ASD-curvature.

Proof.

The Yang–Mills action has the representation using SD and ASD curvatures as

\displaystyle\mathscr{I}_{\hskip-1.63885pt\textsc{y\hskip-0.70004ptm}}^{~}

\displaystyle=\text{det}[{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}}]^{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\hskip 1.99997pt\int_{{T^{*}\hskip-1.63885pt{\cal M}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt\theta}}\|\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\theta}\|^{2}\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta}=\text{det}[{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}}]^{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\hskip 1.99997pt\int_{{T^{*}\hskip-1.63885pt{\cal M}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt\theta}}\left(\|\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{\theta}\|^{2}+\|\mathfrak{F}^{-}_{\theta}\|^{2}\right)\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta},

(4.5.5)

where we assume
{{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}}
is the closed and oriented manifold.
On the other hand, the Chern index in the amphometric space is provided as

\displaystyle c^{~}_{2}\left(\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{{T^{*}\hskip-1.63885pt{\cal M}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt\theta}}\left({\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}\left[\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\theta}\wedge\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\theta}\right]-{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}\left[\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\theta}\right]^{2}\right)\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta}=\frac{\text{det}[{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}}]^{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}\hskip 1.99997pt}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{{T^{*}\hskip-1.63885pt{\cal M}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt\theta}}\left(\|\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{\theta}\|^{2}-\|\mathfrak{F}^{-}_{\theta}\|^{2}\right)\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta},

(4.5.6)

where we use
{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}\left[\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\theta}\right]=0
.
When
\theta=0
, equivalently, when the space-time manifold has the Euclidean metric,
\|\mathfrak{F}^{\pm}_{\textsc{e}}\|^{2}
is positive definite and we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathscr{I}_{\hskip-1.63885pt\textsc{y\hskip-0.70004ptm}}^{~}

\displaystyle\geq 8\pi^{2}\left|{c}_{2}\left(\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\textsc{e}}\right)\right|.

Therefore, the extremal of the action integral is given by
\|\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{\textsc{e}}\|=0
or
\|\mathfrak{F}^{-}_{\textsc{e}}\|=0
.

At the general point other than
\theta=\pm 1,0
, the amphonorm
\|\mathfrak{F}^{\pm}_{\theta}\|^{2}
is complex valued in general; thus, it is not the ordinal number, and the magnitude relationship cannot be defined.
In the Lorentzian metric space, the pseudo-norm
\|\bullet\|^{~}_{\textsc{l}}\in\mathbb{R}
is neither positive definite nor non-degenerate.
Therefore,
Remark
4.2
is maintained only in the Euclidean metric space.
We note that when the Lagrangian density is given by the curvature norm squared in the local inertial space-time,
Remark
4.2
holds even with
{\mathfrak{w}}\neq 0
.
On the other hand, the whole YMU action includes the
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
gauge connection, and the Lagrangian density is not simply given by the
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
curvature norm squared as (
4.5.5
); thus, the
Remark
4.2
holds only in vacuum.
∎

Though
Remark
4.2
holds only in the Euclidean space, we consider extending it to the entire amphometric space.
Hereafter, we assume a solution exists and is SD.
We introduce a space

\displaystyle{\cal W}^{+}

\displaystyle:={{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}}\otimes\theta\in[0,1],~~~{\cal W}^{-}:={{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}}\otimes\theta\in[-1,0]~~\text{and}~~{\cal W}:={\cal W}^{+}\cup{\cal W}^{-}={{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}}\otimes\theta\in[-1,1].

Suppose that the equation of motion
(\ref{ELEoMpm})
has solutions at any
\theta\in[0,1)
.
At
\theta=1
, the solution exists at least in a subspace of
{{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}
.
More precisely, at
\theta=1
, we assume that singularities of the curvature are at most countable number of poles of the type

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}^{~}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}(\|\bm{r}^{i}_{\theta}\|^{2}+\lambda)^{-m}+\beta

(4.5.7)

on a space-like Cauchy surface, where
\bm{r}^{i}_{\theta}
is position vectors of an
i^{\text{th}}
pole on the Cauchy surface at
\theta=0
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
0<\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.

\{\alpha_{i},\beta\}
are regular functions in
{{T\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}}
.
In this case, the singularity does not exist in
{\cal W}^{+}{\hskip-1.00006pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt}{\cal W}^{+}_{1}
since
\|\bullet^{~}_{\textsc{e}}\|\geq 0
and
\mathrm{Im}\|\bullet^{~}_{\theta\neq 1}\|\neq 0
.
At
\theta=0
, we assume the equation of motion gives the SD curvature
\mathfrak{F}_{0}^{+}
as its solution.
Suppose
{\mathfrak{A}}_{0}^{+}
is the vacuum solution of the Yang–Mills equation providing the SD curvature through the structure equation at
\theta=0
; we obtain that

\displaystyle\hat{d}\mathfrak{F}_{0}^{+}=0~~\text{and}~~\mathfrak{F}_{0}^{+}=d{\mathfrak{A}}_{0}^{+}-i\hskip 1.00006ptc^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{A}}_{0}^{+}\wedge{\mathfrak{A}}_{0}^{+}.

(4.5.8)

Owing to the above assumptions, this solution can be extended into
{\cal W}^{+}{\hskip-1.00006pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt}{{\cal W}^{+}_{1}}
, and into
{\cal W}^{+}_{1}
except at poles above.
Consequently, we obtain a diagram for the SD curvature
\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}^{+}
as

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cccccccclc}&&\theta=0&{\longleftrightarrow}&0<\theta<1&{\longleftrightarrow}&\theta=1\\
&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow\\
\Omega^{2}_{\textsc{sd}}\left({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}}\right)&\hookrightarrow&\Omega^{2}_{\textsc{sd}}\left(T^{*}{\cal W}^{+}_{0}\right)&\longleftrightarrow&\Omega^{2}_{\textsc{sd}}\left(T^{*}{\cal W}^{+}_{\theta}\right)&\longleftrightarrow&\Omega^{2}_{\textsc{sd}}\left(T^{*}{\cal W}^{+}_{1}\right)&\hookleftarrow&\Omega^{2}_{\textsc{sd}}\left(\overline{{{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}\right)\\
\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\hskip 10.00002pt\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}\\
\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{e}}^{+}&=&\mathfrak{F}_{0}^{+}&\longleftrightarrow&\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}^{+}&\longleftrightarrow&\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{1}&=&\hskip 8.99994pt\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{l}}^{+}\end{array}

where

\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008ptT^{*}{\cal W}_{\theta}^{+}:=T^{*}\hskip-1.00006pt{\cal M}_{\theta}\otimes\theta\in[0,1],\quad\overline{{{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}:={{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}{\hskip-1.00006pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt}\left\{\bm{r}^{i}_{\textsc{l}}(t)\big|\|\bm{r}^{i}_{\textsc{l}}(t)\|^{2}+\lambda=0\right\},

(4.5.14)

and
\Omega^{2}_{\textsc{sd}}
is a vector space
\Omega^{2}
spanned by the SD basis as

\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008pt{\mathfrak{a}}\in\Omega^{2}_{\textsc{sd}}\left(T^{*}{\cal W}_{\theta}\right)\implies{\widehat{\mathrm{H}}_{\theta}^{~}}\left({\mathfrak{a}}\right)=+{\mathfrak{a}}.

(4.5.15)

The connection and curvature forms with
\theta\in[0,1)
also belong to the adjoint representation of the causal subgroup, and they at
\theta=1
belong to
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
.
(See (
4.2
)).
We set the Lie algebra assignment as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{l}}^{+}{\hskip 1.00006pt\in\hskip 1.00006pt}\Omega^{2}_{\textsc{sd}}\left(\overline{{{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}\right)\otimes{Ad}\left({\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C})\right).

(4.5.16)

The Euclidean SD solution can be extended to
{\cal W}^{-}
, too.
The diagram for
{\cal W}^{-}
is

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cccccccclc}&&\theta=-1&{\longleftrightarrow}&-1<\theta<0&{\longleftrightarrow}&\theta=0\\
&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow\\
\Omega^{2}_{\textsc{sd}}\left(\overline{{{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}\right)&\hookrightarrow&\Omega^{2}_{\textsc{sd}}\left(T^{*}{\cal W}^{-}_{-1}\right)&\longleftrightarrow&\Omega^{2}_{\textsc{sd}}\left(T^{*}{\cal W}^{-}_{\theta}\right)&\longleftrightarrow&\Omega^{2}_{\textsc{sd}}\left(T^{*}{\cal W}^{-}_{0}\right)&\hookleftarrow&\Omega^{2}_{\textsc{sd}}\left({T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}}\right)\\
\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\hskip 10.00002pt\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}\\
\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{l}}^{+}&=&\mathfrak{F}_{-1}^{+}&\longleftrightarrow&\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}^{+}&\longleftrightarrow&\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{0}&=&\hskip 8.99994pt\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{e}}^{+}.\end{array}

We define the Chern index in the Lorentzian metric space as the limit

\displaystyle\infty>\left|\lim_{\theta\rightarrow\pm 1}\frac{\text{det}[{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}}]^{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}\hskip 1.99997pt}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{\overline{{{T^{*}\hskip-1.63885pt{\cal M}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.73616pt\theta}}}}\|\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{\theta}\|^{2}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta}\right|

\displaystyle=:\left|c^{~}_{2}\left(\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{l}}\right)\right|=\left|\mathscr{I}_{\hskip-1.63885pt\textsc{y\hskip-0.70004ptm}}^{\textsc{l}}\right|,

(4.5.22)

when it exists as, at least, the one-sided limit of
\theta\rightarrow\pm 1\mp\varepsilon
with
0<\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}
.
This quantity is homotopy equivalence to the Chern index in the Euclidean space; thus, the Chern index has the same number as that in the Euclidean space.
The Lorentzian SD curvature
\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{l}}^{+}
is provided from Lorentzian gauge-connection
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\textsc{l}}
, which fulfils the structure equation and equation of motion in the Lorentzian metric space.
Consequently, when the Euclidean Yang–Mills gauge curvature is SD and fulfils (
4.5.7
), the Lorentzian gauge curvature is also SD providing the Chern index as above.
We discuss it in detail from the cobordism point of view in
Appndix
B
for the instanton solution.

We showed that SD solutions in the Lorentzian metric space correspond to those in the Euclidean metric space above.
We can also show the Lorentzian Yang–Mills equation has no solutions other than them.
Suppose the Lorentzian Yang–Mills equation has solutions of a type

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{l}}

\displaystyle=\alpha\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{l}}^{+}+\beta\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{l}}^{-}~~\text{with}~~\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C},

(4.5.23)

in a domain
\overline{{{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}
and singularity of solution
\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{l}}
is a type (
4.5.7
).
Solutions
\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{l}}^{\pm}
in (
4.5.23
) are, respectively, spanned by basis
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\textsc{l}}
given by (
2.2.12a
) and (
2.2.12b
).
We introduce
\overline{T^{*}{\cal W}^{+}_{1}}
as submersion of
\overline{{{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}
by means of a differentiable map

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\textsc{l}}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm\hskip 0.81949pta}_{\theta}~~\text{and}~~\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\textsc{l}}\rightarrow\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}~~\text{in}~~\theta\in[-1,1].

(4.5.24)

The same replacement applying to the Lorentzian Yang–Mills equation provides the amphometric Yang–Mills equation having the solution
\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}
in
T^{*}{\cal W}^{+}_{\theta}
, which is
closer of
\overline{T^{*}{\cal W}^{+}_{\theta}}
at
\theta\neq 1
.
In this case,
\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}
is a solution of the Yang–Mills equation and the structure equation in
T^{*}{\cal W}^{+}_{\theta}
at
\theta\in[0,1)
.
The Chern index has the same number in the Lorentzian and Euclidean metric spaces owing to the cobordism; thus,
\|\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{e}}^{-}\|=0\implies\|\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{l}}^{-}\|=0
.
Consequently, we have concluded that either the SD or ASD solution gives the solution of the Lorentzian Yang–Mills equation.

b) gravitational gauge boson:

The gravitational Lagrangian form, replacing the metric tensor from Lorentzian to amphometric, provides the Lagrangian as

\displaystyle(\ref{EHLL})

\displaystyle\implies{\mathfrak{L}}_{\textsc{g}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{r}}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~},{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{~}):=-\frac{\text{det}[{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}}]^{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}{\hbar\kappa^{~}_{\textsc{E}}}{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{c}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{p}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\|\mathfrak{F}^{\theta}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{c}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{p}}\|^{2}_{\theta}\right]\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta}=-\frac{1}{\hbar\kappa^{~}_{\textsc{E}}}{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{c}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{p}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\mathfrak{F}^{\theta}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{c}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{p}}\wedge\hat{\mathfrak{F}}^{\theta}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{c}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{p}}\right],

(4.5.25)

where co-Poincaré connection and curvature are given by (
2.1.20a
) and (
2.1.20b
) with the Lorentzian metric.

\mathfrak{F}^{\theta}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{c}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{p}}
is a Co-Poincaré curvature written by
{\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}
and
{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}
.
A space of the two-form objects is split into two subspaces owing to the Hodge-dual operator as (
2.2.7
).
According to this splitting, we obtain the splitting

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}[\mathfrak{R}_{\theta}^{~}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})\wedge{\mathfrak{S}}_{\theta}^{~}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{~})]

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}[\mathfrak{R}^{+}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})\wedge{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{~})]+{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}[\mathfrak{R}^{-}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})\wedge{\mathfrak{S}}^{-}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{~})],

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}^{\pm}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})

\displaystyle:={P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}\mathfrak{R}_{\theta}^{~}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})~~\text{and}~~{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{~}):={P^{\pm}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\hat{\textsc{h}}}}{\mathfrak{S}}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{~}).

We can write them using the Rich and scalar curvature as

\displaystyle-{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}[\mathfrak{R}_{\theta}^{~}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})\wedge{\mathfrak{S}}_{\theta}^{~}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{~})]=R({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})\hskip 1.99997pt\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta}~~\text{and}~~-{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}[\mathfrak{R}^{\pm}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})\wedge{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{~})]=\frac{1}{2}\left(R({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta})\pm(R^{0}+R_{0})\right)\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta},

where

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002ptR^{0}:=i\frac{\kappa(-\theta)}{2}\epsilon^{\hskip 10.65283pt{\star\star}}_{\theta\hskip 0.81949pt0{\circ}}\hskip 1.99997ptR^{0{\circ}}_{\hskip 5.7361pt{\star\star}}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})=i\kappa(-\theta)\left(R^{01}_{\hskip 5.7361pt23}-R^{02}_{\hskip 5.7361pt13}+R^{03}_{\hskip 5.7361pt12}\right)({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})

and

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002ptR_{0}:=i\frac{\kappa(\theta)}{2}\epsilon^{\hskip 10.65283pt0\star}_{\theta{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.99997ptR^{{\circ\circ}}_{\hskip 5.7361pt0\star}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})=i\kappa(\theta)\left(R^{12}_{\hskip 5.7361pt03}-R^{02}_{\hskip 5.7361pt13}+R^{23}_{\hskip 5.7361pt01}\right)({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~}).

When the torsion is null, space-time curvature has additional symmetry.
Owing to the definition (
2.1.10
), the torsion-less condition yields

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle={{\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~}}^{a}_{\hskip 2.45836pt{\circ}}\wedge{\mathfrak{T}}^{\circ}_{\theta}={{\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~}}^{a}_{\hskip 2.45836pt{\circ}}{\wedge}d{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{\circ}+{{\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~}}^{a}_{\hskip 2.45836pt{\circ}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~}}^{{\circ}}_{\hskip 2.45836pt\star}\wedge{{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{~}}^{\star}={{\eta^{~}_{\theta}}}^{~}_{\circ\circ}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{R}_{~}^{a{\circ}}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}-d({{\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~}}^{a}_{\hskip 2.45836pt{\circ}}{\wedge}{\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{\circ}),

where
{{\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~}}^{a}_{\hskip 2.45836ptb}:={{\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~}}^{a{\circ}}{{\eta^{~}_{\theta}}}^{~}_{b{\circ}}
.
A total-derivative term does not contribute the equation of motion; thus, we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{T}}^{a}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{~})=\dot{0}

\displaystyle\implies{{\eta^{~}_{\theta}}}^{~}_{\circ\circ}\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{R}_{~}^{a{\circ}}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}=\dot{0},

(4.5.26)

yielding

\displaystyle\eta^{~}_{\theta a{\circ}}\eta^{~}_{\theta b{\circ}}\hskip 1.00006ptR^{{\circ\circ}}_{~~cd}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})

\displaystyle=\eta^{~}_{\theta c{\circ}}\eta^{~}_{\theta d{\circ}}\hskip 1.00006ptR^{{\circ\circ}}_{~~ab}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~}),

(4.5.27)

as the solution to the algebraic equation (
4.5.26
) for any values of
\theta
.
We denote (
4.5.27
) shortly as

\displaystyle R^{~}_{ab\hskip 1.63885ptcd}

\displaystyle=R^{~}_{cd\hskip 1.63885ptab}.

(4.5.28)

Symmetry (
4.5.28
) induces the relation

\displaystyle R^{0}=-R_{0}.

Consequently, one of
\mathfrak{R}^{\pm}
can yield the scalar curvature, like

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}[\mathfrak{R}^{+}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})\wedge{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{~})]

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}[\mathfrak{R}^{-}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})\wedge{\mathfrak{S}}^{-}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{~})]=-\frac{1}{2}R({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta};

(4.5.29)

thus, the Einstein–Hilbert Lagrangian can be written using one of the subspaces
{\mathbb{V}}^{\pm}(\Omega^{2})
.
When we choose SD curvature
\mathfrak{R}^{+}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})
to construct the Lagrangian, the gravitational Lagrangian form is represented as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{L}}_{\textsc{g}\hskip-0.40974pt\textsc{r}}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})

\displaystyle=-2\frac{\text{det}[{\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}}]^{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}{\hbar\kappa}{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}[\mathfrak{R}^{+}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{w}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}^{~})\wedge{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{~})],~~\textrm{with}~~{\mathfrak{T}}^{a}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta})=0.

The volume form can also be written using one of
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\pm}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{~})
such that:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3!}\mathfrak{v}

\displaystyle=-f(\theta)\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}\left[{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{~})\wedge{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{~})\right]=f(\theta)\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptO\hskip-0.81949pt}^{~}\left[{\mathfrak{S}}^{-}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{~})\wedge{\mathfrak{S}}^{-}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{e}}_{\theta}^{~})\right],

where

\displaystyle f(\theta)

\displaystyle:=\frac{i}{4}\left(3\kappa(-\theta)+\kappa(\theta)\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{crr}1,&\theta=&0\\
-i/2,&\theta=&\pm 1\end{array}\right..

4.5.2
Fermions

The fermion Lagrangian in the Lorentzian metric space is provided by (
3.2.15a
) and (
3.2.16a
).
We can extend them to the amphometric space by replacing the Lorentzian metric with the amphometric.
Correspondingly, a weak pair of the dual-spinor is extended to that of the dual-amphospinor as

\displaystyle\bm{\Xi}^{~}_{\textsc{w}}(\theta_{\nu},\theta_{e})

\displaystyle:=\bm{\xi}^{\nu}_{~}(\theta_{\nu})\otimes{\hat{\bm{\phi}}_{\textsc{w}}^{\textsc{u}}}+\bm{\xi}^{e}_{~}(\theta_{e})\otimes{\hat{\bm{\phi}}_{\textsc{w}}^{\textsc{d}}}\longrightarrow\begin{cases}{\bm{\xi}}^{\nu}_{~}\otimes{\hat{\bm{\phi}}_{\textsc{w}}^{\textsc{u}}}+{\bm{\xi}}^{e}_{~}\otimes{\hat{\bm{\phi}}_{\textsc{w}}^{\textsc{d}}}=\text{(\ref{VVXIenu1})},&{\theta_{\nu}=\theta_{e}=+1}\\
\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{\nu}_{~}\otimes{\hat{\bm{\phi}}_{\textsc{w}}^{\textsc{u}}}+\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{e}_{~}\otimes{\hat{\bm{\phi}}_{\textsc{w}}^{\textsc{d}}}=\text{(\ref{VVXIenu2})},&{\theta_{\nu}=\theta_{e}=-1}\end{cases};

(4.5.30)

thus, dotted and undotted weak dual-spinor spinors are unified.
We introduce the chiral-left fermion Lagrangian in the amphometric space as

\displaystyle{\cal L}^{\theta\!L}_{\textsc{f}_{\hskip-0.73616pt\textsc{m}}}(\bm{\Xi}^{~}_{\textsc{w}},\bm{\Xi}^{\dagger_{\hskip-0.73616pt\epsilon}}_{\textsc{w}}):=-\hskip 1.00006pt\left\langle{\bm{\Xi}}^{*}_{\textsc{w}}(\theta_{\nu},\theta_{e}),i{\not\!d}^{\hskip 1.63885pt{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}}_{\Theta}\hskip 1.00006pt{\bm{\Xi}}^{~}_{\textsc{w}}(\theta_{\nu},\theta_{e})\right\rangle\!,

(4.5.31a)

where

\displaystyle i{{\not\!d}}^{\hskip 1.63885pt{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}}_{\Theta}:=i{{\not\!d}}^{\hskip 1.63885pt{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}}_{\theta_{\nu}}{\hat{\bm{\phi}}_{\textsc{w}}^{\textsc{u}}}+i{{\not\!d}}^{\hskip 1.63885pt{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}}_{\theta_{e}}{\hat{\bm{\phi}}_{\textsc{w}}^{\textsc{d}}},~~\text{with}~~i{{\not\!d}}^{\hskip 1.63885pt{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}}_{\theta}:={\gamma}^{\circ}_{\theta}\left(\bm{1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\otimes\left(i\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{\circ}-\frac{1}{2}{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\hskip 1.99997pt\omega^{\hskip 4.91673pt{\star\star}}_{\theta{\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt{S}^{~}_{\theta{\star\star}}\right)+{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\hskip 1.99997pt\hskip 1.00006pt{\mathscr{A}}^{J}_{\theta\hskip 0.81949pt{\circ}}\right)\!.

(4.5.31b)

From (
4.5.31a
), the chiral-left fermion-Lagrangian is provided as

\displaystyle\text{(\ref{LfmenuQ2})}\xrightarrow{\theta_{\nu}=\theta_{e}=-1}{\cal L}^{L}_{\textsc{f}_{\hskip-0.73616pt\textsc{m}}}

\displaystyle=\text{(\ref{WspinEoML})}.

(4.5.32)

For the equation of motion (
3.2.18
) with the source term in the Lorentzian metric space, we implement the source term in the equation of motion (
4.5.2
) as

\displaystyle\hat{d}_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\theta}}\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}=c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.99997pt{\bm{\Xi}}^{\dagger_{\hskip-0.73616pt\epsilon}}(\theta_{\nu},\theta_{e})\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma^{\circ}_{\textsc{s}\theta}\hskip 1.00006pt{\bm{\Xi}}(\theta_{\nu},\theta_{e})\hskip 1.00006pt\mathfrak{V}_{\theta\hskip 0.81949pt{\circ}}\xrightarrow{\theta_{\nu}=\theta_{e}=-1}\text{(\ref{YMGBEoMDWS})}.

(4.5.33)

The chiral-right fermion-Lagrangian in the amphhometric space consists of the undotted weyl spinor as

\displaystyle{\cal L}^{\theta\!R}_{\textsc{f}_{\hskip-0.73616pt\textsc{m}}}(\bm{\xi}^{e}_{~},\bm{\xi}_{~}^{e\dagger_{\hskip-0.73616pt\epsilon}})=\langle\bm{\xi}^{e*}_{~}(\theta),i{{\not\!d}^{\hskip 0.81949pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}_{\theta}}\hskip 1.00006pt\bm{\xi}^{e}_{~}(\theta)\rangle\xrightarrow{\theta=1}{\cal L}^{R}_{\textsc{f}_{\hskip-0.73616pt\textsc{m}}}=\text{(\ref{WspinLagR})},~\text{with}~i{{\not\!d}}^{\hskip 1.63885pt{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}_{\theta}:={\gamma}^{\circ}_{\theta}\left(i\hskip 1.00006pt\partial_{\circ}-\frac{1}{2}{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\hskip 1.99997pt\omega^{\hskip 4.91673pt{\star\star}}_{\theta{\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt{S}^{~}_{\theta{\star\star}}\right)\!.

(4.5.34)

We draw a structure of the dual-spinors in the whole
{\cal W}
space schematically as

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccrclcc}\theta=-1&{\longleftrightarrow}&&\theta=0&&{\longleftrightarrow}&\theta=+1\\
\downarrow&&\hskip 25.00003pt\rotatebox[origin={d}]{-60.0}{$\downarrow$}&&\rotatebox[origin={d}]{60.0}{$\downarrow$}&&\downarrow\\
(\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{\nu}_{~}&\otimes&{\hat{\bm{\phi}}_{\textsc{w}}^{\textsc{u}}})&&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\hat{\bm{\phi}}_{\textsc{w}}^{{}^{\prime}\textsc{u}}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\otimes}&{{\bm{\xi}}^{\nu}_{~})}\\
&+&&&&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}+}\\
(\dot{\bm{\xi}}^{e}_{~}&\otimes&{\hat{\bm{\phi}}_{\textsc{w}}^{\textsc{d}}})&&({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{\bm{\phi}}_{\textsc{w}}^{{}^{\prime}\textsc{d}}}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\otimes}&\bm{\xi}^{e}_{~})\\
\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$=$}&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\hskip 5.0pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}}&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}=}}&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}\\
\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C})}&\hskip 3.99994pt\dot{\bm{\Xi}}^{~}_{\textsc{w}}&({\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}_{L}^{~}(2)&\oplus&{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}_{R}^{~}(2))&\hskip 3.99994pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bm{\Xi}}^{~}_{\textsc{w}}}&{{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C})}\\
&&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$=$}\\
&&&{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{o}(4)\\
\end{array}.

The table draws the right-handed Higgs spinor and chiral-right spinors as symmetric to chiral-left ones.
In reality, the right-handed Higgs spinor shown in red is not observed.
Though the right-handed neutrino is not directory observed, a neutrino oscillation phenomenon indirectly proves its existence; thus, we draw it in black.
In this understanding, the electron–spinor and the neutrino-spinor themselves are not
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
symmetric; the
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
-group acts on them though the Higgs-spinor in the dual-spinor as shown in (
3.1.33
).

Suppose a single source spinor exists in the Lorentzian metric space as a classical current of the
SU(2)
charge.
Singular points of the connection form given in (
4.5.14
) configure the trajectory of the classical particle in the Lorentzian metric space as
\bm{j}(t):=c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\hskip 1.99997pt\bm{r}(t)
, which is nothing other than the classical weak current.
Therefore, the equation of motion (
3.2.18
) with the source term is reduced to the vacuum equation (
4.5.8
) in
\overline{{{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}
.
Consequently, the vacuum solutions of the Yang–Mills gauge boson in the Lorentzian metric space are homotopically equivalent to the SD curvature through the amphometric. However, the solution is not SD at
\theta=1
.
The current author precisely discuss the existence of the solution of the Poisson equation in
{\cal W}^{+}{\hskip-1.00006pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt}{\cal W}^{+}_{1}
in Ref.
[
28
]
.

4.5.3
Higgs spinor

The Higgs spinor Lagrangian in the Lorentzian metric space given as (
3.2.19a
)=(
3.2.24b
) is cast into the amphometric one by replacing metric tensors and spinors as

\displaystyle\text{(\ref{LHiggs1})}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{L}}^{~}_{\textsc{h}}({\bm{\xi}}(\theta),{\bm{\xi}}^{\dagger_{\hskip-0.73616pt\theta}}_{~}(\theta))

\displaystyle:={\mathfrak{L}}^{kin}_{\textsc{h}}({\bm{\xi}}(\theta),{\bm{\xi}}^{\dagger_{\hskip-0.73616pt\theta}}_{~}(\theta))-V\left({\bm{\xi}}(\theta),{\bm{\xi}}^{\dagger_{\hskip-0.73616pt\theta}}_{~}(\theta)\right)\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta}

(4.5.44a)

with

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{L}}^{kin}_{\textsc{h}}({\bm{\xi}}(\theta),{\bm{\xi}}^{\dagger_{\hskip-0.73616pt\theta}}_{~}(\theta)):=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}(i{{\not\!d}}_{\theta}){{\circ}}(i{{\not\!d}}_{\theta})\langle{\bm{\xi}}(\theta),{\bm{\xi}}(\theta)\rangle\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta}.

(4.5.44b)

When we set the amphometric parameter at
\theta=0
, the Higgs Lagrangian (
4.5.44a
) reproduces (
3.2.19a
).
In
section
3.2.3
, we discussed operator
\widehat{\not\!{\hskip-3.00003pt\not\!\mathfrak{F}}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}
based on Clifford algebra (
2.4.1a
), and the results are independent of their component representations.
Therefore, amphoClifford algebra (
4.4.1
) ensures that a simple replacement of the metric tensor provides the Higgs Lagrangian in the amphometric space.
According to this change, we obtain the equation of motion as

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\mathscr{I}_{\textsc{h}}({\bm{\xi}}(\theta),{\bm{\xi}}^{\dagger_{\hskip-0.73616pt\theta}}_{~}(\theta))}{\delta{\bm{\xi}}^{\dagger_{\hskip-0.73616pt\theta}}_{~}(\theta)}=0\implies\left(\Delta^{~}_{\theta}+i{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\not\!{\hskip-3.00003pt\not\!\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\theta}+\frac{\delta V({\bm{\xi}}(\theta),{\bm{\xi}}^{\dagger_{\hskip-0.73616pt\theta}}_{~}(\theta))}{\delta{\bm{\xi}}^{\dagger_{\hskip-0.73616pt\theta}}_{~}(\theta)}\right){\bm{\xi}}(\theta)=0,~~\text{where}~~\not\!{\hskip-3.00003pt\not\!\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\theta}:=\not\!{\hskip-3.00003pt\not\!\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\big|^{~}_{\gamma^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.44165pt\textsc{l}}\rightarrow\gamma^{~}_{\theta}}.

(4.5.45)

As mentioned in
section
3.1.3
, the Higgs spinor does not interact directly with the spin connection since the
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
group is not the space-time symmetry but the internal symmetry.
When we identify the Higgs spinor with the amphospinor
{\bm{\xi}}(\theta)
at
\theta=0
, it belongs to the
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2)
group owing to the space-time
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptO\hskip-1.00006pt}(4)
symmetry as in (
4.5.2
) and may directly couple with the spin connection.
The Higgs Lagrangian is provided through the
Spin(4)\otimes{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}(2)
invariant Dirac operator in Euclidean space-time.
In this case, the Lichnerowicz formula in curved space-time is provided as

\displaystyle\text{(\ref{Lf0})}\rightarrow-\left(i{{\not\!d}}_{\theta}^{~}\right)^{2}=\Delta_{\theta}+\widehat{\not\!{\hskip-3.00003pt\not\!\mathfrak{F}}}^{~}_{\theta},~~\text{where}~~\widehat{\not\!{\hskip-3.00003pt\not\!\mathfrak{F}}}^{~}_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006pt:=\iota_{\gamma_{\theta}}\iota_{\gamma_{\theta}}(d^{~}_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}{\wedge}\hskip 1.00006ptd^{~}_{\hskip-1.63885pts\hskip-0.81949ptg}).

(4.5.46)

The twisting curvature of the Clifford module is provided owing to (
3.1.15
) as

\displaystyle\widehat{\not\!{\hskip-3.00003pt\not\!\mathfrak{F}}}^{~}_{\theta}

\displaystyle=\iota_{\gamma_{\theta}}\iota_{\gamma_{\theta}}\left(d-i\hskip 1.00006pt{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}({{\mathfrak{A}}}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\otimes\bm{1}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt})-i\hskip 1.00006pt{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}(\bm{1}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\otimes{{{\mathfrak{A}}}}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt})\right)^{2\wedge},

\displaystyle=-i\hskip 1.99997pt\iota_{\gamma_{\theta}}\iota_{\gamma_{\theta}}\left({c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}(d{{\mathfrak{A}}}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}{{\mathfrak{A}}}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{A}}}_{{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}})+{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}}(d{{\mathfrak{A}}}_{{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}-i\hskip 1.00006pt{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}}{{\mathfrak{A}}}_{{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}\wedge{{\mathfrak{A}}}_{{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}})\right)

(4.5.47)

The connection in the spinor bundle in the amphometric space is provided as

\displaystyle\text{(\ref{connectSp})}\rightarrow\not\!{\mathfrak{A}}_{{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}(\iota_{\gamma_{\theta}}{\mathfrak{w}}_{\theta}^{\hskip 2.45836pt{\circ\circ}}){S}_{{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}\hskip 0.81949pt{\circ\circ}}\big|^{~}_{\gamma_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.44165pt\textsc{l}}\rightarrow\gamma_{\theta}}=-\frac{1}{8}\omega_{\diamond}^{\hskip 2.45836pt{\circ}\star}\gamma_{\theta}^{\diamond}\hskip 1.00006pt{\eta^{~}_{\theta}}_{\circ\circ}{\eta^{~}_{\theta}}_{\star\star}\left[\gamma^{\circ}_{\theta},\gamma^{\star}_{\theta}\right]=-\frac{1}{4}\omega_{\diamond}^{\hskip 2.45836pt{\circ}\star}\gamma_{\theta}^{\diamond}\hskip 1.00006pt{\eta^{~}_{\theta}}_{\circ\circ}{\eta^{~}_{\theta}}_{\star\star}\gamma^{\circ}_{\theta}\gamma^{\star}_{\theta}.

As a result, we obtain the twisting spin-curvature of the Clifford algebra as

\displaystyle\widehat{\not\!{\hskip-3.00003pt\not\!\mathfrak{R}}}

\displaystyle=-{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}}\iota_{\gamma_{\theta}}\iota_{\gamma_{\theta}}\not\!{\hskip-3.00003pt\not\!\mathfrak{R}}=-\frac{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}}{8}R^{\hskip 2.45836pt{\circ\circ}}_{\theta\hskip 5.7361pt{\star\star}}\hskip 1.99997pt\gamma_{\theta{\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma_{\theta{\circ}}(\eta_{\theta}^{\hskip 2.45836pt\star\diamond}\gamma_{\theta\diamond})(\eta_{\theta}^{\hskip 2.45836pt\star\diamond}\gamma_{\theta\diamond}).

(4.5.48)

Hereafter, we omit subscript
\theta
on Riemann curvature
R_{\hskip 2.45836pt\cdot\cdot}^{\cdot\cdot}
to avoid too many subscripts.
We first calculate the twisting spin-curvature of the Clifford algebra in the Lorentzian metric space.
The Bianchi identity (
2.1.15
) leads to an identity among space-time curvature components as a=b.

\displaystyle R^{a}_{\hskip 4.09723ptb\hskip 1.63885ptcd}+R^{a}_{\hskip 4.09723ptc\hskip 1.63885ptdb}+R^{a}_{\hskip 4.09723ptd\hskip 1.63885ptbc}=0{\quad\text{for}\quad}R^{a}_{\hskip 4.09723ptb\hskip 1.63885ptcd}:={{\eta^{~}_{\textsc{l}}}}_{\hskip 0.81949ptb{\circ}}R^{a{\circ}}_{\hskip 6.5556ptcd},

(4.5.49)

yielding

\displaystyle\sum_{c\neq b\neq d}R^{a}_{\hskip 4.09723ptb\hskip 1.63885ptcd}\hskip 1.99997pt\gamma_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{b}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{c}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{d}=0.

(4.5.50)

Thus, we obtain the twisting spin-curvature of the Clifford algebra as

\displaystyle R^{~}_{ab\hskip 1.63885ptcd}\hskip 1.99997pt\gamma_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{a}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{b}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{c}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{d}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(\ref{RBianchicoe})}}{=}{{\eta^{~}_{\textsc{l}}}}^{\circ\circ}\left(-R^{~}_{{\circ}b\hskip 1.63885pta{\circ}}+R^{~}_{{\circ}b\hskip 1.63885pt{\circ}a}\right)\gamma_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{a}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{b}=2{{\eta^{~}_{\textsc{l}}}}^{\circ\circ}\hskip 1.00006ptR^{~}_{b{\circ}\hskip 1.63885pta{\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{a}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{b},

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(\ref{RabcdeqRcdab2})}}{=}2R^{{\circ}\star}_{\hskip 5.7361pt{\circ}\star}\implies\text{(\ref{RssQ})}\xrightarrow{\theta=1}-{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\frac{R^{~}_{\theta}}{4}.

(4.5.51)

In the above calculations, we have utilised the Clifford algebra (
2.4.1a
) independent from its representation and a metric tensor.
The final result (
4.5.51
) is given by the scalar curvature after contractions between upper and lower indices in their intrinsic position without metric tensors; thus, (
4.5.51
) is the metric independent.
Consequently, we obtain the Lichnerowicz formula in the amphometric space as

\displaystyle\text{(\ref{LichnerowiczQ})}\rightarrow-\left(i{{\not\!d}}_{\theta}^{~}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\Delta(\theta)-i\hskip 1.00006pt{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\not\!{\hskip-3.00003pt\not\!\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\theta}-{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}}\frac{R^{~}_{\theta}}{4}.

(4.5.52)

The equation of motion for the Higgs spinor in the amphometric space is provided as

(
3.2.24b
)

\displaystyle\implies\left(\Delta^{~}_{\theta}+i{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}\not\!{\hskip-3.00003pt\not\!\mathfrak{F}}^{~}_{\theta}+{{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}}\frac{R^{~}_{\theta}}{4}+\frac{\delta V({\bm{\xi}}(\theta),{\bm{\xi}}^{\dagger_{\hskip-0.73616pt\theta}}_{~}(\theta))}{\delta{\bm{\xi}}^{\dagger_{\hskip-0.73616pt\theta}}_{~}(\theta)}\right){\bm{\xi}}(\theta)=0.

(4.5.53)

4.5.4
Fermion mass term

The fermion mass term in the Lorentzian metric space is provided owing to the interaction between the weak dual-spinor and the Higgs spinor with the SSB as shown in
section
3.2.4
.
It utilised the
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
-invariant bilinear form between dotted and undotted Weyl spinors.
Since the dual-spinor unifies dotted and undotted spinors in the amphometric space, the fermion mass term consists of only the amphospinor, setting appropriate
\theta
-values.

We replace a dual-spinor in a Higgs-fermion interaction term (
3.2.28
) by the dual-amphospinor (
4.5.30
) as

\displaystyle\{\text{(\ref{XwPhiw})}\leftarrow\text{(\ref{VVXIenu2Ampho})}\}

\displaystyle\implies\langle{\bm{\Xi}}^{~}_{\textsc{w}}(\theta_{\nu},\theta_{e}),\bm{\xi}^{\textsc{h}}_{~}(\theta_{\textsc{h}}=0)\rangle,

(4.5.54)

where we note

\displaystyle\bm{\xi}^{\textsc{h}}_{~}(0)={\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{w}}^{~}}.

We obtain the fermion mass term using (
4.5.54
