\displaystyle{\cal L}^{\theta m_{e}}_{\textsc{f}_{\hskip-0.73616pt\textsc{m}}}=\lambda\langle\langle{\bm{\Xi}}^{*}_{\textsc{w}}(-\theta_{\nu},-\theta_{e}),{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{w}}^{~}}\rangle,{\bm{\Xi}}^{~}_{\textsc{w}}(\theta_{\nu},\theta_{e})\rangle=

\displaystyle-\lambda\hskip 1.00006pt\phi^{~}_{\textsc{d}}\left(\langle\bm{\xi}^{e*\hskip 0.81949pt}_{\textsc{u}}(-\theta_{e}\hskip 1.00006pt),\bm{\xi}^{e}_{\textsc{d}}(\theta_{e}\hskip 1.00006pt)\rangle-\langle\bm{\xi}^{e*\hskip 0.81949pt}_{\textsc{d}}(-\theta_{e}\hskip 1.00006pt),\bm{\xi}^{e}_{\textsc{u}}(\theta_{e}\hskip 1.00006pt)\rangle\right)

\displaystyle-\lambda\hskip 1.00006pt\phi^{~}_{\textsc{u}}\left(\langle\bm{\xi}^{\nu*}_{\textsc{u}}(-\theta_{\nu}),\bm{\xi}^{\nu}_{\textsc{d}}(\theta_{\nu})\rangle-\langle\bm{\xi}^{\nu*}_{\textsc{d}}(-\theta_{\nu}),\bm{\xi}^{\nu}_{\textsc{u}}(\theta_{\nu})\rangle\right)\!,

(4.5.55)

We obtain the fermion mass term (
3.2.28
) in the Lorentzian metric space by setting
\theta
-values as

\displaystyle\text{(\ref{meQ2})}\big|^{~}_{\theta^{\nu}=\theta^{e}=1}\implies\text{(\ref{me2})}.

(4.5.56)

5
Graviweak correspondence

In this report, we have constructed the Yang–Mills theory including the gravitational force as the local
SO(1,3)
gauge interaction, namely the Yang–Mills–Utiyama theory, in which the gauge theory of the space-time symmetry is treated similarly to that of the internal gauge symmetry.
We have extended the YMU theory in the Lorentzian metric space further into the amphometric space, which smoothly connects the Lorentzian metric to the Euclidean one.
The amphometric method has connected two metric spaces and allowed us to import mathematical achievements established in Euclidean space into Lorentzian one.
This report showed two examples: a role of the SD (ASD) curvature and an instanton solution in the Lorentzian metric space.

Furthermore, the amphometric has connected two symmetries
SU\!(2)
and
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
.
We have schematically drawn several gauge and spinor structures in the amphometric space, revealing the novel relation between weak and gravitational interactions.
This section discusses the relationship between internal and space-time symmetries and examines their correspondence.

5.1
Gauge bosons

First, we overview a chiral structure of the bosonic part of the YMU theory.
A space of two-form objects in a four-dimensional space has the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading structure owing to the Hodge-dual operator.
The eigenvectors of the Hodge-dual operator are named SD (eigenvalue
=+1
) and ASD (eigenvlue
=-1
).
We assigned the chiral-right to the SD and the chiral-left to the ASD curvature.
Both the Yang–Mills gauge-boson Lagrangian and the second Chern index consist of a squared curvature.
The SD or the ASD curvature gives the solutions of the equation of motion in the Euclidean metric space; this is proven by comparing it with the second Chern index.
Whether the curvature is SD or ASD depends on the orientation of the manifold.
This is not true in the Lorentzian metric space.
Even though the solutions in the Lorentzian metric space are not SD nor ASD, they are homotopical equivalent to SD or ASD curvature in the Euclidean metric space in the amphometric space.

On the other hand, the Einstein–Hilbert gravitational Lagrangian,
{\mathfrak{L}}_{\hskip-0.81949ptE\hskip-0.81949ptH}=\mathfrak{R}\wedge{\mathfrak{S}}
, consists of the curvature two-form and the surface two-form.
Thus, the extremal of the Lagrangian is not bounded by the second Chern index, consisting of the curvature squared.
However, when we construct the gravitational Lagrangian in the co-Poincaré bundle, the Lagrangian consists of the co-Poincaré curvature squared; the Lagrangian is bounded owing to the second Chern-index as a functional of the co-Poincaré curvature.
In reality, we can construct the Einstein–Hilbert gravitational Lagrangian in half the space-time degree of freedom, the SD or the ASD two-form objects.
The chirality is shared between Yang–Mills and space-time curvatures; in this study, we fixed it as SD (chiral-right).

The structure groups of the gauge and spinor bundles are, respectively,
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
and
SO(1,3)
; they have the Lie-algebra homomorphism as (
2.3.1a
) and (
2.3.1b
).
The space-time curvature belongs to an adjoint representation of
{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C})
, which is a half of
{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{o}(1,3)
owing to (
2.3.1b
).
We prepare the SD curvature with the causal subgroup
\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{\theta}{\in}\Omega^{2}_{\textsc{sd}}(T^{*}{\cal W}^{+}_{\theta}){\oplus}Ad\!\left({C\hskip-1.00006ptaus}_{\theta}(2)\right)
in the amphometric space
{\cal W}^{+}_{\theta}
; we obtain the following diagram:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cclcl}\mathfrak{R}^{+}_{\textsc{l}}&{\in}&\Omega^{2}_{\textsc{sd}}({{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})&{\oplus}&Ad\!\left({{\mathfrak{C}}{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{u}}{\mathfrak{s}}}_{\theta=1}(2)={\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{l}(2,\mathbb{C})\right)\\
\uparrow&&\theta\rightarrow 1\uparrow&&\hskip 16.99998pt\uparrow\theta\rightarrow 1\\
\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{\theta}&{\in}&\Omega^{2}_{\textsc{sd}}(T^{*}{\cal W}^{+}_{\theta})&{\oplus}&Ad\!\left({{\mathfrak{C}}{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{u}}{\mathfrak{s}}}_{\theta}(2)\right)\\
\downarrow&&\hskip-1.99997pt\theta\rightarrow 1\downarrow&&\hskip 16.99998pt\downarrow\theta\rightarrow 0\\
\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}&{\in}&\Omega^{2}_{\textsc{sd}}({{T^{*}\hskip-1.99997pt{\cal M}}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}})&{\oplus}&Ad\!\left({{\mathfrak{C}}{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{u}}{\mathfrak{s}}}_{\theta=0}(2)={\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}^{~}_{\!R}(2)\right)\end{array},

where we assume the existence of curvatures.
In the diagram, the Yang–Mills gauge curvature and the space-time curvature have a common origin in the amphometric space; However, both curvatures are in the Lorentzian metric space setting
\theta=1
, the structure group split into
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
and
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
setting
\theta=0
and
\theta=1
, respectively.
This is one consequence of the graviweak correspondence.

5.2
Higgs and Weyl spinor interaction

The fermion Lagrangian consists of the weak dual-spinor in the Lorentz metric space as (
3.2.16b
), and it is extended to the weak dual-amphospinor in the amphometric space schematically shown in (
4.5.2
).
When we allow mixing different
\theta
values in the dual-amphospinor as (
5.1
), the dual-amphospinor
\bm{\Xi}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
has the diagram in the amphometric space under the graviweak point of view like

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccccccccc}\bm{\Xi}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})&=&\bm{\xi}(\theta_{1})&\otimes&&\bm{\xi}(\theta_{2})\\
\downarrow&&\downarrow&&&\downarrow\\
\bm{\Xi}(-1,0)&=&\dot{\bm{\xi}}&\otimes&({\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{\textsc{u}}}&\oplus&{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{\textsc{d}}})\\
&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}&&\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\in$}\\
&&\Gamma^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{w}}\left({{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}},{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{l}}_{\overline{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}}\right)&\otimes&\left(\Gamma^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{w}}\left({{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}},{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{eu}}_{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\right)\right.&\oplus&\left.\Gamma^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{w}}\left({{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}},{\mathbb{V}}^{\textsc{ed}}_{{1\hskip-0.81949pt/\hskip-0.40974pt2}}\right)\right)\\
\end{array}\!,

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\{P^{-}_{\textsc{se}},P^{+}_{\textsc{se}}\}}\hskip 90.00014pt\left\{\dot{\bm{\Xi}}^{\textsc{u}}_{\textsc{w}}=\dot{\bm{\xi}}\oplus{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{\textsc{u}}},\dot{\bm{\Xi}}^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{w}}=\dot{\bm{\xi}}\oplus{\bm{\phi}_{\textsc{h}}^{\textsc{d}}}\right\}.

(5.2.1f)

It suggests the
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
symmetry of the Higgs spinor originates at the space-time
{\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}\!(2)
symmetry.

When we accept the graviweak correspondence, we can understand a fermion mass is induced by the Yukawa coupling of
{S\hskip-1.00006ptL(2,\mathbb{C})}
Weyl-spinor and
SU^{~}_{\hskip-1.22911pt\textsc{w}}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)={\hskip-1.00006ptS\hskip-1.00006ptU\hskip-1.00006pt}_{\hskip-1.22911ptL}\hskip-1.00006pt(2)
Higgs spinor.
From the quantum field theoretic view, We reconsider an electron mass term here.
We respect the Higgs field as the space-time
SU(2)
spinor and utilise the first differential formalism for a gravitational interaction term, the same as the Weyl spinor;

\displaystyle(\ref{LfmenuQ3})\implies-\frac{1}{2}{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\hskip 1.00006pt\omega^{\hskip 2.45836pt{\star\star}}_{\circ}\hskip 1.00006pt({\bm{\phi}}_{\textsc{h}}^{\dagger}{\gamma}^{\circ}_{~}{S}^{~}_{{\star\star}}\hskip 1.00006pt{\bm{\phi}}_{\textsc{h}}),

(5.2.2)

where we omit the subscript
l
on the spin connection and the generator for simplicity.
We consider a configuration in which an electron induces a gravitational field of the Schwarzschild solution around it, and the Higgs spinor interacts gravitationally with the curved space-time in the electron centre-of-mass system.
An electron has an electric charge and intrinsic angular momentum (a spin); thus, the Kerr– Newman metric may be a solution to the Einstein equation for an electron.
We ignore a contribution of the electric charge since it is suppressed by
({c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}/c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt})^{2}\approx 1/137
and also ignore a contribution from spin
\hbar/2
since it gives a more minor contribution than the mass does.

The Schwarzschild solution with a polar-coordinate
x^{a}=(t,r,\vartheta,\varphi)
is

\displaystyle ds_{\text{\hskip-0.79727ptSchw}}^{2}

\displaystyle=f^{2}_{\text{\hskip-0.79727ptSchw}}(r)dt^{2}-f^{-2}_{\text{\hskip-0.79727ptSchw}}(r)dr^{2}-r^{2}\left(d\vartheta^{2}+\sin^{2}{\varphi}~d\varphi^{2}\right),\quad\text{where}\quad f^{2}_{\text{\hskip-0.79727ptSchw}}(r)=1-16\pi\kappa^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}m_{e}/r

(5.2.3)

and
m_{e}
is the electron mass.
The vierbein form can be extracted from (
5.2.3
) as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{e}}^{a}_{\text{\hskip-0.79727ptSchw}}

\displaystyle=\left(f_{\text{\hskip-0.79727ptSchw}}dt,f_{\text{\hskip-0.79727ptSchw}}^{-1}dr,r~d\vartheta,r\sin{\vartheta}~d\varphi\right).

(5.2.4)

From the above solutions and the torsion-less condition, a unique solution of the spin form can be obtained
[
24
,
29
]
:

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{w}}_{\text{\hskip-0.79727ptSchw}}^{~}=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&-8\pi\kappa^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}m_{e}/r^{2}~dt&0&0\\
&0&f_{\text{\hskip-0.79727ptSchw}}d\vartheta&f_{\text{\hskip-0.79727ptSchw}}\sin{\vartheta}~d\varphi\\
&&0&\cos{\vartheta}~d\varphi\\
&&&0\\
\end{array}\right).

In the region
r\ll 1
, component
\omega_{t}^{\hskip 2.45836pttr}=-m_{e}/r^{2}
dominates over other components.
The gravitational coupling between the Higgs-spinor and an electron is provided as

(
5.2.2
)

\displaystyle\approx-\frac{1}{2}{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\sum_{p^{~}_{i}\in\{t,r,\vartheta,\varphi\}}\eta_{p^{~}_{1}p^{~}_{2}}^{~}\hskip 1.00006pt\eta_{p^{~}_{3}p^{~}_{4}}^{~}\hskip 1.00006pt\omega_{t}^{\hskip 2.45836ptp^{~}_{1}p^{~}_{3}}\hskip 1.00006pt\gamma^{t}_{~}\hskip 1.00006ptS_{~}^{p^{~}_{2}p^{~}_{4}},

(5.2.9)

where
\bm{\gamma}_{~}^{p}
is the Clifford algebra with the polar coordinate as

\displaystyle\gamma_{~}^{t}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{0},

\displaystyle\gamma_{~}^{r}

\displaystyle=\sin{\vartheta}\cos{\varphi}\hskip 1.99997pt\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{1}\hskip 1.99997pt+\sin{\vartheta}\sin{\varphi}\hskip 1.99997pt\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{2}+\cos{\vartheta}\hskip 1.99997pt\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{3},

\displaystyle\gamma_{~}^{\vartheta}

\displaystyle=\cos{\vartheta}\cos{\varphi}\hskip 1.99997pt\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{1}+\cos{\vartheta}\sin{\varphi}\hskip 1.99997pt\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{2}+\sin{\vartheta}\hskip 1.99997pt\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{3},

\displaystyle\gamma_{~}^{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\hskip 84.00006pt-\sin{\varphi}\hskip 1.99997pt\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{2}+\cos{\varphi}\hskip 1.99997pt\gamma_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}^{3},

\displaystyle\implies\quad\eta^{~}_{tt}

\displaystyle=1,\quad\eta^{~}_{rr}=\eta^{~}_{\vartheta\vartheta}=\eta^{~}_{\varphi\varphi}=-1,\quad\text{otherwise}\quad\eta^{~}_{ab}=0,

yielding a Clifford algebra as

\displaystyle\text{(\ref{ClQ})}\implies

\displaystyle\{\gamma_{~}^{p^{~}_{1}},\gamma_{~}^{p^{~}_{2}}\}=2\hskip 1.00006pt\eta_{\textsc{l}}^{p^{~}_{1}p^{~}_{2}}\bm{1}_{\hskip-0.81949pts\hskip-0.81949ptp\hskip 0.81949pt}\quad\text{for}\quad p^{~}_{i}\in\{t,r,\vartheta,\varphi\}.

The direct calculations provide the coupling as

\displaystyle\text{(\ref{cWgS2})}=4\pi{i}\hskip 1.00006pt{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptg\hskip-0.81949ptr}}\hskip 1.00006pt\frac{\kappa^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}m_{e}}{r^{2}}\left\langle{\bm{\phi}}_{\textsc{h}},\gamma^{1}_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{l}}{\bm{\phi}}_{\textsc{h}}\right\rangle.

(5.2.10)

We note that
m_{e}
is an electron’s gravitational mass observed by the asymptotic observer appearing in the Schwarzschild solution.
Though we may obtain a quantum field theoretic coupling constant after appropriate averaging over
r
and a renormalisation, we can expect that it is proportional to an electron’s gravitational mass.
This coupling appears as the Yukawa coupling between an electron and the Higgs boson and provides a pole in the electron propagator, the QFTheoretic mass.
We can rephrase Einstein’s equivalence principle in the QFT’c context as “
The pole in the fermion propagator is equivalent to the gravitational mass
”.
The graviweak correspondence exemplifies the equivalent principle realised in the QFT’c context.

Appendix A
Induced representation

Subgroups of
\bm{{S\hskip-1.15005ptL}(2)}
:

When a simple representation
\bm{g}
of a group
G
is given, we can construct new representations induced from the original one, namely the induced representation.
E.g.,
G=S\hskip-1.00006ptL\!\left(2,\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}\right)
has following subgroups:

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}SO(2)&(\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R})\\
SU(2)&(\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C})\end{cases},

(A.1a)

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle:=\left\{\left.t(a)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cl}a&0\\
0&a^{-1}\end{array}\right)\right|a\in\mathbb{F}\hskip-1.00006pt\setminus\hskip-1.99997pt\{0\}\right\},

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle:=\left\{\left.a(\varphi)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{\varphi}&0\\
0&e^{-\varphi}\end{array}\right)\right|\varphi\in\mathbb{R}\right\},

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle:=\left\{\left.\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&b\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\right|b\in\mathbb{F}\right\},

\displaystyle B:=TN^{t}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left.\left(\begin{array}[]{cl}a&0\\
b&a^{-1}\end{array}\right)\right|a\in\mathbb{F}\hskip-1.00006pt\setminus\hskip-1.99997pt\{0\},b\in\mathbb{F}\right\},

\displaystyle M:=K{\cap}T

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\hskip 20.00003pt\pm\bm{1}_{2}&(\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R})\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{i\varphi}&0\\
0&e^{-i\varphi}\end{array}\right)&(\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C})\end{cases}.

(A.1j)

They yield decomposition

\displaystyle T=M\hskip-1.00006ptA,~~G=KTN=K\hskip-1.00006ptAN,~~\text{and}~~G=KTN^{t}=K\hskip-1.00006ptAN^{t}.

(A.2a)

Especially, the decomposition

\displaystyle G=K\hskip-1.00006ptAN=K\hskip-1.00006ptAN^{t}

(A.2b)

is known as the
Iwasawa decomposition
.

For the Euclidean vector
\hat{\bm{v}}^{~}_{\textsc{e}}
in the spinor representation (
2.6.1
), two subgroups
K=SU(2)
in (
A.1a
) and
M
in (
A.1j
) are introduced as

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle:=\left\{\left.k(\vartheta)=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}\cos{\left(\vartheta/2\right)}&i\sin{\left(\vartheta/2\right)}\\
i\sin{\left(\vartheta/2\right)}&\cos{\left(\vartheta/2\right)}\end{array}\right)\right|0\leq\vartheta<4\pi\right\},

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle:=\left\{\left.m(\varphi)=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}e^{i\varphi/2}&0\\
0&e^{-i\varphi/2}\end{array}\right)\right|0\leq\varphi<4\pi\right\}.

From these subgroups, we obtain an induced (unitary) representation as

\displaystyle m(\varphi)k(\vartheta)m(\psi)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\hskip 5.0pt\cos{\left(\vartheta/2\right)}e^{i(\varphi+\psi)/2}&i\sin{\left(\vartheta/2\right)}e^{i(\varphi-\psi)/2}\\
i\sin{\left(\vartheta/2\right)}e^{-i(\varphi-\psi)/2}&\hskip 3.99994pt\cos{\left(\vartheta/2\right)}e^{-i(\varphi+\psi)/2}\end{array}\right),

with

\displaystyle 0\leq\psi<4\pi,~~~0\leq\vartheta<\pi,~~\text{and}~~0\leq\varphi<2\pi,

(A.4c)

where
(\psi,\vartheta,\varphi)
is called the Euler angle.

(
A.4
) provides the representation of dimension-2 for the unit Euclidean vector
\hat{\bm{v}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}
, yielding the representation in dimension-4 as

\displaystyle(\ref{generalrepSU2})=\hat{\bm{v}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}\implies\hat{\bm{v}}^{~}_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}=\Sigma^{-1}_{\textsc{e}}\hat{\bm{v}}^{~}_{\textsc{s}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{r}\cos{\left(\vartheta/2\right)}\cos{\left(\left(\varphi+\psi\right)/2\right)}\\
\sin{\left(\vartheta/2\right)}\cos{\left(\left(\varphi-\psi\right)/2\right)}\\
-\sin{\left(\vartheta/2\right)}\sin{\left(\left(\varphi-\psi\right)/2\right)}\\
\cos{\left(\vartheta/2\right)}\sin{\left(\left(\varphi+\psi\right)/2\right)}\end{array}\right),

yielding a normalized (
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{u}}(2)\hskip-1.99997pt=\hskip-1.99997pt{\mathfrak{s}}\mathfrak{o}(4)
)-invariant measure as

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{V}}^{0}_{\textsc{e}}=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\sin{\hskip-1.00006pt\vartheta}\hskip 1.00006pt\sin{\varphi}\hskip 1.99997ptd{\vartheta}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\varphi}\hskip 1.00006ptd\psi.

(A.9)

We have another representation of
\hat{\bm{v}}^{~}_{\textsc{e}}
using the four-dimensional polar coordinate as

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{v}}^{~}_{\textsc{v}\hskip-0.49164pt\textsc{e}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\cos{\vartheta}\\
\sin{\vartheta}\cos{\varphi}\\
\sin{\vartheta}\sin{\varphi}\cos{\psi}\\
\sin{\vartheta}\sin{\varphi}\sin{\psi}\end{array}\right)~~\text{with}~~0\leq\psi\leq 2\pi,~~0\leq\vartheta<\pi,~~\text{and}~~0\leq\varphi<\pi,

yielding the normalized invariant measure as

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{V}}^{0}_{\textsc{e}}=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\sin^{2}{\hskip-1.99997pt\vartheta}\hskip 1.00006pt\sin{\varphi}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\vartheta}\hskip 1.00006ptd{\varphi}\hskip 1.00006ptd\psi.

(A.14)

Appendix B
Instanton in amphometric space

An instanton is a solution to the classical equation of motion for the Yang–Mills gauge field in Euclidean space-time, given by the SD- or the ASD-curvature.
We extend it into the amphometric space and discuss topological aspects of the instanton solution in the Lorentzian metric space.
We utilise the index theorem for the Dirac operator, and the cobordism.
This appendix summarises the main conclusion of Ref.
[
10
]
.

B.1
Cobordism

René Tom has proposed a method to categorise compact and orientable
C^{\infty}
-manifolds into the equivalence classes named “
cobordant
[
30
,
31
]
” in 1953.
When a disjoint union of two
n
-dimensional manifolds is the boundary of a compact
(n\hskip-1.00006pt+\hskip-1.99997pt1)
-dimensional manifold, they are cobordant to each other; the cobordant is an equivalence relation and quotient space

\displaystyle\Omega_{C}^{n}:=\{n\textrm{-dimensional~closed~manifolds}\}/\hskip-1.99997pt\sim

forms an abelian group, where
\sim
is an equivalent relation concerning cobordism.
We present a definition and elementary remarks on cobordism in this section.
For details of the cobordism theory, see textbooks, e.g., Chapter 7 in Ref.
[
32
]
.

Definition B.1

(cobordant)
.

Suppose
M
and
N
are compact and oriented
n
-dimensional smooth manifolds, and
\omega_{\bullet}
denotes an orientation of oriented manifold
\bullet
.

(M,\omega_{M})
and
(N,\omega_{N})
are called “cobordant” to each other, if there exists a compact and oriented
(n\hskip-1.00006pt+\hskip-1.99997pt1)
-dimensional smooth manifold
(W,\omega_{W})
such that

\displaystyle(\partial{W},\hskip 1.99997pt\partial{\omega_{W}})

\displaystyle=(M,-\omega_{M})\amalg(N,\omega_{N}),

and
W
is called a cobordism from
M
to
N
.
When
M
and
N
are cobordant to each other owing to
W
, we denote them as
M\sim_{W}N
in this report.
Cobordant induces an equivalent class among compact and oriented
n
-dimensional smooth manifolds, namely, a cobordant class denoted by
\Omega_{C}^{n}
.

We utilise the following remarks and lemma to discuss the topological properties of the instanton solutions in the amphometric space.

Remark B.2
.

Cobordant class
\Omega_{C}^{n}
forms the abelian group concerning the disjoint-union operation.

Proof.

When map
M_{0}\rightarrow M_{1}
is diffeomorphic,
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
are cobordant; thus,
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
are cobordant with respect to closed period
\theta=[0,1]
;
M_{0}\sim_{M_{0}\otimes[0,1]}M_{1}
such that

\partial{(M_{\theta}\otimes[0,1])}=(M_{0}\otimes 0)\amalg(M_{1}\otimes 1)

with appropriate orientations.
It fulfils the associative and commutative laws trivially.
Any compact manifold
V=\partial{W}
is the zero element owing to
(M\amalg V)\sim_{W}M
.
The inverse of
(M,\omega_{M})
is given by
(M,-\omega_{M})
.
Therefore, the remark is maintained.
∎

Remark B.3

(Pontrjagin)
.

Characteristic numbers are cobordism invariant.

Proof.

A proof is provided for the Pontrjagin number; it is sufficient to show that the Pontrjagin numbers on
M
vanish when
M=\partial W
.
There exists one-dimensional trivial bundle
\xi
such that
TW\bigl|_{M}=TM\oplus\xi
; thus, for any inclusion map
\iota:M\hookrightarrow{W}
, relation
p_{i}(M)=\iota^{\#}(p_{i}(W))
is fulfilled.
Therefore, for any invariant polynomial
f\in{\mathfrak{W}}(M)
to define characteristic numbers, it is provided such that:

\displaystyle f\left(p_{1},p_{2},\cdots\right)[M]=\int_{M}f\left(p\left(M\right)\right)=\int_{\partial M}\iota^{\#}\left(f\left(p\left(W\right)\right)\right)=\int_{W}df\left(p\left(W\right)\right)=0,

owing to the Stokes theorem; thus, the lemma is maintained.
Here,
[M]
denotes a cobordism class of manifold
M
, and
\iota^{\#}(\bullet)
shows the pull of
\bullet
.
∎

Remark B.4
.

A transformation between the Euclidean space and the Minkowski space owing to the amphometric is a homeomorphism.

Proof.

Suppose
{\cal M}_{\textsc{e}}
and
{\cal M}_{\textsc{l}}
are
n
-dimensional (pseud-)Riemannian manifolds with, respectively, Euclidean and Lorentzian metrics, which are topological spaces with the
\mathbb{R}^{n}
Euclidean topology.
We introduce the
(n\hskip-1.00006pt+\hskip-1.00006pt1)
-dimensional manifold
{\cal W}_{\theta}:=\mathbb{R}^{n}\otimes(\theta\in[0,1])
which has the submersion of

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{\textsc{e}}\hookrightarrow{\cal W}_{\theta}:{\cal M}_{\textsc{e}}={\cal W}_{0}~~

\displaystyle\text{and}~~~{\cal M}_{\textsc{l}}\hookrightarrow{\cal W}_{\theta}:{\cal M}_{\textsc{l}}={\cal W}_{1}.

The rotation-invariant (isotropic) bilinear form
\langle\bullet|\bullet\rangle_{\theta}
of two vectors,
\bm{v}=(v^{1},\cdots,v^{n},\theta=\text{const.})
and
\bm{u}=(u^{1},\cdots,u^{n},\theta=\text{const.})
, in the foliation
{\cal W}_{\theta}
is defined as

\displaystyle\langle v|u\rangle^{~}_{\theta}:=\sum_{a,b=1,n}\left[\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}\right]_{ab}v^{a}u^{b}\in\mathbb{C},~~

\displaystyle\text{where}~~\eta_{\theta}^{~}:=\text{diag}\left[1,e^{i\pi\theta},e^{i\pi\theta},\cdots,e^{i\pi\theta}\right].

Tensor
\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}
provides the metric tensor at
\theta=0
and
\theta=1
.
A map

\displaystyle\pi_{\theta}:{\cal M}_{\textsc{e}}\rightarrow{\cal M}_{\textsc{l}}:\left(x^{1},\cdots,x^{n},0\right)\in{\cal W}_{0}\mapsto\left(x^{1},\cdots,x^{n},1\right)\in{\cal W}_{1}

is a bijection, continuous, and the inverse
\pi^{-1}_{\theta}
is continuous.
Moreover,
\mathrm{det}[\bm{\eta}^{~}_{\theta}]\neq 0
at any point in
\theta\in[0,1]
; thus, the tensor is invertible.
Therefore,
{\cal M}_{\textsc{e}}
and
{\cal M}_{\textsc{m}}
are homeomorphism as the
n
-dimensional manifold equipping the isotropic bilinear form.
∎

B.2
Instanton

The four-dimensional Euclidean space-time has the SD- or ASD curvature as a solution of the equation of motion obtained as the stationary point of the Yang–Mills action as shown in
Remark
4.2
.
The instanton solution
[
33
]
, is an example of the (A)SD solution, and the instanton number is a topological invariant in the four-dimensional Euclidean space-time.
This section treats the instanton solution in a flat space-time (
{\mathfrak{w}}=0
) with the amphometric and the instanton number in Euclidean and Lorentzian metric spaces.
We set coupling constant
{c^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}}
to unity for simplicity.

a) unphysical solution:

The instanton connection at
\bm{\rho}^{t}=(t,x,y,z)
in the amphometric space is provided as

\displaystyle{\mathscr{A}}_{\theta}(\bm{\rho})

\displaystyle=i\kappa(\theta)\frac{\bm{\rho}^{2}}{\bm{\rho}^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\bm{g}(\bm{\rho})\hskip 1.00006ptd\bm{g}^{-1}(\bm{\rho}))~~\text{with}~~0<\lambda\in\mathbb{R},

(B.2.1a)

where we set

\displaystyle\bm{g}(\bm{\rho})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bm{\rho}^{2}}}\hskip 1.99997pt\bm{\sigma}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}\cdot\bm{\rho}~~\textrm{with}~~\bm{\rho}^{2}:=\eta^{~}_{\theta\hskip 0.81949pt{\circ\circ}}\hskip 1.00006pt\rho^{\circ}\hskip 1.00006pt\rho^{\circ}.

(B.2.1b)

Direct calculations of (
B.2.1a
) with (
B.2.1b
) provide instanton connection as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{A}}_{\theta}^{I}

\displaystyle=\left[{\mathscr{A}}_{\theta}^{I}\right]_{\circ}{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}_{\theta}=\frac{1}{\bm{\rho}^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}x^{~}_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006ptdt-t^{~}_{~}dx^{~}_{\theta}+z^{~}_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006ptdy^{~}_{\theta}-y^{~}_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006ptdz^{~}_{\theta},&(I=1)\\
y^{~}_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006ptdt-z^{~}_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{~}_{\theta}-t^{~}_{~}\hskip 1.00006ptdy^{~}_{\theta}+x^{~}_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006ptdz^{~}_{\theta},&(I=2)\\
z^{~}_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006ptdt+y^{~}_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006ptdx^{~}_{\theta}-x^{~}_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006ptdy^{~}_{\theta}-t^{~}_{~}\hskip 1.00006ptdz^{~}_{\theta},&(I=3)\end{array}\!,\right.

where
(x^{~}_{\theta},y^{~}_{\theta},z^{~}_{\theta}):=i\kappa(\theta)(x,y,z)
and
{\mathfrak{e}}^{a}_{\theta}=(\ref{eeeQ})
.
The instanton connections in Euclidean and Lorentzian metric spaces are provided by setting
{\mathscr{A}}_{\textsc{e}}:={\mathscr{A}}_{\theta}\big|_{\theta\rightarrow 0}
and
{\mathscr{A}}_{\textsc{l}}:={\mathscr{A}}_{\theta}\big|_{\theta\rightarrow\pm 1}
, respectively.
The structure equation provides the instanton curvature as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}^{I}=\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathscr{F}_{\theta}^{I}\right]_{\circ\circ}{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}_{\theta}\wedge{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}_{\theta}=-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\bm{\rho}^{2}+\lambda^{2}}\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}dt{\wedge}dx_{\theta}+dy_{\theta}{\wedge}dz_{\theta}={\mathfrak{S}}_{\theta}^{+\hskip 0.81949pt1}&(I=1)\\
dt{\wedge}dy_{\theta}-dx_{\theta}{\wedge}dz_{\theta}={\mathfrak{S}}_{\theta}^{+\hskip 0.81949pt2}&(I=2)\\
dt{\wedge}dz_{\theta}+dx_{\theta}{\wedge}dy_{\theta}={\mathfrak{S}}_{\theta}^{+\hskip 0.81949pt3}&(I=3)\end{array}\!.\right.

Owing to the last expression of (
B.2
) with (
4.3.3c
), this is the SD curvature at any values of
\theta\in[0,1)
.
The amphometric instanton-curvature provides Euclidean and Lorentzian curvatures as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\theta}\big|_{\theta=0}=\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{\textsc{e}},~~\mathfrak{F}^{~}_{\theta}\big|_{\theta=1}=\mathfrak{F}^{+}_{\textsc{l}}.

Owing to the
Remark
4.2
, the SD curvature is a solution of the Yang–Mills equation in the Euclidean space.
In reality, direct calculations show the instanton curvature (
B.2
) is the solution of the Yang–Mills equation (
4.5.2
) in the amphometric space.
We consider to extent the
Remark
4.2
to the amphometric space here.

Compact manifold
\overline{{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}}
is constructed by the following compactification of
{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}
:
First, we introduce a four-dimensional polar coordinate in
{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}
as

\displaystyle\bm{\rho}^{t}_{\textsc{e}}

\displaystyle:=\left(r_{\textsc{e}}\hskip 1.00006pt\cos{\vartheta_{1}},r_{\textsc{e}}\hskip 1.00006pt\sin{\vartheta_{1}}\cos{\vartheta_{2}},r_{\textsc{e}}\hskip 1.00006pt\sin{\vartheta_{1}}\sin{\vartheta_{2}}\cos{\vartheta_{3}},r_{\textsc{e}}\hskip 1.00006pt\sin{\vartheta_{1}}\sin{\vartheta_{2}}\sin{\vartheta_{3}}\right),

yielding

\displaystyle\int\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\textsc{e}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}dr_{\textsc{e}}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\vartheta_{1}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\vartheta_{2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\hskip-1.00006ptd\vartheta_{3}\hskip 1.99997ptr^{3}_{\textsc{e}}\hskip 1.00006pt\sin^{2}{\hskip-1.99997pt\vartheta_{1}}\hskip 1.00006pt\sin{\hskip-1.00006pt\vartheta_{2}}.

We note that
\langle\bm{\rho}^{~}_{\textsc{e}},\bm{\rho}^{~}_{\textsc{e}}\rangle=r^{2}_{\textsc{e}}>0
.
We apply variable transformations

\displaystyle r^{~}_{\textsc{e}}\mapsto r^{\prime}_{\textsc{e}}=\begin{cases}r^{~}_{\textsc{e}}/\lambda,&0\leq r^{~}_{\textsc{e}}\leq\lambda\\
\lambda/r^{~}_{\textsc{e}},&\lambda<r^{~}_{\textsc{e}}<\infty\end{cases}.

(B.2.5)

When the one-point compactification introduces the infinite point equating with the origin, we obtain two compact manifolds
D^{4}
as
D^{4}_{\textrm{in}}~(r^{~}_{\textsc{e}}\leq\lambda)
and
D^{4}_{\textrm{out}}~(r^{~}_{\textsc{e}}\geq\lambda)
.
Then, surfaces of
D^{4}_{\textrm{in}}
and
D^{4}_{\textrm{out}}
are equated to each other with keeping three angular variables; thus,
{\cal M}_{E}
is compacitified into union of two compact manifolds such that

\displaystyle\overline{{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}}

\displaystyle=D_{\textrm{in}}^{4}\cup D_{\textrm{out}}^{4}~~\text{with}~~D^{4}_{\textrm{in}}\cap D^{4}_{\textrm{out}}=\partial{D^{4}_{\textrm{in}}}=-\partial{D^{4}_{\textrm{out}}}=S^{3}.

Consequently,
\overline{{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}}
is boundary-less:
\partial(\overline{{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}})=\emptyset
.

The second Chern class defined as (
4.5.6
) in the amphometric space is calculated from (
B.2
) as

\displaystyle c^{~}_{2}\left(\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int{\mathrm{Tr}\hskip-0.50003pt}_{\hskip-0.81949ptS\hskip-0.81949ptU\hskip-0.81949pt}\left[\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}\wedge\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}\right]=e^{3i\pi\theta/2}\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\int\left(\frac{\lambda}{\bm{\rho}^{2}_{\theta}+\lambda^{2}}\right)^{4}\mathfrak{v}^{~}_{\theta}.

After the variable transformations (
B.2.5
) we obtain the Chern class in Euclidean disks
D_{\textrm{in}}^{4}
and
D_{\textrm{out}}^{4}
as

\displaystyle\int_{D^{4}_{\textrm{in}}}c^{~}_{2}(\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{e}})

\displaystyle=\int_{D^{4}_{\textrm{out}}}c^{~}_{2}(\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{e}})=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{r^{\prime 3}_{\textsc{e}}}{(r^{\prime 2}_{\textsc{e}}+1)^{4}}dr^{\prime}_{\textsc{e}}\int_{S^{3}}d\Omega_{4}=\frac{1}{2},

where
d\Omega_{4}
is an integration measure for an angular integration on
S^{3}
, which gives
\int_{S^{3}}d\Omega_{4}=2\pi^{2}
.
The Chern index is obtained as an integration of them as

\displaystyle ch(\overline{{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}})=\int_{D^{4}_{\textrm{in}}}c^{~}_{2}(\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{e}})+\int_{D^{4}_{\textrm{out}}}c^{~}_{2}(\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{e}})=1.

Consequently, we obtain the first Pontrjagin index (equal to the second Chern index) of the instanton curvature in the compact Euclidean manifold as unity.
We referred to this integer as the instanton number.

On intersection
D^{4}_{\textrm{in}}\cap D^{4}_{\textrm{out}}=S^{3}
, angular variables coincide as

\displaystyle\partial D_{\textrm{in}}\ni\vartheta_{i}^{\textrm{in}}=\vartheta_{i}^{\textrm{out}}\in-\partial D_{\textrm{out}},

for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
with keeping an orientation on their surfaces.
When
\vartheta_{3}^{\textrm{in}}
maps to
\vartheta_{3}^{\textrm{out}}
as
n\times\hskip 1.99997pt\vartheta_{3}^{\textrm{in}}=\vartheta_{3}^{\textrm{out}}
with
0<n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the instanton number is given as
c^{~}_{2}(\overline{{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}})=n
due to the
\vartheta_{3}
integration from
0
to
2n\pi
, which corresponds to the winding number around infinity.

The first Pontrjagin index is defined in the entire space of
{\cal W}_{\theta}
.
After the same compactification as in a Euclidean space in
{\cal W}_{\theta}
, a four-dimensional polar coordinate is introduced in the amphometric space as
\bm{\rho}^{~}_{\theta}:=\bm{\rho}^{~}_{\textsc{e}}\big|^{~}_{r_{\textsc{e}}{\rightarrow}r_{\theta}}
.
The replacement (
B.2.5
) provides the Chern index as

\displaystyle ch(\overline{{\cal M}_{\theta}})

\displaystyle=e^{i\pi\theta/2}\frac{6\cdot 2^{4}}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}{dr^{~}_{\theta}}\int_{0}^{\pi}{d\vartheta_{1}}\int_{0}^{\pi}{d\vartheta_{2}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}{d\vartheta_{3}}\hskip 1.99997ptr^{3}_{\theta}\hskip 1.00006pt\sin^{2}{\hskip-1.99997pt\vartheta_{1}}\sin{\vartheta_{2}}

\displaystyle\times\left[\left(2+r^{2}_{\theta}\left(1+\cos{2\vartheta_{1}}+2e^{i\pi\theta}\sin^{2}{\hskip-1.99997pt\vartheta_{1}}\right)\right)^{-4}+\left(1+2r^{2}_{\theta}+\cos{2\vartheta_{1}}+2e^{i\pi\theta}\sin^{2}{\hskip-1.99997pt\vartheta_{1}}\right)^{-4}\right],

\displaystyle=e^{i\pi\theta/2}\frac{2^{3}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}{d\vartheta_{1}}\frac{\sin^{2}{\hskip-1.99997pt\vartheta_{1}}}{\left(1+\cos{2\vartheta_{1}}+2e^{i\pi\theta}\sin^{2}{\hskip-1.99997pt\vartheta_{1}}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}+1&4n-1<\theta<4n+1\\
-1&4n+1<\theta<4n+3\\
\text{indefinite}&\theta=2n+1\end{cases},~~~~~~~~~~~n\in\mathbb{Z}.

At
\theta=2n+1
with
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we obtain
e^{i\pi\theta}=-1
; thus, the integrand has a pole on the
\vartheta_{1}
integration contour yielding indefinite.
With
\theta
at other than an odd integer, the
\vartheta_{1}
integration provides
\pm 1
depending on the sign of the imaginary part of the integrand.
Consequently, the Chern index with the Lorentzian metric is not well-defined.
A domain of the instanton solution in the Lorentzian metric space is not the entire space of
{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}
but is
{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}\hskip-3.00003pt\setminus\hskip-3.00003pt{\cal M}_{\lambda}
, where
{\cal M}_{\lambda}:=\{\rho^{~}_{\textsc{l}}\in{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}\mid{{{\eta^{~}_{\textsc{l}}}}_{\circ\circ}\rho^{{\circ}}_{\textsc{l}}\rho^{{\circ}}_{\textsc{l}}+\lambda^{2}=0}\}
; thus, manifold
{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}
is not closed as a principal bundle with the instanton connection.
Taking closure of manifold
{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}
concerning
\theta
from the left,
{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}
has the Chern index as

\displaystyle ch({{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}):=ch({\cal M}^{~}_{\theta=1-\epsilon})\big|^{~}_{\epsilon\rightarrow+0}=1.

The instanton solution in the Minkowski space is an example of
Remark
B.3
: manifolds
{\cal M}_{0}={{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}
and
{\cal M}_{1}={{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}
are cobordant to each other, and the instanton solutions in the Euclidean and Lorentzian metric are homotopical equivalent in
{\cal W}^{~}_{\theta}
; thus, the Chern index of
{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}
is consistently yielding the same number as that of
{{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}}
.

The instanton curvature (
B.2
) with the Lorentzian metric is

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\textsc{l}}^{I}=-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\bm{\rho}^{2}+\lambda^{2}}{\mathfrak{S}}_{\textsc{l}}^{+\hskip 0.81949pta=I}.

(B.2.6)

In four-dimensional space, the SD two-forms have three bases as the same number as the
SU(2)
generators; thus, we can express the instanton solution as in (
B.2.6
).
After the quantisation, it squared may provide a probability to find the instanton in the Lorentzian space-time.
For an observer at the origin of the coordinate, the probability peaks around
t^{2}-|\bm{r}|^{2}+\lambda^{2}=0
, which is in the space-like region of the observer.
In other words, the instanton has a tachyon trajectory and is unphysical.

b) physical solution:

We consider the Euclidean instanton solution which provides a physical particle in the Lorentzian metric space.
We modify connection (
B.2.1a
) as

\displaystyle{\mathscr{A}}_{\theta}(\bm{\rho})

\displaystyle=(\ref{instantonsol})\big|_{\lambda\rightarrow\lambda_{\theta}},\quad\text{where}\quad\lambda_{\theta}:=i\kappa(\theta)\lambda,

(B.2.7)

yielding

\displaystyle(\ref{physInstA})

\displaystyle\implies\mathfrak{F}_{\theta}^{I}=\begin{cases}-{\lambda^{2}}/{(\bm{\rho}^{2}+\lambda^{2})}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{S}}_{\textsc{e}}^{+\hskip 0.81949pta=I},&\theta=0\\
+{\lambda^{2}}/{(\bm{\rho}^{2}-\lambda^{2})}\hskip 1.99997pt{\mathfrak{S}}_{\textsc{l}}^{+\hskip 0.81949pta=I},&\theta=\pm 1\end{cases}.

(B.2.8)

Physical instanton (
B.2.8
) has a pole on the Cauchy surface only at
\theta=\pm 1
in
{\cal W}^{~}_{\theta}
.
This curvature fulfils the Yang–Mills equation in vacuum with
\theta\in(-1,1)
.
Direct calculations with the same compactification as above show the Chern index of this curvature as

\displaystyle 1=ch({{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\theta}})=ch({{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{e}}}})=ch({\cal M}^{~}_{\theta=1-\epsilon})\big|^{~}_{\epsilon\rightarrow+0}=:ch({{{\cal M}^{~}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\textsc{l}}}}).

(B.2.9)

After the quantisation, this solution squared may again provide a probability of finding the instanton in the Lorentzian space-time.
For an observer at the origin of the coordinate at
t=0
, the probability peaks around
t^{2}-|\bm{r}|^{2}=\lambda^{2}
, which is in the time-like region of the observer.
In this case, the instanton is physical.
In conclusion, we obtained the exact solution of the
SU(2)
Yang–Mills equation in Lorentzian vacuum as follows:

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{A}}_{\textsc{l}}^{I}

\displaystyle=\left[{\mathscr{A}}_{\textsc{l}}^{I}\right]_{\circ}{\mathfrak{e}}^{\circ}_{\textsc{l}}=\frac{1}{\bm{\rho}^{2}-\lambda^{2}}\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}x\hskip 1.00006ptd\tilde{t}-\tilde{t}\hskip 1.00006ptdx-z\hskip 1.00006ptdy+y\hskip 1.00006ptdz,&(I=1)\\
y\hskip 1.00006ptd\tilde{t}+z\hskip 1.00006ptdx-\tilde{t}\hskip 1.00006ptdy-x\hskip 1.00006ptdz,&(I=2)\\
z\hskip 1.00006ptd\tilde{t}-y\hskip 1.00006ptdx+x\hskip 1.00006ptdy-\tilde{t}\hskip 1.00006ptdz,&(I=3)\end{array}\right.\hskip-5.0pt,~~\text{with}~~\bm{\rho}^{2}=t^{2}-x^{2}-y^{2}-z^{2},

where
\tilde{t}:=it
.
